So, if we just do what we are supposed to do, or we *really* do what we are already doing, we’ll discover that we have already entered our eternal life.

One caveat: Śrīla Prabhupāda cautioned us that as long as we are in the material world, we should never feel ourselves safe. Especially if circumstantially we have to associate with materialistic people, we can easily get influenced, or infected, and forget. The enjoying spirit animates social activity: “Let’s go do this! Let’s do that!” People never cease building up stupid projects in the material world. We are all social beings, and whatever our associates are enthusiastic about, we, too, may find ourselves enthusiastic about—especially if some seed of the enjoying spirit remains in our heart. We have to be careful to avoid reinfection by the enjoying spirit, which reinforces the false ego. It is a deadly germ. It is a plague that’s going around in the material world—the plague of death.

## Death and Dying in the Vedic Tradition

A talk by Giriraj Swami to the doctors and nurses of San Diego Hospice and The Institute for Palliative Medicine on May 19, 2005.

It is a great pleasure for me to address you all here, especially because this hospice is recognized as one of the best and largest in the world and as the global leader in hospice education.

In the *Bhagavad-gītā*, which is considered the essence of the Vedas, Lord Krishna informs us:

*yaṁ yaṁ vāpi smaran bhāvaṁ tyajaty ante kalevaram*  
*taṁ tam evaiti kaunteya svadā tad-bhāva-bhāvitaḥ*

**“Whatever state of being one remembers when he quits his body, O son of Kuntī, that state he will attain without fail.” (*****Bg*** [**8.6**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/8.6)**)**

*anta-kāle ca mām eva smaran muktvā kalevaram*  
*yaḥ prayāti sa mad-bhāvaṁ yāti nāsty atra saṁśayaḥ*

**“And whoever, at the end of his life, quits his body remembering Me alone at once attains My nature. Of this there is no doubt.” (*****Bg*** [**8.5**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/8.5)**)**

In 1969, when I was a student of psychology at Brandeis University, I met my spiritual teacher, Śrīla A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda. In 1970, after studying with him for a year and a half, I accompanied him to India. And in 1977, as our instructor, he taught us by his example how to leave the world in proper, pure consciousness. He retired to Vrindavan, a holy place in India, and surrounded himself with devotees who were always chanting or reading to him from sacred literature. Being in such a holy place was itself conducive to spiritual consciousness, to God consciousness. And the atmosphere was enhanced by loving disciples singing songs of the Lord and reading books about Him.

Some years later, one of my students, who was older than me, was diagnosed with cancer. She was a renowned artist from South Africa who would travel all over the world to find subjects for painting. At one point, she decided that she wanted to combine her spiritual interests with her artwork, and so she bought a plot of land in the same holy place, Vrindavan, to establish a studio, and she was kind enough to build a floor upstairs for me, for when I would visit. Although the doctors had given her six months, she actually lived for three years. For the last two or three months of her life, I was with her almost constantly, because the goal—not just in the Vedic tradition but in others as well—is to think of God, to chant the name of God, or at least hear the name chanted, at the time of death, and I wanted to help her do that.

During the thirty years that I was based in India, I would travel frequently to Pakistan and Sri Lanka, and in Pakistan I came across a book called *The Ninety-nine Names of Allah*. There is a similar work in Sanskrit called *The Thousand Names* *of Vishnu* (another name for Krishna)*,* the *Viṣṇu-sahasra-nāma.* So, in *The Ninety-nine Names of Allah* there is a section about how a pious Muslim should meet death. The best thing a dying Muslim’s friends and relatives can do, the book states, is chant the names of God and help the loved one to either chant or at least hear others chant the name of God at the final moment. I saw that the same practice that we have in the Vedic tradition, in Krishna consciousness, is also there in Islam, and it is there in other traditions as well.

With my student, we followed the same practice that we had seen with our spiritual master, and devotees would come and chant. The chanting can be done individually in a quiet, meditative way or communally and more loudly, with musical instruments. The whole idea is to help the person fix his or her consciousness on the Supreme and keep it fixed on the Supreme at the time of death. And along with the chanting is the reading and talking about God consciousness.

In Vedic culture, the time of death is considered life’s final test. In school we attend lectures, complete assignments, take quizzes, and write the midterm, but whether or not we graduate depends on whether we pass the final examination. In life, passing the final examination means thinking of God. That is why the whole focus at the time of death is on helping the person remember God. And the other activities that we perform during our lives, besides freeing us from acts that will result in our taking birth again, are practice for thinking of God. And we get little tests along the way—sicknesses, setbacks, and various hardships. When we face them, do we remember God, or do we look only for material solutions? These are the tests along the way. And the time of death is the final examination, and if we can remember God then, we graduate. We are free: no more material bodies, no more repetition of birth and death.

LADY (1): In the work we do we are often looking at physical pain. Should medication to alleviate physical pain and suffering be discouraged? Would it interfere with consciousness at the time of death?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Very good question. The goal is to remember God at the time of death. So our general approach is that we want to take enough medication so that the pain is not so excruciating that our consciousness is just absorbed in the pain but at the same time not take so much medication that it dulls us to the point that we cannot really think of God. That is the delicate balance we try to achieve.

GENTLEMAN (1): Is remembering God just saying the name or thinking the name of God, or is it something else— coming back into a state of divine consciousness where one actually feels a connection with God?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Now, what do we mean when we talk about remembering God? To begin, God is a person—that is the first point. He is not a person like you and me, with a body made of flesh and bones. He is spiritual, transcendent. But He is a person. The Bible says, “Man is made in the image of God.” We are persons, and so our supreme father or mother must also be a person. This is a difficult point, because we are so conditioned by the material concept of personality that when we hear about God’s personality we think in terms of our material experience. Sometimes people think, “If you say that God is a person, you are limiting Him.” For example, I am sitting here. Because I am sitting here, I cannot be in the temple at Pacific Beach; I cannot be at my ashram in Santa Barbara. But God—He is a person, but He is in this room, He is in the temple in Pacific Beach, He is in Santa Barbara, He is in our hearts, He is in every molecule and every atom. But He is still a person.

Let us take the example of a person who holds a high office, say the president of a country. The term *president* describes his office. But there is also the person who occupies the office. And that person has a name, an appearance, and personal qualities; he or she engages in certain activities. When we remember the president . . . yes, there are things about the office that we consider, but to really remember the president means to know his name, his form, his features, his qualities, his activities. So, God, too, has a name—He has many names. And He has a form— many forms. And He has many qualities and engages in many activities.

The real goal is to love God, because when you love someone, you naturally think of the person. When we develop love for God, we will naturally remember Him and think of how beautiful His form is, how sublime His qualities are, how wonderful His activities are. We will like repeating His name, just as one would repeat the name of a loved one, and we will think how best we can serve Him and please Him.

But how can we know God well enough that we can actually come to love Him? We can learn about Him in detail—in the greatest detail—from Vedic literatures. They describe not just that He is the supreme authority, the creator, the maintainer, the destroyer, and the protector, omniscient, omnipresent, and omnipotent—which He is—but also His personal attributes: His names, His forms, His qualities, and His activities. And when one comes to know Him in a more personal way and develops affection and attachment—love—for Him in a personal relationship, it is very easy to think of Him all the time, and very natural to think of Him at the time of death.

GENTLEMAN (2): As hospice workers we work with the families of patients and deal a lot with the grief, the loss of the soul in this body that they have enjoyed in the physical context. It seems that after a loved one’s death, a family member could hear the name of God or think of the name of God, but because we’re outside of that person, we don’t know what he or she is actually feeling. So in what ways can we bring comfort and assurance to the family members? How can we support them in their grief and the loss of their loved one?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: The first instruction of the *Bhagavad-gītā,* which is also a central principle in many other traditions, is that the person is not the body. The person is the soul living

in the body. When the person leaves the body, we say that the person is dead, but actually the body was never really alive; it was just animated by the presence of the soul. At the moment the soul leaves the body, all the elements of the body are still there, lying on the bed. You may lament: “Oh, my husband is gone.” “My wife is gone.” But why do you say they are gone? The body is lying on the bed. You might have thought that the body was your husband or your wife or your father or your mother, but when they pass away, you say, “Oh, my father is gone.” “My mother is gone.” But the body is still there. So why do you say they are gone? Intuitively, we know that the person was not the body. That loved one is something other than the body that he or she has just left; that person is the soul.

When a person has led a good life, and even more so when he or she has tried to develop a relationship with God and hear the name of God and think of God at the time of death, we can be assured that the person will go to a better destination. There are many emotions. There is the sense of personal loss, that the person whom we loved and shared so many good times with is gone. Out of love, there is also concern. Where is the person now? But if we know that the person has gone to a better place, we feel solace. And in painful illnesses such as cancer, we may also think, “All right. The soul has left this body, and I am sad, but this body had become very difficult; it had become a painful place for the soul to inhabit, so it is actually better that the person has gone elsewhere.” The person, the soul, continues to exist. He or she has gone to live somewhere else, where there will be less pain and suffering.

Still, we do not deny the sense of personal loss. Even among transcendentalists there are various emotions. When a perfect yogi leaves the body, we know that the person is qualified to enter the kingdom of God, so we are happy. We are both—happy and sad. We are happy because we know that the person has gone to God, to serve God, but we are sad because we will miss the person’s association. Still, such departed souls, we believe, can inspire and guide their loved ones who remain behind.

Then there is also the idea that God is responsive to prayer. When a loved one leaves the body, we know that the soul exists somewhere. We don’t know where, but we want to help the soul, because we love that person. So we can pray to God, “Wherever my mother is now”—or my father or whoever—“please be merciful. Please help her come closer to You.” And I believe that those prayers will not only help the departed soul but will also give us a chance to continue the relationship and to try to help our loved one even after he or she has left the body.

Then there are the bereaved’s own spiritual practices— chanting or meditation or prayer or whatever. Yes, we are attached to and miss our loved one, but through genuine spiritual practice we come in contact with the Supreme. Of course, in the initial stage the bereaved may be too distraught. But then when we do engage in some practice that brings us in touch with God, we feel, “God is there. It is all right. God is taking care. My real relationship is with God. By God’s grace I can gain His shelter in the future.”

DR. BHARADWAJ: Many patients ask, “Why is this happening to me? Is God punishing me?” and this causes a lot of spiritual suffering. How would you approach this?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: If someone is suffering from a terminal disease, I would not get into the idea that God is angry with the person or is punishing him. Rather, I would say, “Anyone who takes birth in a material body has to die.” That is the point. “Birth, disease, old age, and death are inevitable for every conditioned soul. It may be this disease or that, it may be this symptom of old age or that, or it may be this or that way to death. But these factors are there for everyone. And now that you are in this position, you should use whatever time you have left to develop your relationship with God, so that you do not have to take birth in another material body and suffer through the same cycle again.” And if patients feel some specific spiritual regret or guilt, we can hear them with empathy and help them work it out—perhaps take some practical measures to resolve it—and go beyond it.

As for the suffering, on the material platform its value is that it burns up bad karma. That is why many Hindus prefer to tolerate rather than protest; they know that by tolerating their suffering they are exhausting their sinful reactions, and they would rather get it over with than try to postpone it and then have to suffer the bad karma later, in some other form. On the spiritual platform, the benefit is that suffering can serve to make us more detached from the body and from the world. We are trying to become transcendentalists, yet we still have material attachments. But when there is some upheaval or calamity we can realize, “Actually, the material world is not a happy place. I should not be spending my energy trying to make it happy, because by nature it is not. I should be using my energy to realize the Supreme and get out of here.” Lord Krishna confirms in the *Bhagavad-gītā* ([8.15](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/8.15))*, duḥkhālayam aśāśvatam*: “This material world is a place of misery, and it is temporary.”

DR. BHARADWAJ: A lot of patients fear that bad karma is causing their suffering.

Giriraj Swami: Well, it may be. Still, our teacher gave the example of someone drowning and another person coming in a boat to rescue him. The drowning person says, “Now, wait a minute. How did I get here?” But that is not the point. The rescuer would say, “Don’t worry about the past; we can talk about that later. You are drowning. Just get in the boat!” So, we are drowning in this body, in this *saṁsāra,* this ocean of repeated birth, death, old age, and disease. Let’s not worry how we got here. Let’s try to get out. And it is never too late. That is the power of God’s name and God’s mercy. There are stories in the Vedic literature of people who were very sinful but at the last moment chanted God’s name and were delivered. So it is never too late.

LADY (3): A lot of our patients never make it to old age, and it’s very difficult for everybody—the patient’s family and us— when they seem to not complete, in our perception, their life. What’s the approach to that?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Their destiny is caused by their activity, their karma. For whatever reason, they did something that is causing them to leave the body before the normal time. But the positive side—we always have to see the positive, the spiritual side—is that, depending on the circumstance, a younger person may be better equipped to think of God at the time of death than an older one who has lost more of his or her faculties. In fact, there is a Sanskrit prayer:

*kṛṣṇa tvadīya-pada-paṅkaja-pañjarāntam*  
*adyaiva me viśatu mānasa-rāja-haṁsaḥ*  
*prāṇa-prayāṇa-samaye kapha-vāta-pittaiḥ*  
*kaṇṭhāvarodhana-vidhau smaraṇaṁ kutas te*  
*\[MM* [*33*](https://vedarama.com/en/books/mm/33)*\]*

**The devotee prays to the Lord, “My dear Krishna, please let me die immediately so that the swan of my mind may be encircled by the stem of Your lotus feet. Otherwise, at the time of my final breath, when my throat is choked up, how will it be possible for me to remember You and chant Your holy name?”**

This may seem contrary to our materialistic culture, which is so preoccupied with pampering and preserving the body, with staying in the body as long as possible and squeezing out of it the last possible drop of pleasure. This prayer, in opposition to that mad, vain pursuit, offers a more philosophical perspective. It is a different perspective on dying, even before old age—one that is positive and spiritual.

## Thinking of Krishna at the Time of Death

A talk by His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda in Los Angeles, December 25, 1968.

*kṛṣṇa tvadīya-pada-paṅkaja-pañjarāntam*  
*adyaiva me viśatu mānasa-rāja-haṁsaḥ*  
*prāṇa-prayāṇa-samaye kapha-vāta-pittaiḥ*  
*kaṇṭhāvarodhana-vidhau smaraṇaṁ kutas te*

**“My Lord Kṛṣṇa, I pray that the swan of my mind may immediately sink down to the stems of the lotus feet of Your Lordship and be locked in their network; otherwise at the time of my final breath, when my throat is choked up with cough, how will it be possible to think of You?” (*****Mukunda-mālā-stotra*** [**33**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/mm/33)**)**

This verse was sung by King Kulaśekhara, a great king but at the same time a great devotee of the Lord. His songs are recorded in the book known as *Mukunda-mālā-stotra*. It is a very famous book, sung by many devotees. So, it does not matter whether a man is a king or a poor mendicant; everyone has the facility to become the greatest devotee of the Lord.

He is praying, “My dear Krishna, Your feet are lotus.” Generally we say “lotus feet.” Where the lotus flower is, the white swans come and try to play with the stem. They sport, going under the water, and become entangled with the stem of that lotus flower. That is their sporting. So King Kulaśekhara is praying, “Let the swan of my mind immediately enter into the network of the stem of Your lotus feet.” That means he wants to engage his mind on the lotus feet of the Lord immediately. There is no question of delaying. He says, “Now I am in sound mind. If I think that I will think of Your lotus feet at the time of death, there is no certainty.” Because at the time of death, the whole body becomes dislocated, the whole function becomes dismantled.

The body is supposed to be conducted by three elements, *kapha*, *pitta*, and *vāyu*—cold \[mucus\], bile, and air. When these three elements work simultaneously, there is no disease in the body, but as soon as there is overlapping of these three elements, there is disruption and the body becomes diseased. And when it is not possible to bring them again into their regular function, a man dies. That is the verdict of *Āyurveda-śāstra*. So death takes place when these three elements overlap with one another. And the symptom is that there is a sound in the throat, *garhh*, *garhh*. That means the patient cannot speak. The throat is choked up, and he becomes suffocated and dies. This is the last-stage symptom of this body.

So King Kulaśekhara says, “I cannot wait up to that time when everything will be topsy-turvy. Now my mind is sound. Let me enter immediately into the stem of Your lotus feet.” That means he’s praying, “Let me die in the sound condition of my life so that I can think of Your lotus feet.” In other words, he’s giving us the lesson that if we do not practice to engage our mind on the lotus feet of Krishna when our mind is sound, how will it be possible to think of Him at the time of death?

## Chapter II – Caring for the Terminally Ill

## Mother Arcā-vigraha—An Inspiration

Giriraj Swami

My main inspiration for developing a hospice facility came from Mother Arcā-vigraha, in her last days. Arcā-vigraha had been a prominent artist in South Africa, and she built a house in Vrindavan, in Raman Reti, near the Krishna-Balaram Mandir, to serve as a residence and art studio whenever she visited India. But not long thereafter, she was diagnosed with cancer, and so she sold her lovely home in Johannesburg and settled permanently in Vraja.

She was the first Western devotee to leave her body in Vrindavan and receive so much attention. Her spiritual qualities attracted many devotees to spend time with her, and her departure became paradigmatic of how to pass away in Krishna consciousness with the support of the community. And she wanted other devotees to have the chance to leave their bodies in the same way, in the spiritual atmosphere of Vrindavan. At the same time, she became acutely aware of the deficiencies in the medical care there. So she wanted us to have what she called a “back-to-Godhead clinic.”

Now, of course, there is more facility for acute care, but then there was very little, and no system for palliative care. When during the course of her terminal illness Arcā-vigraha got jaundice, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s French disciple Gopasvāmī dāsa, who was a doctor, treated her, but the local care was very poor. The local people who came could not do an IV properly, and there was no local lab.

But Arcā loved just being in Vrindavan. The plot where she built her house turned out to be next to Śivarāma Swami and Govinda Swami’s, on a little lane called Radha Raman Bagicha, off the Parikrama Marg on the way to the Krishna-Balaram tree. It’s the same lane where Balaram Baba has his ashram. She didn’t have air conditioning, but she had coolers that cooled the air by blowing it through screens of wet *khus* grass. And everything in her house was always spotlessly clean and well maintained. From her bed, through her windows, she could see trees, creepers, peacocks, parrots, cows, and calves. Even when she was bedridden, we would open the windows and she would look out and see the trees and vines and flowers, feel the breezes, and smell the aromas.

Because of her spiritual advancement and sublime qualities, Arcā-vigraha attracted a lot of people. Devotees were always coming to visit her, and those with special abilities would offer specialized services. One devotee knew massage, and she would come once or twice a week and massage Arcā. Various types of alternative healers from different countries also came and offered their services, which Arcā graciously and gratefully— and affectionately—accepted. It all sort of just happened.

At the same time, Arcā had her personal staff, devotees who were there specifically to serve her, headed by Kuntīdevī. They took care of the house and grounds, the medical equipment, the laundry, the purchases, and the regular services of cooking, cleaning, bathing her, dressing her, feeding her, and administering her medication. As the cancer progressed, she got exactly what food she needed—simple foods, then soft food, then purees, then juice, and finally just crushed ice.

On the spiritual side there was constant chanting and hearing. Devotees came to do *kīrtana* or chant *japa* with her, and many, such as Bhakti Bhṛṅga Govinda Swami, Tamal Krishna Goswami, Śivarāma Swami, and Keśava Bhāratī Maharaja, would come and read to her. Bhūrijana Prabhu came every day. And when there was no chanting or reading, a tape would be playing. Arcā also had her altar, with various pictures and deities. And whatever food she ate was always first offered to God. Indradyumna Swami allowed her to keep his Radha-Krishna Deities, Sri Sri Gāndharvikā-Giridhārī, and every day Govinda Maharaja would bring over his Gaura-Nitāi Deities and sometimes his *govardhana-śilā*.

Arcā-vigraha reciprocated with her guests perfectly. Mainly she was just eager to hear and speak about Krishna. But depending on who came, she would give them a sympathetic ear, wise counsel, Krishna conscious philosophy, or even practical knowledge. With as much energy as she had, for as long as she could, she extended herself to others. Even in her last months, she gave art lessons to the *gurukula* students (who would also come and perform *kīrtana* for her), she painted portraits of Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī and Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī for their *bhajana-kuṭīra* at Rādhā-kuṇḍa, and she did some artwork for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *samādhi*. Whoever you were, whatever your mood, Arcā was always ready to reciprocate.

And there was always a high level of cooperation. As the director of the Santa Barbara hospice Serenity House recently told us, that is what attracts people. Not only the patients are touched by the mood of love and care; the staff and volunteers are too. That very positive mood of care and concern for the patients, for their loved ones, for their guests, and for the other caregivers is very, very appealing.

Often, good relations have much to do with good management, with having the right people in the right places interacting properly. In any machine, if one of the pieces is out of place, it can cause friction. So we have to take care that we have competent management and leadership.

It is really since Arcā-vigraha’s departure that I have come to learn about hospice care. Before then I did not know what “hospice” meant, and Arcā herself never used the word. Her idea, her desire, seemed to have sprung directly from her experience and realization, from her beautiful heart—she wanted devotees to have the same facility she had.

At one stage we even considered building small cottages so that devotees could feel that they too had their own place in Vrindavan. That was beyond our means, but we do want to do whatever we can to create a congenial, beautiful, spiritual atmosphere. On the site, we have kept all the trees—so many trees— and that is good. Hospices try to offer patients their own private balconies or patios, their own little areas where they can have direct access to the outdoors, to sunlight, fresh air, and nature’s beauty. So we must try our best to create that sense of home in Vraja.

Vision is a subtle thing, comprising many diverse elements. Our vision, Arcā’s vision, is for the hospice to afford devotees the same kind of experience that she had. And this project, drawing all those elements together to make that vision a reality, has been inspired by her, that saintly lady, that great soul, Arcā-vigraha dāsī.

## The Value of Care, the Wish of a Vaishnavi

A talk by Bhakti Bhṛṅga Govinda Swami in Carpinteria, California, May 29, 2007.

The work of the hospice is really such an important thing, and such a valuable gift to ISKCON. Just before coming here, I stopped in Nashville, Tennessee, which is my hometown. About two weeks ago I got an e-mail that said that my eldest brother had been diagnosed with brain cancer and liver cancer and that they had just moved him directly from the hospital to a hospice. There was no consideration of sending him back home.

When I got to the hospice, I saw how wonderful it was that people who had the ability had created such a facility for terminal patients and that the patients could live in a comfortable environment with caregivers who were professional but also very warm and caring. I met Carmiel and Ginger, two absolutely beautiful people—as humans, just beautiful people—highly qualified nurses but dedicated to helping people make the transition from this world to the next. I was intrigued, so I asked Ginger, “How long have you been working in hospice?” She said, “I have been a registered nurse for twenty-seven years, but I have been in hospice for only three.” Then she added, “For me, working in hospice is much more rewarding. Working in a hospital, we are bound by so many oaths that even if it’s painful or inhumane, we have to keep people alive. Here we just try to create the best quality of life for people so that they can move on to the next life.”

I had the good fortune of assisting, serving, two devotees in Vrindavan. One was Buddhimanta, who was the first big book distributor in ISKCON. He passed away from brain cancer. And the second was Mother Arcā-vigraha, who was a disciple of Giriraj Maharaja. It was such a rewarding experience. But it was tough, especially being a layperson with no medical experience or training in the hospice field. We were fortunate that sometimes we had Gopasvāmī dāsa from France, a medical doctor with a certificate in hospice care. But yes, it was very rewarding being with Arcā-vigraha.

Her previous name was Angel Lipkin, and her name, Angel, indicated her nature. She was actually an angel. She had breast cancer, and it got into the lymph nodes, and the lymph nodes were strangling her esophagus and nerves, and it was quite painful. Arcā was a bit of a hardhead. She was one of those natural-type people who would never want to take pain medication; she would take only some homeopathic tablets that were less strong than even aspirin. Sometimes I would see her in the depths of pain, but people would come to her door and say, “Arcā, I have a problem.” They would come in, and she would speak to them with such nice Krishna consciousness.

We were neighbors, so I would also pop in—morning, noon, and night. One night I came into her place and she was alone, sitting on her bed crying. I went over and said, “I have to give you some medicine.” She said, “No, I don’t take medicine.” I said, “No, I have to give you some medicine.” I opened the *Kṛṣṇa-bhāvanāmṛta* and sat and read to her about Krishna, and hearing about Krishna, even in that painful condition, she became totally transformed. By nature she was an energetic person. And after hearing for some time, she would become animated and exclaim, “Ooh! Ooh!”

Arcā attracted so many nice devotees to come and be with her. Devotees would come just to speak to her about Krishna and read to her about Krishna and have *kīrtana* with her. Tamal Krishna Maharaja used to visit her. Sometimes we would sit around her bed and read Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *Vidagdha-mādhava*. We would play different roles, each of us—Śivarāma Swami, Keśava Bhāratī Maharaja, Giriraj Maharaja, Tamal Krishna Goswami, and me—reading the parts of different characters. It was so nice. And she was always so sweet and so humble; she was wonderful.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Bhūrijana Prabhu would come every day and read to her.

BHAKTI BHṚṄGA GOVINDA SWAMI: Over time, Arcā-vigraha’s condition became worse. And she made a specific request to Giriraj Maharaja and me that we should think of establishing a hospice. In the course of time, I have been pulled in another direction, to the other side of the Himalayas. But it is wonderful that Giriraj Maharaja has taken up this project and that you all have formed a committed and dedicated team to see that it actually takes shape. It is the fulfillment of a wish, the wish of a Vaishnavi.

When I was in my brother’s hospice the other day, I told my mother, “This is so wonderful, that people actually create facilities like this for terminal patients”—what to speak of doing it for Vaishnavas, what to speak of doing it in Vrindavan, where the hospice is coming up. So it is a very important service that you are doing, and I am sure that by doing this service you will all be blessed by your spiritual masters and by Śrīla Prabhupāda—and you will certainly be blessed by Vaishnavas everywhere—for taking the burden and carrying the activity of helping Vaishnavas peacefully depart this world. And I am sure that you will receive an abundance of mercy from the angel Arcā-vigraha as well, because she was really an angel. She really was an angel.

## The Perspective of Caregivers

Vaishnava Care—How and Why

In 2009, devotees in Houston formed Vaishnava Care, a group committed to serving devotees who are dealing with illness and death. The following is a discussion between Giriraj Swami and the Vaishnava Care team on March 3, 2010.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Thank you for inviting me to come and speak with you today. To begin, I would like to hear from some of you. Kṛṣṇa-bhakti Mātājī, what has been your involvement?

KṚṢṆA-BHAKTI DĀSĪ: I served Jananīvāsinī Rādhā. Whenever I visited her I would take her to the temple. That is what she enjoyed most, and I took it as my primary responsibility and would drive her there. Sometimes I would just spend time with her and give her some medication as required or try to give her some comfort by massaging her body and reading books to her. But especially I would take her to the temple. She would go first to Gaura-Nitāi, stand there for a few minutes, maybe five minutes, and then look at my face, and I would say, “Okay.” Then I would walk with her and help her sit in a chair near the window. She would gasp for air. So I would massage her body, starting from the legs—massaging and massaging for ten minutes. I would massage her legs and hands and head. Then, when she looked at my face, I would ask, “Are you ready?” and she would say, “Yes,” and next I would take her to Nīla-mādhava and then to Giri-Govardhana, each time bringing her back for rest and massage.

That gave me the realization that even though we want to depend on the Lord in the mood of Krishna consciousness, the bodily pains sometimes take over. She didn’t want to leave the deities to go and sit down. She just wanted to look at them, but she couldn’t. She had to come back, because she would be gasping for air and her legs and hands would be trembling. That’s when I realized that even though we want to act on the basis of spiritual knowledge, sometimes the bodily pain is so strong that we are unable to ignore it. Even though we know that we are not the body but are the soul and that we have to take complete shelter of the lotus feet of the Lord, sometimes the bodily pain takes over and we get carried away by it.

She wanted to hear Lord Chaitanya’s pastimes, which are also my favorite. So we used to discuss them a lot, and sometimes she would laugh. I also got a lot of pleasure making her laugh with little jokes. Sometimes when I came she would say, “I want to help you,” and I would reply, “Yes, so get well quick. Then you can cook and I will come and have a feast.” Sometimes she would look at me and say, “Do you remember that pastime?” There is one she especially liked. When Gadādhara Paṇḍita was worshiping at the Ṭoṭā-gopīnātha temple in Jagannath Puri, Lord Nityananda brought some good rice, and Gadādhara went just behind the temple, picked some *śāk* (green leafy vegetable), and started cooking. When they were about ready to honor their *prasāda*, Lord Chaitanya appeared and asked, “Gadādhara, you are having a feast and you did not invite Me?” Gadādhara Paṇḍita became very shy and said, “It is only rice and *śāk*.” But Lord Chaitanya persisted: “You know I like those things and you didn’t invite Me?” And she would laugh. It was very nice. By serving these Vaishnavas, we gain a lot.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Śobhā Kiśorī?

ŚOBHĀ KIŚORĪ DĀSĪ: I was sick for a long time, and the devotees helped me a lot. They came and visited me at the hospital and brought *prasāda*. They helped me in so many ways. And after I got better, I felt that it was my turn to help the devotees in whatever way I could.

Once when I was sick late at night, I went to Guru-bhakti’s home, and she took care of me like a mother. My mum was not here—she was in India—and Guru-bhakti massaged my legs for nearly two hours. Then she tucked me inside the comforter, gave me a goodnight kiss, and put me to sleep. That simple care touched me so much and meant so much to me.

It is really good to take care of devotees. I really enjoy it, and I feel satisfied doing a little *sevā* for devotees in need.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Dāmodara-śaraṇa?

DĀMODARA-ŚARAṆA DĀSA: I was drawn to participate because my wife was ill continuously for many years—eight years—and I am basically a weak-minded person, and at that time I lost faith in Krishna. Before then, I used to chant nicely. I used to have some taste, but with this episode with my wife, I lost it. I neglected my spiritual practices. So I thought this would be a good service and a good way to control my mind. It was a selfish desire, but I thought that doing this service might help strengthen me and calm me down and move me in the direction of devotional service.

Jananīvāsinī Rādhā had a very sweet character. I knew her when she was healthy. I was attracted by her book distribution. She was so dedicated to her *guru mahārāja*, and she sold me two hundred dollars worth of books. I was delighted to get them, especially from her. She was so good-natured, and always happy. So when I saw her come down with her illness, it really troubled me. I couldn’t help her much, because I was so scared that I might lose her, lose her friendship. I had gone through the experience of my wife’s being ill—there was a lot of trauma involved, and we didn’t know what was happening to her. And now I was in a similar situation. In a way, I was troubled because Jananīvāsinī was so nice to me and even though I didn’t know her very well, I felt especially close to her. So I was scared that I would lose her, because Guru-bhakti told me that although they didn’t have the exact diagnosis for her illness, it was tending toward cancer.

One thing Jananīvāsinī told me was, “Don’t take it for granted that you will wake up tomorrow morning and be able to chant.” That instruction struck me. I felt there was a lot of truth in that, because she said, “Last week I was able to wake up in the morning, do deity worship, and chant sixteen rounds. And in just a matter of days I couldn’t do it anymore.” That was a deep realization she had, and I learned from it.

I know theoretically that we are not this body, but when I see people suffering, it is difficult for me to accept their suffering. But slowly I am learning from other devotees, like Kāminī-priyā, Nitya-kiśorī, and Kṛṣṇa-bhakti, how to serve better. Kṛṣṇa-bhakti is much older than me, but despite her advanced age and failing health, she enthusiastically extended herself so much, taking Jananīvāsinī to the temple, massaging her, and going to be with her whenever she needed. And Kāminī-priyā, who is much younger than me and was not yet initiated, was similarly enthusiastic. She had a heartfelt desire to serve Jananīvāsinī and went out of her way to help her. And she felt so bad when she could not do certain things for her. And Nitya-kiśorī also was so enthusiastic to serve. From these three devotees especially, I have gotten more strength to do this service. Seeing them serve without envy or jealousy, seeing them go out of their way with such enthusiasm, without any hidden agenda or personal motivation, has given me strength and faith that this process of *bhakti-yoga* really works and that by following I can also develop that mood of selfless service. Advaita Candra and Kalāsudhā always emphasized loving relationships, but it was when I saw these devotees that I knew the theory could actually be realized in practical life.

I was much better dealing with Vijaya Prabhu. I was happy to go meet with him.

GURU-BHAKTI DĀSĪ: I would like to add that Dāmodara and his wife took such good care of Vijaya Prabhu that Vijaya would call him his son. For his final rites we were looking for a priest, but we could not find anyone. Then Kṛṣṇa-kṛpā trained Dāmodara-śaraṇa, and he performed the final rites. And we thought it was very fitting that he was able to do the final rites for him. It was definitely Krishna’s arrangement. And even now when Vijaya Prabhu’s wife sees him, she’ll say, “This is my son, my husband’s son.” They took such nice care.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Viśākhā, would you like to speak?

VIŚĀKHĀ DĀSĪ: When my husband, Gopīnātha, was sick, everybody helped us so much. And I realized how nice it is for someone to be part of a spiritual family. When somebody is sick, he or she needs support—moral as well as physical. Now whoever is sick in the community, I feel I should help. And I am trying to do that.

When I went to the hospital to meet Vijaya Prabhu, I brought him *prasāda*. The first time I went I didn’t know what he could or could not eat. He was the one who began, asking, “What do you call those rolled things you make?” I said, “Do you mean *khandvi*?” And he said, “Yes, yes.” So I asked him, “Do you want to eat that?” And he said, “Yes, if you bring, I will eat.” So the very next morning I made *khandvi*, and on my way to work I stopped by the hospital and gave it to him, and I felt real satisfaction, because days later he passed away. Now if anybody in the devotee community, even in the congregation, is sick and doesn’t have anyone who can bring *prasāda*—like when Mañjuālī was in the hospital and her husband, Dīnanātha, was staying with her—I like to bring them *prasāda* so that at least they can have *prasāda* and don’t have to eat the hospital food.

For the last four weeks we have taken a training course for hospice volunteers so that we can serve better. They also showed us how to listen, because when a person is sick the family also needs support, somebody who can listen to them and then give advice. They taught us to listen to the family as well as to the patient.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Will you do volunteer work for the hospice where you were trained?

VIŚĀKHĀ DĀSĪ: They want us to do service. It is not mandatory, but they like if we can do service. Yes, if I get the chance I will do that.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Who else took the training?

VIŚĀKHĀ DĀSĪ: Guru-priyā.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: And Guru-priyā, are you planning to do volunteer work at the hospice?

GURU-PRIYĀ DĀSĪ: Yes, Maharaja. One day we went to the hospice to do *kīrtana*. First we did *kīrtana* outside, in the nursing area, with all the doors open. And the authorities said that if any patient liked, we could go into his or her room but that one of the hospice volunteers had to be with us. They said that if one of us got the training, we could come with the *kīrtana* party for an hour any time and they wouldn’t have to supervise us. We could do *kīrtana*, and if somebody wanted us to come into the room, if a trained devotee was there, we could go in and the hospice wouldn’t have to assign someone to us.

When I finished the course last Saturday, the lady who was training us said she was looking forward to our coming and doing the chanting. She said she wanted to know more about it and learn the words. And when we went last time, it was amazing. Two people wanted us to go into their rooms, and one lady was raising her hands. You could see she was having difficulty, but she was clapping her hands. And when we finished she said, “You don’t know what you have done for me. All of you don’t know what you just did. You made this the best day of my entire life.” She really meant it. It was amazing. She was an older lady, good looking. She said that we made it the best day of her entire life. Maybe she didn’t know what she was saying, but we did: we had brought her Lord Chaitanya’s mercy.

So it is very important. I know there are several devotees who want to do *kīrtana* in the hospice, and people like the idea, so I thought I should take the training so that maybe I could help out in this way.

Another reason I took the training is that when Vijaya Prabhu was passing away, his wife asked me to make certain decisions for her, and it was very hard for me because I was not trained. When our Vaishnava Care team requested that I ask her what funeral arrangements would be made, I did, but she became very upset. She was screaming at me, “I don’t know. My husband is not listening to me. You have to make the arrangements. My husband said you would do it.” And I was thinking, “I don’t know how to make funeral arrangements. I don’t know anything about it.” But when it happened and she expected me to do it, I thought, “Krishna is giving me this opportunity, so I have to raise myself up to the standard and learn what is involved and do it.” I didn’t ask to arrange for the funeral or make these decisions for the family, but it seemed that Krishna wanted me to do it. So I thought I should educate myself, because there will be need in the future, and then maybe I can help.

I also felt inadequate in dealing with Jananīvāsinī Rādhā. I wished I had some training so that I could help better. I hope I can be better at this service. I feel there is a need, so I want to fulfill that. It is very nice to take care of a person.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Would you like to speak, Kālindī?

KĀLINDĪ DĀSĪ: One of the main reasons I got involved is that when I was sick and had to go through surgery, the care that came my way was so very encouraging. I was amazed, and at some point I just felt that I wanted to help others. Before Jananīvāsinī passed away, we asked Saṅgītā dāsī, the hospice nurse who has organized hospice care in ISKCON, to come here and help us. And soon after Jananīvāsinī left, Saṅgītā gave a seminar on her organization, Vaishnava Care. We sent out invitations and forty devotees signed up, but at the seminar fifty people showed up. Afterward, Saṅgītā encouraged us to start a group here. She asked me to see which devotees would be interested, and eighteen devotees came forward to participate. It was overwhelming to see how ready devotees were for this.

While we were trying to care for Jananīvāsinī, we encountered many difficulties and realized that we had to become more organized to take proper care of devotees who would be in similar situations in the future. We did what we could, but it was not really the best. We realized that we could do better and should be more organized.

In this way we started the group. Now we have monthly meetings, and we encourage everyone to attend. We have a core group who come regularly, and some devotees do not come to the meetings so much but do service when required in their areas.

Apart from the terminal patients—Vijaya, Jananīvāsinī Rādhā, and Sukhada—we have tried to help other sick devotees, such as Prabha and Vṛndāvanendra. Now we also visit devotees who are in the hospital. And we are trying to establish more connections—for hospice training, for chanting at the hospice, and for other programs. We went to some funeral homes to learn what services they offer, and we have collected information about various healthcare facilities. We chanted in the nursing home where Vṛndāvanendra stayed for several weeks, and now we have an arrangement to go there every two months to chant. And we have more places where we could do chanting. Śobhā Kiśorī is approaching different facilities where devotees could go and chant at certain times. So, this is where we are heading, trying to branch out here and there.

GURU-BHAKTI DĀSĪ: I just want to add that usually when devotees are sick they come to me. I can only take care of them as a doctor, to some extent, but the caring part is not always there. I tried to arrange it, but it would strain me. But since Saṅgītā came and helped organize the Vaishnava Care team, it has become so much easier. Now when I find that someone is sick, I just send the team a note and everybody mobilizes. It makes it very easy to take care of the devotees, and I am very grateful to the team for what they do. All I have to do is tell them that someone is sick and what he or she needs, and the devotees just mobilize—they have such a nice attitude. In fact, Saṅgītā said that if she had to leave her body she wouldn’t mind coming to Houston, because we have such a caring attitude. We thought that was a great compliment coming from her, because she sees so many caregivers and is involved with so many care programs.

KĀLINDĪ DĀSĪ: Saṅgītā tells us that her Vaishnava Care team members always say they are taking inspiration from our team in Houston.

GURU-BHAKTI DĀSĪ: Actually, the credit is all Saṅgītā’s, because she helped us organize. We had the elements, but with her experience she really helped bring us together. In just the short time she was here, she helped put together the team and gave us some initial, basic training. She was here, and we had already been taking care of Jananīvāsinī Rādhā, and the team just came together. It wasn’t difficult. But the credit is hers, because she had the organizational principles down, and then it was easy to form the team. And it was very valuable to have her here when Jananīvāsinī was passing away. Without her help, we would have had a lot more difficulty, but because she was here, with her experience, we were able to cope better. It was very nice to have a person we could always consult and who could guide us expertly. So I was very grateful that she was here.

As a doctor, I take care of so many patients, but what strikes me is that it is so different when we take care of devotees, whether they are dying or not. For instance, devotees, because their bodies are generally clean and pure, need less strong medication than other people.

Actually, when a person in the material world is dying, it can be a very unpleasant experience, because the person may have so many unfulfilled desires and be very troubled. It is not usually a joyful occasion. Once in a while you see somebody who has lived his or her life fully and whose family is supportive and who is peaceful about leaving. But with devotees, whether they are sick or giving birth or dying, it is all joyful. There is a big contrast, and it gives me a lot of hope and inspiration that the process of *bhakti-yoga* really works. You see it in action. Sometimes when devotees don’t experience working in the outside world, they take the qualities of devotees for granted, but actually devotees are special.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: One last word from Kṛṣṇa-kṛpā Prabhu.

KṚṢṆA-KṚPĀ DĀSA: Actually, I am not an official member of the Vaishnava Care team, and this is my first opportunity to participate with the whole group. What they have been able to accomplish in such a short time has been wondrous. They’ve all had personal experiences that have impelled them toward this service. If you think about it, this is the largest organized group within our community. And many of the members are quite young. It is very encouraging.

One aspect of our movement that has been lacking—just from the fact that we are such a new institution—is how we take care of each other. And the work here could set a very wonderful precedent. It is easier to be compassionate and friendly in offering service when you know this is the last service the person is going to receive. We feel compelled. It is a very good place to start, where we naturally feel affection for persons with whom we may have had very little connection before beginning to assist them. I was amazed how much help came to Jananīvāsinī Rādhā and Vijaya. And we have done so much for Carolina, Vijaya’s wife, for Vṛndāvanendra, and for Sukhada. Sukhada was basically a punk rocker, and our devotees just fell all over to do whatever they could for his family. They had little connection with him, but they felt connected as Vaishnavas. And from this beginning, the concept of care can extend to caring for the healthy among us, as well. We will be able to have these kinds of peaceful relationships and really add all this love and care. We will consider, “Here I am doing this for this person who is leaving; what about those people I will be with for the next twenty, thirty years?” It is going to be a big thing. It is really going to help a lot of devotees, and it is going to help the movement profoundly, over time—what to speak of what it does to help each and every one of us as individuals participating in this effort.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: I said I came here to learn from you all how to serve devotees, and I definitely have. I think that what you are doing is wonderful. And you are feeling it yourselves; you are experiencing the rewards from your service. So I need not tell you what you already know.

I will just pick up on two themes.

In bhakti, whatever we do is for Krishna’s pleasure, and Lord Krishna says that we should perform our duties but not be attached to the results of our performance.

*karmaṇy evādhikāras te mā phaleṣu kadācana*  
*mā karma-phala- hetur bhūr mā te saṅgo ’stv akarmaṇi*

**“You have a right to perform your prescribed duty, but you are not entitled to the fruits of action. Never consider yourself the cause of the results of your activities, and never be attached to not doing your duty.” (*****Bg***  [**2.47**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.47)**)**

Just today I was listening to a talk in which Śrīla Prabhupāda said that this body is just a dress and this life just a drama. You may play a role in a drama, for three hours or whatever, and you may be a king or a queen in the play, but at the end you are just an ordinary person. You have to take off your costume. Similarly, these bodies are dresses and we are playing roles in a drama, but in the end, after a short while, we take off our costumes and this drama is over.

Then Prabhupāda chuckled and said, “One of our students became very much disturbed when I said this, and now he has left us.” But, Prabhupāda said, this is the fact: these bodies are just costumes, and the drama will end. And he said that the devotee who had left had wanted to enjoy the drama and at the same time be Krishna conscious—but that the two don’t go together. He said that you can make spiritual advancement only when you are convinced that there is no real, permanent happiness in the material world. As long as you are trying to find happiness in the material world, you can’t progress.

I considered Prabhupāda’s chuckling about his student who had left, who had wanted to find happiness in this mundane drama and at the same time be Krishna conscious, and I realized that although Prabhupāda was very compassionate and concerned about all his students—and about every living entity—he knew that people have their free will and that they can either use it properly or misuse it. Krishna has given them the choice. So, Prabhupāda was laughing at the foolishness of it—trying to enjoy the world and at the same time be Krishna conscious.

One of our devotees in Bombay, Mahāprabhu dāsa, came to Krishna consciousness just after Śrīla Prabhupāda left, and I showed him some videos of Prabhupāda. He was struck by Śrīla Prabhupāda’s demeanor and made a profound observation—that he had never seen anyone with so much compassion and love as Śrīla Prabhupāda but also had never seen anyone so detached. And he said that it was a paradox, because usually if you love or care for someone, you are attached, but Śrīla Prabhupāda had more care and more love than anyone and at the same time was totally detached.

So, we do our duty, but we should not be attached to the results. We are attached to Krishna, and we do our duty to please Him, and if we sincerely try our best to please Krishna, He will be satisfied—whether others are or not.

*anāśritaḥ karma-phalaṁ kāryaṁ karma karoti yaḥ*  
*sa sannyāsī ca yogī ca na niragnir na cākriyaḥ*

**“One who is unattached to the fruits of his work and who works as he is obligated is in the renounced order of life, and he is the true mystic, not he who lights no fire and performs no duty.” (*****Bg***  [**6.1**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/6.1)**)**

And Lord Krishna further states,

*cetasā sarva-karmāṇi*  
*mayi sannyasya mat-paraḥ*  
*buddhi-yogam upāśritya*  
*mac-cittaḥ satataṁ bhava*

**“In all activities just depend upon Me and work always under My protection. In such devotional service, be fully conscious of Me.**

*sarva-karmāṇy api sadā kurvāṇo mad-vyapāśrayaḥ*  
*mat-prasādād avāpnoti śāśvataṁ padam avyayam*

**“Though engaged in all kinds of activities, My pure devotee, under My protection, reaches the eternal and imperishable abode by My grace.” (*****Bg***  [**18.57**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/18.57)**,** [**56**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/18.56)**)**

The two departures I know best here, Jananīvāsinī Rādhā’s and Vijaya’s, were exemplary. Jananīvāsinī Rādhā was an incredible devotee—very advanced—but at the very end we weren’t allowed in the house, because of her family. So we were unable to help her as we, and she, would have wanted. And there is a practical lesson to be learned—that devotees should make living wills, written declarations of their wishes, so that family members who may be unfavorable to the devotee’s actual desires, or to Krishna consciousness don’t take control by default. Ultimately it came down to her spiritual connection with her *guru mahā-rāja*, Krishna, and the holy name. And we should use our lives to develop our eternal relationships with them.

And Vijaya’s departure was truly exemplary. To the end, he was cheerful—always appreciating devotees, always remembering Śrīla Prabhupāda, and always chanting and hearing about Krishna.

I am not aware of Sukhada. Who is he? What was his story?

KṚṢṆA-KṚPĀ DĀSA: He was a disciple of Saṅkarṣaṇa dāsa Adhikārī. He had been coming here since he was fourteen. He was a young man, about thirty, with many tattoos. He was sick for a long time with neurological complications from a head injury. He was given notice that he had six months to live, and we offered him and his family our assistance. And within days he died. As he desired, we dressed him as a devotee, applying *tilaka* and *mahā candana* and flower garlands, and performed *kīrtana* at the funeral home and then at the crematorium. And we offered whatever assistance we could to the family.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: One thought that strikes me is that we want people to know about us and what we can offer so that they can let us know of their situation as soon as possible. Do we have information about Vaishnava Care on the ISKCON Houston website? It is a major service that we offer.

KĀLINDĪ DĀSĪ: We were a little hesitant about it—how much we can offer the congregation and the public—because anyone can visit the website.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: That is a legitimate concern.

As Kṛṣṇa-kṛpā said, it is often easier to express your care and appreciation and affection to someone who is leaving. It is easier because they are rising above the bodily platform, realizing that they are not the body but the soul and that their real relationship with others is based on the soul, with the Supersoul, the Supreme Soul, at the center. In that mood of preparing to leave their bodies, they are not interested in the petty things of the world. They focus on what’s really essential, their relationship with Krishna and guru and Vaishnavas. So it is good, very good, for us to be in their association; it is an extremely rewarding experience.

I am friends with a rabbi who came to a large congregation from another, smaller one. He confided in me that he sometimes felt depleted in his new temple. I asked, “Which activities drain you, and which nourish you?” And he said that one thing that nourished him was ministering to people who were dying. “It may sound odd,” he said, “but when I minister to people who are dying, I enter into a very sacred, spiritual space.” “Yes,” I replied. “That’s true; I feel the same way.” Leaving one’s body is a momentous event, and especially if the person is God conscious, it heightens the spiritual atmosphere and the consciousness of everyone associated with him or her.

On the idea that caring for the dying is a beginning point from which we can expand our care and appreciation and affection to others who aren’t leaving their bodies, my friend Sridhar Swami once gave me a book, popular at the time, called *Tuesdays with Morrie*. Morrie was a professor of sociology at

Brandeis University who developed ALS, in which you gradually lose the function of your muscles to the point where you don’t digest, don’t swallow, don’t breathe. It leads to death—it is inevitable—but it takes time. You gradually lose more and more muscle function. Morrie had the idea that after someone dies there’s a funeral and people express so much love and affection and appreciation for the person that they never expressed while he was alive. It is nice that they express appreciation, but they don’t get the satisfaction of having done it while the person was there to hear it, to receive it.

I remember that after Tamal Krishna Goswami left, so many people said, “I wasn’t expecting it. I wanted to tell him something but I never did and now he’s gone.” So, Morrie had the idea to have a funeral for himself before he died. It was like a regular funeral, and people came forward and said all the things they would have said at his funeral about him and his life, but he was there to hear it. He got to relish their affection and appreciation, and they got the satisfaction of expressing it to him when he could hear it.

And then again, none of us really knows how long any of us have. We don’t want to be in a situation where we left something pending. It could be a disagreement we never resolved or an expression of appreciation we never made.

Carl Herzig, Tamal Krishna Goswami’s brother, once told me that whenever any of his family members leave the house, they say to the other family members, “I love you,” and the others reciprocate, because when you leave the house you never know for sure if you will return; you never know if you will live to come back. By leaving the house saying, “I love you,” and then the other reciprocating, “I love you,” whatever happens after that, the last memory will be favorable. You don’t want a situation where you have a fight, someone storms out of the house, and then there’s a mishap and he or she never comes back, and the last thing was that fight.

Taking Kṛṣṇa-kṛpā’s point, it would be good for us if we could develop that consciousness that we don’t know how long any of us have and we don’t want to leave anything pending, like, “Oh, we had this grudge and we could have dealt with it, but we just never did, and now . . .” Or, “I always wanted to tell this devotee how much he helped me, how much he meant to me, but I never did.”

Our specific mission here is to deal with terminal patients, but our broader mission is to care for Vaishnavas, devotees, in general, especially the sick and aged, and ultimately the dying. But in a sense, we are all sick and aging and going to die. But we get so embroiled in these dramas and so identify with these costumes that we forget our true spiritual identities and kinship. Still, we can consciously try to rise above that and see ourselves and each other as spiritual entities and appreciate the good in others and serve them however we can. It can even happen when someone is dying that something negative comes into our mind—“Oh, he did this to me.”—it happens even at the very end, but then one is more in the mood to let it go: “Anyway, he is dying. Now we should just come forward and help him, and let it go. Forgive and forget.”

Saṅgītā began with hospice care, but that same mood of care can extend to long-term care, for people with long-term illnesses or just old age, and to acute care, for people who are just sick. She is concerned that everyone is cared for, in every way.

But this is a good beginning. And our mission, our most specific, most immediate mission, is terminal care. Either because the world is so complicated or because we are so stunted, we need specialists in every field. So we need this group. And we also want to take care of the children; we want to have a school for them. So many things. And everything requires training, although, of course, someone could have an aptitude, be a natural. So I am glad to see that you all are receiving such nice training.

As Kṛṣṇa-kṛpā has expressed, life is a continuation and what we call death is just a transition. But death is a critical event. It is the final examination, and to help someone at the time of death—to take care of the person physically and spiritually and help him or her remember Krishna— is a most important service. A perfect example is the service Īśvara Purī rendered to his spiritual master, Mādhavendra Purī. He would personally clean his stool and urine and at the same time recite verses and sing songs about Krishna to encourage his guru’s mood of Krishna consciousness. So, following in the footsteps of Īśvara Purī, we want to care for devotees physically and at the same time help them remember Krishna. Devotees already want to remember Krishna; we just have to help them. And from that service to his *guru mahārāja*, Īśvara Purī got the greatest blessing: that he would attain pure love of Krishna.

*īśvara-purī gosāñi kare śrīpāda-sevana*  
*svahaste karena mala-mūtrādi mārjana*

**“Īśvara Purī, the spiritual master of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, performed service to Mādhavendra Purī, cleaning up his stool and urine with his own hand.**

*nirantara kṛṣṇa-nāma karāya smaraṇa*  
*kṛṣṇa-nāma kṛṣṇa-līlā śunāya anukṣaṇa*

**“Īśvara Purī was always chanting the holy name and pastimes of Lord Kṛṣṇa for Mādhavendra Purī to hear. In this way he helped Mādhavendra Purī remember the holy name and pastimes of Lord Kṛṣṇa at the time of death.**

*tuṣṭa hañā purī tāṅre kailā āliṅgana*  
*vara dilā—‘kṛṣṇe tomāra ha-uka prema-dhana’*

**“Pleased with Īśvara Purī, Mādhavendra Purī embraced him and gave him the benediction that he would be a great devotee and lover of Kṛṣṇa.” (*****Cc Antya***  [**8.28–30**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-antya/8.28)**)**

The devotees we are serving are all exalted souls, and by serving them at the end of their lives we get their blessings— they are in a position to give blessings—as Īśvara Purī received from his spiritual master.

Hare Krishna.

Any other questions or comments?

LINDY: One time you said that not only is Lord Krishna eternal and our relationship with Him eternal, but that all spirit souls are eternal and our relationships with them are also eternal in Krishna consciousness, and that therefore our relationships with other living entities, especially those who are devotees, are also important, just like our relationship with Krishna.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Yes, that is a very expansive vision. Your statement reminds me of a letter Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote to Tuṣṭa Kṛṣṇa dāsa. There had been quarrelling between different groups of devotees, and Tuṣṭa Kṛṣṇa, who was attached to the leader of one group, wrote Śrīla Prabhupāda extolling this devotee, Siddha-svarūpa dāsa. Apparently, he wanted to follow him and not others. And Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote back, “You speak of pure devotee, that he is *śaktyāveśa-avatāra*, that we should obey him only—these things are the wrong idea. If anyone thinks like that, that a pure devotee should be obeyed and no one else, that means he is a nonsense.

We advise everyone to address one another as ‘Prabhu.’ *Prabhu* means ‘master,’ so how the master should be disobeyed? Others are also pure devotees. All of my disciples are pure devotees. Anyone sincerely serving the spiritual master is a pure devotee—it may be Siddha-svarūpa, or others, non-Siddha-svarūpa. This must be very clearly stated. It is not that only your Siddha-svarūpa is a pure devotee and not others. Do not try to make a faction. Siddha-svarūpa is a good soul. But others should not be misled. Anyone who is surrendered to the spiritual master is a pure devotee, it doesn’t matter if Siddha-svarūpa or non-Siddha-svarūpa. Amongst ourselves we should respect others as Prabhu, master. As soon as we distinguish here is a pure devotee, here is a non-pure devotee, that means I am a nonsense.”

So, in Krishna consciousness our relationships are eternal and integral to our service to Krishna—to guru and Gaurāṅga.

KṚṢṆA-KṚPĀ DĀSA: You were saying how exalted the Vaishnavas are to disconnect themselves from their attachments.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Those two in particular: Jananīvāsinī Rādhā and Vijaya—and many others.

KṚṢṆA-KṚPĀ DĀSA: We may have trouble seeing ourselves—or others—in this position. We may have more Krishna consciousness within ourselves than we give ourselves credit for. And because we do have that Krishna consciousness in us, when we are faced with a terminal illness, we easily disconnect ourselves from our material attachments and focus our efforts on Krishna consciousness, which is the only thing that really matters then. Devotees then do not even care for their bodily pains.

Does anyone in this room not have Krishna consciousness to one degree or another? And has anyone not helped bring someone to Krishna consciousness?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Everyone has. Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura said, “If I could deliver one soul back to Godhead I would consider my mission successful.” So that’s true. We all, in some way or another, to one degree or another, have brought people to Krishna consciousness—like Nimāi Sundarī brought Prabha, and now she is a very good devotee.

VIŚĀKHĀ DĀSĪ: In one meeting we were discussing that we are taking care of devotees who are sick, but sometimes nobody is sick. So what should we do if nobody is sick? What should we do to further our cause?

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: That is an excellent question. Any suggestions?

KĀMINĪ-PRIYĀ DĀSĪ: We can get more and more training, medical training. We should know at least a little. We should not only go and chant. Sometimes sick devotees need practical medical assistance. With Jananīvāsinī Rādhā, I was not able to even turn on the oxygen machine. That always hurt me, that I didn’t know how to do those things. At least small things we should learn to do, and we can utilize the time in that way.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Everyone should read *The Final Journey:* *Complete Hospice Care for Departing Vaishnavas* by Saṅgītā dāsī. It is very well written, instructive, and informative.

The first time I invited Saṅgītā to Houston, we met Dr. Porter Storey, an important figure in hospice and palliative care. It was a very interesting meeting. She asked him many questions about the physical and even emotional care of patients, and he said, “Focus on the spiritual—that is most important. The physical and emotional are temporary.” And he was a regular medical practitioner.

I have visited a number of hospices, and by seeing the differences I was able to separate the essence from the details, not only in terms of care but also in terms of the physical facility, because we were building the hospice in Vrindavan and had to think about the design. So even just visiting a hospice can be instructive, especially if you want to create a physical facility. But by talking with people—hearing what they say—you can learn even more. I’ve learned much just by going to hospices and talking with medical directors, nurses, administrators, volunteers, and those patients who were willing. By talking to all different types of people, you learn.

One of the best hospices anywhere is San Diego Hospice and The Institute for Palliative Medicine, recognized as the world leader in hospice education. Not only is it extraordinary as a hospice, but it is an exemplary institution. I haven’t walked into another institution that is so well organized, with absolutely everything at such a high standard. Our guide explained that because at the time of death the patients and their loved ones are so sensitive and filled with emotion, the hospice trains everyone—even the janitors—how to deal intelligently and compassionately and competently with people, because a relative of a patient who is in intense pain or is dying may come out into the hallway to get some information or help and the only person there may be the janitor. So the management carefully and systematically and thoroughly trains everyone: janitors, technicians, kitchen staff, maintenance staff—people who aren’t meant to interact with the patients and their families but who may happen to. They really train them to be considerate, kind, patient, and helpful, and they give them all the information they may require.

One good thing about hospices is that they are very open to anything that could give comfort or encouragement to a patient. At the San Diego Hospice there is a lady who comes in once a week and plays the harp. Sometimes people bring around pets so a patient can play with a puppy or whatever. And in that mood they may also allow *kīrtana* parties. They don’t mind what it is as long as it can give some solace or relief and the patient wants it. And that can apply to Krishna consciousness. At the San Diego Hospice we put books in the library. And I am sure other hospices would welcome our books, too.

Many hospices now are sensitive to the fact that people from different cultures have different traditions regarding death. For example, some traditions say that when a person leaves the body, you must vacate the room immediately. Other traditions say that you must remain in the room with the body for a certain period. The hospice staff should be aware of and sensitive to the traditions of the patient’s family. Otherwise they could make a mistake and cause some offense. The San Diego Hospice wanted to know about Hinduism, and in that context they invited me to speak on death and dying in the Vedic tradition.

To begin, I explained that the basic text for Hindus is the *Bhagavad-gītā* and that the *Gītā* teaches that the living entity is an immortal spiritual soul, distinct from the perishable physical body. I also explained what happens to the soul when it leaves the body and what the patients and their loved ones can do to help the soul reach the best destination. The whole philosophy of Krishna consciousness is relevant. And it can become a reciprocal relationship: the hospice staff can learn from us spiritually, and we can learn from them medically.

So it has been a very beneficial relationship. We gave the San Diego Hospice staff quite a lot of spiritual information, and they gave us medical information. And many hospice people are very spiritual. When I went back to San Diego the next time, many of the staff wanted to meet me again. So we had a program for them in the temple complex, and many came. They were so inspired, with the potential to become a wonderful adjunct group with evolved spiritual beings, some taking up the practice of *bhakti-yoga*. Most were Christians, but they were open and receptive. Some even wanted to go to India.

In *Death: The Final Stage of Growth*, Elisabeth KṜbler-Ross, the great pioneer in the field of death and dying and advocate for hospice and palliative care, wrote of Hinduism (and Buddhism), “At a time of increased interest in Eastern religion and philosophy, not as a substitute for our more familiar ideas, but rather as a source of complementary ideas to be integrated with our own, this . . . should provide you much food for thought. Some of you may find the descriptions of death and the afterlife as it relates to life here on this earth a more comforting explanation of the relationship between life and death than that offered in many Western religions. All of you should find it an interesting contrast to our current Western tendency to dissociate death from life.” So there is a lot we can do with hospice people, learning from them and even giving them tools to help them deal with Hindu patients and with life and death in general.

In any case, we want to help everyone, and especially devotees. One great sage prayed, “Let me become a sincere servant of the devotees, because by serving them one can achieve unalloyed devotional service unto the lotus feet of the Lord. The service of devotees diminishes all miserable material conditions and develops within one a deep devotional love for the Supreme Personality of Godhead.” (*SB* [3.7.19](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-3/7.19) as quoted in *NOD* Ch 12)

In fact, everyone is ultimately a devotee. Every soul is by nature a servant of God, Krishna, and by association, by receiving beneficent loving care from sincere, pure devotees, a person’s dormant love for God—and for His devotees—can awaken.

## All Obstacles Removed: Caring for Mother Kaumudakī

Based on a talk by Kriyā-śakti dāsī at Mother Kaumudakī’s memorial program in New Dwaraka, Los Angeles, July 31, 2008.

I first met Kaumudakī in 2005, in what she thought would be the last year of her life. She had been diagnosed with cancer and had decided to go to India to leave her body. For three months we stayed together in one room of a small bungalow next to the LA temple. We shopped for medications, beddings, and other supplies, as she prepared to leave for India. When I realized that she was also shopping for medical supplies for leaving her body, I thought, “Wow, it takes so much courage for her to be doing this.” She had a long list of hospice-care items recommended by Mother Saṅgītā and would make a check mark beside each item to make sure she had everything so that no one would be put into any kind of hardship because of her negligence. She never complained or expressed any kind of sadness or reservation about what she was facing.

Kaumudakī went to India with a visa that could be extended for one year, thinking she would leave her body within that time. She also visited Tibet, which had been a long-cherished desire. But when a year had passed and she was still alive— and an infection and severe pain had developed in her leg—she returned to Vancouver, where as a Canadian citizen she could receive treatment. The infection in her leg turned out to be cancer, and soon she was given a prognosis of three months left to live. After a month of endeavoring to take care of herself, she admitted herself into a hospice in Vancouver—and asked me to come and help take care of her.

By the time I arrived, Kaumudakī had been bedridden for a couple of weeks. She had gone very quickly from being mobile to being bedridden, and that was a difficult transition for her. It was like she had lost the last bit of her independence, and the imminence of death became very real. In India she had begun to have some discomfort in her leg, and it had become unbearably painful. It turned out that the cancer had mutated, spread to her lymph glands, and broken through the skin. It began in her leg but later moved up the side of her body. She was taking pain medication every four hours.

Her room was in a little bit of disarray, so I cleaned and tidied up. I set up some pictures of Śrīla Prabhupāda on a wall shelf along with a small altar for my Gopāla deity. From there, seated on a small brass rocking chair, Gopāla Lāla presided over the room. Sometimes I would seat Him on Kaumudakī’s tray table and she would touch His chair to make it rock back and forth, saying, “You are such a good boy, Lāla.” And she had a small picture of Śrīla Prabhupāda, which was the focus of her meditation.

I slept in her room and showered down the hall.
