One evening, after we’d bid each other good night, she said, “I need a priest.” So I turned on a small light, went to her bedside, and said, “Do you have something you need to talk about?” She said, “Yes, but I don’t know if you’re the right person.” “I’m the only one here,” I said. “But I don’t know if you are the right person.” I assured her, “Whatever you say to me is in the strictest confidence; I’ll never repeat anything.” “Okay,” she said, “get a pen and paper,” and for two hours she poured out her heart, things that she had held inside for so long—about her life, her trials, and her tribulations. When she finished, she said, “That was a lot of stuff.” She had unburdened a lifetime of confessions, traumatic experiences, introspective thoughts, personal regrets for wrongs or things she wished she had done differently. I said, “It’s okay, now it’s finished. We’ll do *pūjā*, purify the room, and start fresh.” So I washed the room and sprayed Rādhā-kuṇḍa water and Nṛsiṁha oil. By then it was midnight, and she had fallen asleep. Then I read the *Bhagavad-gītā*, the English translations, from beginning to end, finishing at about two-thirty in the morning. Then I took rest.

The next night, after we turned the lights out, I said, “Good night, Kaumudakī. Hare Krishna.” And she said, “Good night, Kriyā-śakti. Hare Krishna.” After a minute, she said, “There’s more.” So I got up and sat by her bed, and again she poured out her heart. “I’ve never told anyone these things,” she said. She spoke until she had no more to say. And then she told me, “You know, I think there is something to this confession. I feel so much better.” She had revealed what she had kept inside for her whole life; she cleared her heart and felt relief.

Again I cleaned the area and sprayed it with Rādhā-kuṇḍa water and Nṛsiṁha oil, and again I read the *Bhagavad-gītā* from beginning to end, finishing at about two-thirty or three in the morning. It was very purifying to read the *Bhagavad-gītā* from start to finish in one swoop . . . two nights in a row.

The next morning, George the cleaning man came around, and Kaumudakī said to me, “Where are the papers that you wrote everything down on?” I said, “I have them here.” She said, “Rip them into tiny pieces and put them in George’s trash. Put them in the trash.”

Kaumudakī’s health was failing. There were times that she was not conscious all day and other times, at random moments, when she would speak. Sometimes she would suddenly start clapping her hands, or hold them in a *mudrā* position, prayerlike under her chin, even in her comatose condition. One time, when she had seemed unconscious, she opened her eyes and picked up her bead bag to chant. She said, “Who are all these devotees in the room? Is there a picnic? There are so many devotees here—where will they all fit? All I can see is faces, like a patchwork quilt. Please tell them to excuse me if I can’t keep my eyes open.” The nurses told me that it was not unusual for people in their last stage to have visions like this, to see “the other side.”

One night—it was very late, two or three o’clock—Kaumu-dakī woke me up and said, “Write this down—get a pen and paper.” She had previously worked as a secretary, and when Śrīla Prabhupāda came to Los Angeles, she served as his. Prabhupāda always read her letters, she told me, and only once made a correction: a tiny check mark beside the error. So, she started dictating a letter to me: “Capital D, ‘Dear,’ capital S, ‘So-and-so,’ capital A, ‘All glories to,’ capital S, ‘Śrīla,’ capital P, ‘Prabhupāda.’ Period. Capital P, ‘Please accept my humble obeisances.’ Period.” It took her five minutes to say this, and then she dozed off. When she woke up a few minutes later, she said, “Okay, read me what you’ve got.” So I read, “Dear So-and-so, Please accept my humble obeisances. All glories to Śrīla Prabhupāda.” She looked at me kind of suspiciously and said, “Is that all?” I said, “Yes.” So she went through it all in her mind again and restarted, but then she dozed off again.

Another time, at around 2 a.m. (it could happen at any time of the day or night), she said to me, “Okay, write this down. You have to make a telephone list.” I said, “We already have a telephone list.” (I was trying to get out of rewriting the same list.) She said, “No, no, this one is different.” I said, “Okay.” So she started to rattle off a whole list of names of people she wanted me to contact after her passing, and then she said, “Now get the telephone numbers for these.” So I got out her personal phonebook and looked up the telephone numbers—home phone and cellphone. Some numbers she dictated to me. She had me read back to her the numbers beside each person’s name. Then she said, “Okay, now put them in alphabetical order.” “I’m not going to put them in alphabetical order,” I said. “You’ve got to stop this. You have to give it up.” She had a moment of realization and we both laughed, and we laughed over it for a long time.

When I first started this journal, I asked Kaumudakī if I could send the writings to some devotees who were interested. She said, “No, I don’t want that.” Then later in the day—she had a habit of waving her hand as if giving blessings—she said, “All right, do what you want. It’s okay.” After I had written a few entries and sent them, I read them to her to get her permission to write further entries. After hearing them she said, “Okay, it’s all right.” She was a very private person and didn’t want her condition, or her daily happenings, to be broadcast. That really wasn’t what I wanted to record or what I wanted the devotees to remember, and I never said anything about her medical condition in any of the journal entries. But you should know that her condition was very painful. The cancer had mutated to another form, settled in her leg, gone to the lymph glands, traveled through the nerves, and broken through the skin. The nurses said it was like leprosy. And what she was experiencing was very painful. But I never once heard her complain. She could have as much pain management as was necessary for her. At one time, when the pain became a different kind of pain—it wasn’t inside but more topical, on her leg—they had to mix a cocktail of different medications to control it.

She would call out, “Oh! Oh!” like a complaint, but I thought it was more like a comment. This was the most I ever heard her complain—a comment like, “Oh! Oh!” And in that situation, the pain is unbearable—the kind of pain she was experiencing, the way the disease was acting to break through the skin. She was bandaged from her toe all the way up her leg and around her abdomen. Later the disease started to move up the side of her body. She often expressed to me that she hoped and prayed that she would leave her body before it got to a place where she could see it. The bandages were hidden under her blankets, but were it to move to her hand or somewhere like that, she thought that would be really difficult to bear.

So, there were times when she would wake me up, and when she spoke I would write things down. She had these apparently random moments, random thoughts, and I decided, “I’m not taking a chance that I’ll misword or forget what she said.” So I made a point to write them down, sometimes as she was speaking.

Once, during her last week, in a moment of clarity, Kaumu-dakī looked me straight in the eye as I bent over her, and said, “We are guided, Kriyā, divinely guided.” Another time she said, “I see trees.” She had her eyes closed and was moving her hands like she was describing a scene and said, “I see trees, grouped in all different colors. There are children, toddlers, all smiling.” I asked, “Do you see Krishna with the children?” She raised her head and opened her eyes and looked at me like I was stupid or crazy. “Krishna is everywhere,” she said, “Don’t you know that? Krishna is everywhere.” Then she laid her head back down, closed her eyes again, and said, “He is in everything. I’m a little boy. I want to say I love you to the children, to the trees.” I asked, “Are you one of the children?” And she said, “Yes.”

Another time, like a small child, she said, “I finally get to go to Krishna! I finally get to go to Krishna!”

One afternoon Kaumudakī seemed to be in an anxious state and was waving her arms in front of her, as if frantically searching for something. “I want Krishna so much,” she said. “How can I get to Krishna?” I told her, “Chant Hare Krishna and you can get to Him.” “It is so subtle, Kriyā,” she said. “The material is subtle. What’s between me and Krishna? Can you see anything there?” She had her eyes closed and was waving her hands to try and clear the way in front of her. “Can you see anything there?” she asked. “I only see Śrīla Prabhupāda between you and Krishna,” I replied. “He’ll bring you to Krishna.”

Another time she was pleading, “I want to go to Rādhā-kuṇḍa. I want to go to Rādhā-kuṇḍa.” So I said, “Okay, let’s go right now.” Then she had a vague realization of her present condition and asked, “But how will I go? Will you wheel the hospital bed?” I told her, “No, no, I’ll carry you.” And she started to sing like a little girl: “Time to go to Rādhā-kuṇḍa. Time to go to Rādhā kuṇḍa.” So we went on a visualized journey to Rādhā-kuṇḍa: “Put your hands on the chains that lead to the water. Feel the stone steps under your feet. Sprinkle some drops on your head and feel the cool water as you enter the sacred *kuṇḍa*.” Another time she called me to her side and said, “I am so fortunate that you are here to show me the way of the Absolute Truth at this time of death. Fortunately there are no material obstacles.” “It is my good fortune to be here,” I replied. “All glories to Śrīla Prabhupāda.”

“I always thought that Krishna consciousness was for everyone else,” she said one day. When I asked, “Do you feel Krishna in your consciousness?” she replied, “Yes, I always thought it was for everyone else.”

The Sunday before Kaumudakī left her body was Ekādaśī, and we both knew that it would be the last one that she would observe in this lifetime, so we made a conscious vow to follow the Ekādaśī and meditate on its meaning. It was Yoginī Ekādaśī, the day when, as we read, “Lord Hari can free one from disease that results from karmic reaction, remove curses, and award supreme liberation. This Ekādaśī delivers those that are drowning in the vast ocean of material existence and transports them to the shore of the spiritual world. In all the three worlds it is the chief of all sacred fasting days.”

So we were very attentive, careful of what we ate, rejecting some ice cream (which helped her take some very bitter medicine) because it contained corn sweetener. Kaumudakī was in and out of consciousness all day. At one point, all of a sudden, she woke up and said, “Just my luck: the lip balm that I am using—I’ll bet it has some corn in it.” She was very conscious of observing the fast. I had read the list of ingredients and assured her that the lip balm was okay. She was very relieved.

By this time I was spoonfeeding her, and she ate only a little bit of fruit, probably a tablespoonful, during the whole day.

At the end of the day, she stretched her arm out as if to reach something far away and said, “I always thought that Radha and Krishna were way up on the shelf, way high, and that I could never reach Them. There was tall grass growing in front of Them, and I could barely find my way there. I could never find my way to Radha and Krishna. But somebody has mowed the grass. It is all flat and clear. I can find my way now. There are no impediments.”

I couldn’t help but notice that Kaumudakī had used the word *mowed*. I was puzzled. “Who mows?” I asked myself. And then it dawned on me: “A gardener!” In the story of Yoginī Ekādaśī, Hemamāli was a gardener in the court of King Kuvera, who was a steadfast devotee of Lord Shiva. It was Hemamāli’s duty to bring flowers to the king every day in time for Shiva’s *pūjā*. But one day the flowers weren’t there. After some investigation, the king discovered that when Hemamāli should have been bringing the flowers, he had been enjoying with his wife. Enraged, King Kuvera cursed Hemamāli to be afflicted with leprosy and cast down to the lower planets.

And so Hemamāli fell, and he wandered, with no food or drink, suffering from the disease. But he also had his mind fixed on Lord Shiva, the greatest Vaishnava, or devotee of Lord Krishna. In this way, his consciousness was gradually purified, and because of his piety he remembered his past life. One day, Hemamāli had the good fortune to come across the effulgent Mārkaṇḍeya Ṛṣi. Hemamāli offered his obeisances from afar, but the sage, ever concerned about the welfare of others, bade him come forward and inquired of his situation. Hemamāli confessed his past misdeeds and begged, “Please help me.” Mārkaṇḍeya Ṛṣi, pleased with Hemamāli’s honesty, told him of the Yoginī Ekādaśī fast day, by which Hemamāli could be relieved of his terrible curse. Hemamāli dutifully observed the fast, became free from his suffering condition, and was reinstated as King Kuvera’s personal gardener.

Scripture concludes, “For one who fasts on this day, the holy mother Ekādaśī destroys heaps of sinful reactions and makes one most pious.”

Yoginī Ekādaśī relieves one from disease that is the result of karmic reaction. Kaumudakī often spoke of her disease as a curse. “This is like a curse,” she would often say. And this Ekādaśī relieves one of curses—and awards supreme liberation. This Ekādaśī was very, very powerful, and only thereafter did I begin to appreciate the power of these two days that occur between the waxing and the waning of the moon each month. Anyone, even if he or she only accidentally follows an Ekādaśī, gets benefit. It can remove heaps of sin. So, I was thinking, “What is a heap? How much is a heap?” You think of chits, and if you throw them in a pile, how many will equal a heap? How many chits, bills, do we have for so many sinful activities that we’ve performed that would form a heap? It is beyond my comprehension. These Ekādaśīs can destroy heaps of sin.

So we observed the Ekādaśī, and the next day’s breakfast time was between 4 and 5:30 in the morning. I had soaked some oatmeal the night before and set my alarm clock. To break the fast, I took some of the oatmeal water and dripped it into her mouth, and I also took some, and so we had kept our Ekādaśī vow and broken fast during the proper time.

Everything about this particular Ekādaśī was exactly what Kaumudakī needed. The boons that went along with it astounded me. It was so very powerful.

That morning, Dvādaśī, was Monday, June 30th, the last day of the month. When Kaumudakī woke up, she asked what day it was, and I told her. “Oh, phooey,” she said. “I’ve got to live at least another day.” She had often commented that she hoped she would be able to live until July, because she would receive a government check on the first of the month and wanted to give some donations and sponsor the feast for her departure, which she knew her family would attend. The rest of the day was touch and go, with a lot of sleeping—as were Tuesday and Wednesday.

Kaumudakī’s wish was that when she passed away there would be no sounds in the room other than Śrīla Prabhupāda’s voice. So pretty much from the time I had arrived, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *bhajanas* and *japa* had been playing continuously, 24/7, even through the night. She particularly liked hearing *japa*, especially Śrīla Prabhupāda’s solo chanting: “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare.” Once, she said, “I know all the words to the song, I just don’t know how to dance to it.” My sense was that she was referring to chanting on her beads—dancing to the mantra—which had become very difficult for her.

On most days, I would go out and get groceries or run an errand, but some days Kaumudakī would say, “Could you please not leave today.” And so I would stay with her the whole day. I would go out of the room as little as possible. I showered down the hall and slept in her room on a chaise longue that became flat, with a pad to make it more comfortable. I would say, “Good night, Kaumudakī. Hare Krishna.” And she would say, “Good night Kriyā-śakti. Hare Krishna.” And we would go to sleep.

So, Thursday morning began as other mornings. There were some wakeful moments for her as she drifted in and out of consciousness. I would get up and chant *japa* and would always have Hare Krishna sound vibrations playing in the room.

Then, at around eleven, Kaumudakī started to gurgle in her throat—it was very difficult for her to breathe. I asked the nurse to come listen and maybe administer a medication to dry up the fluid, but seeing that the difficulty was intermittent she said, “No, it is not quite advanced enough.” Within fifteen minutes, however, her breath became more labored, so the nurse said, “Okay, we can start.” By noon, every breath brought a heavy drowning, choking sound—the death rattle. At one, the medication still hadn’t had any effect, so the nurse administered another dose. At two o’clock, her condition was still the same.

I remembered an experience I’d had of almost drowning— how you get washed over by a wave and can barely get your head up and take a breath before another wave comes crashing over. A couple of times Kaumudakī cried out, “Save me! Save me!” just as someone drowning calls for help. At one point she said, “I don’t want to live anymore.” I called Saṅgītā and asked if there was any other kind of medication that might help, and she gave me the names of two others.

I never left the room, and I kept checking to make sure that Prabhupāda’s *japa* was playing in Kaumudakī’s ears. Sometimes people would come to the door, and I would speak with them there, just outside, careful that there not be any other sound in the room.

By four o’clock there was still no change and I was at my wit’s end. Kaumudakī was the one going through it all, but I was the one who was rattled. It was just so intense to see my friend suffer and not be able to do anything to help. I said, “Krishna, what is this? Your devotee is suffering! Can’t You help her? You say You’ll save Your devotee—You make this promise—but who’s going to believe You? Who will believe You when Your devotee still suffers? How much is enough?” I was crying out to the point of pleading, almost angry with Krishna.

Then a wave of realization washed over me: “I’m not in control of this. This has nothing to do with me; it’s out of my hands. This is completely in Krishna’s hands. Krishna is in control, and I’m only a witness.” It was such a relief that I stood up and started dancing around the room. I was singing, “*Jaya Rādhe Jaya* *Rādhe Rādhe, Jaya Rādhe Jaya Śrī Rādhe/ Jaya Kṛṣṇa* . . .” I was dancing around the room and singing, it was such a relief—it was so amazing.

Then I started cleaning. Jagariṇī dāsī and I were recently talking about compulsive behavior—OCD. I said, “What is that—obsessive cleaning disorder?” (instead of obsessive-compulsive disorder), and we laughed. So, to occupy myself, I started cleaning the room. I moved everything unnecessary into the bathroom and cleared the area around Kaumudakī’s head. I got down on my hands and knees and cleaned the whole floor, making the entire room as clean and pure as I could, making the room fit for the Supreme Lord to enter and take Kaumudakī back home, meditating also on the cleansing effect of the holy name. Then I sprinkled her body with Rādhā-kuṇḍa water and Nṛsiṁha oil.

At five o’clock I stepped halfway into the bathroom to take a phone call from Jagariṇī. I could still hear Kaumudakī’s heavy, labored breathing, which had been going on for five hours. Then suddenly her breathing went quiet. I quickly hung up and rushed back into the room. When I got to Kaumudakī’s side, she was breathing normally. I was amazed. I sat by her, and for the next half hour her breathing was completely normal. Her head, which had been in a tense position, was relaxed on the pillow. Her eyes had tears in their corners, and then she opened them.

There’s a phrase in the *Bhāgavatam*—“Just as a dying man looks at a picture of the Supreme Personality of Godhead”— and that’s all I could think of. Then I remembered a story I had heard of Yuga-dharma Prabhu’s passing. I don’t know if I would have had the presence of mind to do what I did if I hadn’t heard it. Devotees in Vrindavan had advised that a picture of Śrīla Prabhupāda be placed right in front of his face. He was in a coma, but the devotees said that it didn’t matter, that he shouldn’t hear or have his family, friends, or any other attachments before him—only Śrīla Prabhupāda. And right before Yuga-dharma passed away, he opened one eye, looked at the picture of Śrīla Prabhupāda, closed his eye, and left. So I held a picture of Śrīla Prabhupāda in front of Kaumudakī for her to see. She looked and beheld the smiling face of our dear Śrīla Prabhupāda. Then she closed her eyes, took four short breaths, and left.

I started crying and chanting Hare Krishna. I placed a *tulasī* leaf in her mouth, marked her body with *tilaka*, put Vrindavan dust on her head, misted her body with Rādhā-kuṇḍa water, put Nṛsiṁha oil around her body, and sat by her bed and chanted.

Minutes later, the telephone rang, but I was not going to answer. Then I thought, “No, I can do this.” And when I answered, I heard Giriraj Swami’s voice. I said, “Maharaja, is that you?” He said, “Yes.” And I said, “You’re the brahman. You’re the brahman that’s entered the room.” He said, “What?” I said, “Kaumudakī just left her body. You are the brahman. You’ve entered the room.” If an unexpected brahman visitor comes into the room after someone leaves, it is a very auspicious sign; it indicates that the soul has attained liberation— gone back to Godhead. So, Maharaja and I talked for forty-five minutes, and I told him about Kaumudakī’s passing and the days before. During the days before her departure, I had been wondering how I would know that everything was all right, how Krishna would show me. So I was extremely grateful that He had given me a sign.

Then another devotee came—Padyāvalī dāsī—and she and I spoke and read together; sang *bhajanas*, including *Ye Ānilo* *Prema Dhana*, Narottama dāsa Ṭhākura’s song for departed Vaishnavas; and wrote the departure notice for our departed godsister. We chanted and covered Kaumudakī’s body with a *hari-nāma chaddar* (shawl); a picture of Lord Nṛsiṁha from Mayapur had been on her chest since morning. We pulled apart a rose flower garland and covered Kaumudakī with the petals. When the nurses came in, they all commented, “Oh look, she’s closed her mouth,” because her mouth had been open when I’d put the *tulasī* leaf in, but she’d closed it sometime after.

All the nurses came in to pay their respects. Kaumudakī had been so much loved by everyone there. There wasn’t ever a time when someone did something for her that she didn’t say thank you. Her medical care, pain medication, changing of bandages—she would always thank the nurses. At one point there was a chaplain who would come in and pray with us, and after leaving one day, he commented to one of the nurses, “I don’t usually leave a patient’s room feeling like I’m the one that’s been comforted, but I feel comforted. There’s something very special going on there.”

So, we sang for her, and then her body was taken away. The following Wednesday morning, in Vancouver, I gave a class recounting the story of Kaumudakī’s passing. The entire morning program was dedicated to her, with a *śrāddha* ceremony outside in the *tulasī* garden.

Five people were allowed to go into the crematorium. I put Kaumudakī’s *japa* beads and some sacred flowers and articles into a small bag and placed it on her chest. Her cousin, the closest male relative, ignited the fire.

Outside, there was a food offering with bananas and rice-balls and a pot with sacred waters and *tulasī*, tied around with a red cloth. Vāsudeva dāsa, a friend of Kaumudakī’s, put the pot on his head and tipped it back so that it fell to the ground and smashed, with a loud sound, like a cannon. Then he walked away without looking back. The significance is that the pot— the vehicle that was Kaumudakī’s body—had been broken, her attachments finished. In case her soul was not satisfied, or needed appeasement, there was some food and sacred water to take before its departure. Not looking back is also significant: Everything is finished now. We go forward; we don’t look back.

Following the cremation, at the temple, there was the feast that Kaumudakī had paid for with her final check. She had directed us to put the sponsorship in the name of her family—the Anderson family—so that they could benefit from the service. Her cousin was honored to accept Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *prasāda* garland.

It is a very amazing experience to be with a devotee when he or she leaves. What Śrīla Prabhupāda has given us is so amazing. As much as you give, to the point of exhaustion, Śrīla Prabhupāda always gives you more. I never felt alone when I was with Kaumudakī. I always felt that the devotees were there— just like when Kaumudakī said, “Why are all these devotees in the room? Why have they come?”—and I always felt that Śrīla Prabhupāda was present.

## A Promise Fulfilled: Caring for Nirguṇa Dāsa

A talk by Janmāṣṭamī dāsa at the opening of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan on August 27, 2010.

Nirguṇa and I were *saṅkīrtana* partners for eighteen years and we went through a lot of changes in life together. Like all of us, he had his strengths and weaknesses, but he was always dedicated to Śrīla Prabhupāda and his movement. There wasn’t a second in Nirguṇa’s day—and I spent many days with him—when he wasn’t trying to push things along, increase his own service, and help other devotees, serve other devotees.

And I’ve heard from numerous senior devotees—Tamal Krishna Maharaja wrote it in his diary, and Hari-śauri confirmed it—that Prabhupāda said that if someone dedicates his life to pushing on this ISKCON movement, at the end Lord Chaitanya will personally come and take him back to Godhead.

A couple of months after Gaura-pūrṇimā in 2007 I got an e-mail informing me that Nirguṇa was very sick and had gone to Bhaktivedanta Hospital for diagnosis and treatment. When I spoke with him, Nirguṇa told me that he had been diagnosed with cancer. And he had a sense that this was the end. I encouraged him to come to Mayapur and deal with it naturally. We had established a relationship with a homeopathic doctor who was known throughout India for treating terminal cancer patients and turning them around.

At first, Nirguṇa was determined to go through with some chemotherapy, but after two sessions he got so sick that he just couldn’t handle it, so he decided to come. I had to travel out of India at that time, so we met in Kolkata, and I arranged for a friend, Gaura Kṛṣṇa dāsa, Radhanath Swami’s disciple from England, to take over Nirguṇa’s care until I returned. They went back and forth from Mayapur to the National Institute of Homeopathy in Kolkata.

This went on for two and a half months, but then Nirguṇa’s condition got worse. He had a huge tumor in his chest, and the cancer ate into his spinal column, paralyzing him from the waist down and also causing extreme pain. So we mobilized our forces in Mayapur and discovered that by vigorously massaging Nirguṇa’s legs and feet we could relieve much of the pain. We established a team of about twenty-five devotees, and we massaged his legs and feet 24/7, even while he slept, because if we stopped when he was sleeping, he would wake up in more pain and it would take him two or three hours to recover. He was determined not to be heavily sedated, because he wanted to remain as conscious and Krishna conscious as possible toward the end.

After a while, however, the constant, vigorous massaging was too much for us to maintain. Dr. Anantasiṁha came and spent about two months with us, but when I asked Radhanath Swami if he could send some *brahmacārīs* to help, he said, “Actually, Janmāṣṭamī, the fact is that it would be very, very good for the Mayapur community if they handled it without outside intervention. You should try and get the devotees in Mayapur to get involved.” It was right at the beginning of the busiest time for the Mayapur Institute, but he said, “You should drop everything and dedicate yourself completely to serving Nirguṇa.”

So that’s all I did, and it was the most intense, but also the most blissful, marathon I’ve ever been on. First he was in our house, and then we moved him into one of the Mayapur Institute classrooms and we set up a whole community of spiritual caregivers—Paṅkajāṅghri, Jananivāsa, Hari-śauri, Padma-locana—who would read to Nirguṇa and speak with him. There was a whole team caring for his body with the massage. And the young devotees would lead *kīrtanas*. It became more and more and more ecstatic. Everyone just felt so uplifted by the experience.

In the West people want to hide death and dying. When someone is about to leave his body, we don’t want to deal with it; we put him in a nursing home. Then we go to the funeral and we see the body with nice makeup, looking like the person we used to know. There’s a whole cosmetic veneer.

So, initially, there was a bit of hesitation. But it was amazing how much everyone connected with Nirguṇa once we got started—and how much Nirguṇa was uplifted by the experience.

What amazed me the most was how much spiritual progress Nirguṇa made in the last six weeks of his life. He revealed to Mādhurya-līlā that he’d had a vision of his role in the spiritual world. He wasn’t quite sure of it, but he thought he was a *mañjarī*. One day, Hari-śauri was reading to Nirguṇa unpublished sections of his diary, and Nirguṇa said that he was waiting for an invitation to “go back.” He wasn’t quite clear, but he told Hari-śauri that he had experienced some kind of dream or vision, and for the next several weeks they discussed this. It became clear to Hari-śauri that this was a very serious consideration for Nirguṇa.

Nirguṇa was also in the process of severing his material attachments. A couple of years before, his marriage of twenty years had failed, and his ex-wife came to visit him. So that was a challenging experience, but he passed through it, and he actually inspired her to take to Krishna consciousness again (she had stopped practicing). He had processed the pain, and that attachment was severed. He went through one thing after another— different emotional upheavals and strenuous challenges—but he persevered. And devotees were there supporting him all the way.

The first painting of Krishna that Nirguṇa had ever seen was of Him lifting Govardhana Hill. So he had a special attachment to Govardhana and Girirāja. Ramādevī happened to be worshiping a *govardhana-śilā*, so she placed the *śilā* by his bedside. You could see that there was a whole *rasa* intensifying between the *govardhana-śilā* and Nirguṇa.

In the last two weeks, he was very weak. He had always been a small man, but now his thighs were the size of my wrists. He hadn’t been able to swallow for a few weeks, so he was taking only liquids.

Then one night, I arranged for Nirguṇa to speak by phone with Jayapataka Swami. Nirguṇa had been a temple president at the Murāri Sevaka farm in Tennessee under Maharaja’s supervision, and Nirguṇa had married one of his disciples, so they had a relationship. But when I went to tell Nirguṇa that Maharaja would be calling, he pushed me away as if he was annoyed, like “Don’t bother me.” So I was puzzled. Grahila dāsa was one of the devotees who had been massaging Nirguṇa’s legs, so I went to him and asked, “What’s going on with Nirguṇa?” “I don’t know,” he said, “but I think he is about ready to leave his body.”

So then I went to Dr. Anantasiṁha and asked him to check. When he did, he said, “Yes, his pulse is negligible.” He couldn’t even read the blood pressure. So he said it was a matter of just a few hours.

So I called back Paṅkajāṅghri and Jananivāsa and Hari-śauri —they had left just a few minutes before—and we started an all-night *kīrtana*. Then Nirguṇa lapsed into unconsciousness. He was breathing maybe two or three times a minute. This continued through the night.

After *maṅgala-ārati* I made an announcement that Nirguṇa had lost consciousness, and I asked the devotees to come and join the *kīrtana*. Radhanath Swami had been coaching me through the whole experience and had told me that even if Nirguṇa was unconscious, it was very, very important that he hear the chanting of the holy name. So I made this point to the devotees. And before long, there were about eighty devotees crammed into the tiny (sixteen-by-twenty-foot) classroom where Nirguṇa lay. First Madana-mohana, from Canada, led the *kīrtana*—and then, for seven hours, Kūrma, the famous cook and Prabhupāda disciple. It was all so ecstatic.

Bhakti Viśrambha Mādhava Swami was sitting at Nirguṇa’s head, I was next to Maharaja, Hari-śauri was on the other side of the bed, and Jananivāsa and Paṅkajāṅghri were also there. Then, all of a sudden, Bhakti Viśrambha Mādhava Swami grabbed my thigh and said, “Janmāṣṭamī, look at Nirguṇa— his tongue is moving!” And sure enough, I could see that Nirguṇa’s mouth was beginning to move. He started singing along with the *kīrtana*, and then he opened his eyes and raised his arms up in the air—he had this enormous, transcendental smile on his face—and he was singing along with the *kīrtana*. Dr. Anantasiṁha was completely bewildered; Nirguṇa coming back like this defied everything that he understood of the dying process. For two or three weeks you’d had to put your ear right over his mouth to hear what he was saying. But there he was, singing in this tumultuous *kīrtana*.

Then Nirguṇa said, “Get your piece and go.” Hari-śauri asked him, “Nirguṇa, what was that?” “Get your piece and go!” Nirguṇa repeated in a loud voice. “Did someone say something, Nirguṇa?” Hari-śauri asked. Nirguṇa replied, “Yes.” “Who?” “Girirāja.” “So Girirāja said, ‘Get your piece and go’?” Hari-śauri asked. And Nirguṇa said, “Yes.” “What does He mean by ‘your piece’?” Hari Sauri asked. “Girirāja,” Nirguṇa replied—the *govardhana-śilā* he had on his chest. Then Hari-śauri asked, “Did you get your invitation, Nirguṇa?” And Nirguṇa replied, “Yes” and smiled. He looked so blissful. I phoned Radhanath Swami, who was just about to leave for Mayapur, for the big *yātrā*. He spoke with Nirguṇa for maybe half an hour, and then, over the phone, with Nirguṇa listening through an earpiece, he started singing *bhajanas* to him, until Maharaja had to go to catch his plane.

Shortly thereafter, Nirguṇa lapsed again into an apparently unconscious state. The *kīrtana* kept going, and the whole atmosphere was very inspiring, just surcharged with ecstasy. Then, at 1:22 p.m., Nirguṇa passed on.

One interesting thing was that after the caretakers had unclothed and bathed the body and put on a new set of cloth and I had offered *ārati*, we couldn’t find the *govardhana-śilā*. It was nowhere to be found. It had been sitting on his chest the whole time, but now it was nowhere to be found.

Four or five days later, one of the devotee women said, “Somehow or the other I have this *śilā* in my house. Does anyone know who it belongs to?” So it reappeared, but that was a pretty amazing incident.

Nirguṇa’s passing was such a special event—not only in his life, but in the lives of many devotees, including those who connected with him in Mayapur. And there can be no question in the mind of anyone who was there at the time of his departure—Prabhupāda had fulfilled his promise: Nirguṇa went back to the spiritual world.

If I had such an opportunity again—to serve a devotee at the end of his life and help him achieve life’s ultimate goal— I would do it every day. It was the most amazing service—the most memorable experience—of my life.

I take the dust from the feet of the devotees here establishing the Bhaktivedanta Hospice. If there is anything that I can do to serve, please let me know.

His Darkness Ended with Light— His Art Ended with God

## Giriraj Swami with Anthony Adams and Kauśalya Dāsī

From an interview in Los Angeles, December 29, 2012.

A Vedic aphorism states: *tamaso mā jyotir gamaya*: “Do not stay in darkness; go to the light.” *Asato mā sad gamaya*: “Do not stay in illusion; go to the eternal reality.” *Mṛtyor mā amṛtam gama-ya*: “Do not keep taking material bodies; become immortal!” (*Brihad-Aranyaka Upanishad* [1.3.28](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bau/1.3.28))

In the last year of his life, William Jennings “Bill” Adams moved from darkness to light, from illusion to truth, and from death to immortality. How this remarkable change took place was described to me by his son, Anthony, and daughter-in-law, Christina, Kauśalya dāsī.

Bill had a PhD from Stanford University in speech communication and had spent most of his adult life teaching drama, speech, and theater and directing plays, musicals, and operas. He had been a professor at San Diego State University and director of the Old Globe Theater and the Starlight Opera, and had founded the San Diego Opera Company and the Institute for Readers Theater, which he headed for thirty years. The institute put on international workshops and, using different reader’s theater techniques, trained thousands of teachers, from elementary to university level, to help students create scripts and theater from stories of their lives and things that were meaningful to them—current events, geography, history, science—anything.

Bill and his wife, Rosemary, were together for almost sixty years, until she passed away in April 2001. As Anthony describes it, they were real soul mates. Her death was so crushing to Bill that when she was gone, he wrote poems about how his life had lost meaning, how all the familiar objects and pictures and photographs and things that seemed to matter so much were now just empty and hollow. He went through a great deal of existential despair—a very dark period.

Kauśalya and Anthony lived in Los Angeles, and Bill would visit them once or twice a month. But he kept a lot to himself and did not tell them what he was going through. A couple of times he just started crying, but he never told them why. Bill had trouble managing at home alone. Sometimes he would collapse, pass out, just from getting in and out of his car. Finally, he had to admit that he was having difficulty and needed help. In December 2003 he phoned Anthony and Kauśalya and asked if they could come and stay with him for a while. He hadn’t revealed to them that he was ill, but he had been diagnosed with congestive heart failure nine years earlier.

So Anthony and Kauśalya went down to San Diego. The cardiologist informed them that Bill had one year to live. They were shocked. They decided to sell their house and move down and spend his last year with him.

“It was an amazing year,” Kauśalya told me. “When we first went down, he was very, very depressed and morose about everything—very sad. All of his poems—he was a writer, a poet— reflected this melancholy, this nihilistic worldview.”

Soon after they moved, Anthony started working for Taylor Guitars, and Kauśalya had a lot of time alone with Bill. She took him to the doctor and cooked for him, and they would talk about life. Bill’s position was clear—“When it’s over, it’s over,” he told her. “There’s nothing more to it. If it makes you feel better to talk about an afterlife, that’s fine with me, but it’s meaningless.” He thought that death was the end—door closed; it’s over.”

Kauśalya used to counter that there was more to life, that there was an afterlife and that the soul was eternal, but he never wanted to accept that. Then, however, as she related, “with my being with him and my feelings of positivity and conviction that the door doesn’t slam shut when you leave this world, he started becoming more and more positive and more and more cheerful. In his closet we found a used copy of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *Kṛṣṇa* book. He was curious—very intellectual, and brilliant—and decided to find out what I was interested in.”

“With Kauśalya remodeling the house and cooking him great *prasāda* and speaking with him,” Anthony added, “and our always playing music and inviting friends over, he gradually regained his interest in life, and over the year he came to see that there was more to life than what met the eye. He became aware of the eternal.

“After seeing how my mother died—how the hospital can get a hold of you and prolong your life with unnecessary surgeries and then you finally go out on tubes—he determined that he wanted to die peacefully at home. So we had the hospice people come over, and we were there when they told him, ‘You have six months to live.’ His eyes filled with tears; the cardiologist hadn’t actually told him how long he had. For a few days he was depressed, and he didn’t want to see anybody but us. But as he worked through it, he wanted to see everybody. He felt liberated. There was no sadness, and he would say, ‘Everything is fine.’ He made peace with it.”

Kauśalya persistently told Bill that what she knew from the *Bhagavad-gītā* and from her own experiences was that the soul never dies and is never born.

*na jāyate mriyate vā kadācin*  
*nāyaṁ bhūtvā bhavitā vā na bhūyaḥ*  
*ajo nityaḥ śāśvato 'yaṁ purāṇo*  
*na hanyate hanyamāne śarīre*

**“For the soul there is neither birth nor death at any time. He has not come into being, does not come into being, and will not come into being. He is unborn, eternal, ever-existing, and primeval. He is not slain when the body is slain.” (*****Bg*** [**2.20**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.20)**)**

And finally, he came to realize that.

“In his last month,” Anthony told me, “he wrote a series of poems called ‘Winter Song’—the winter of his life. They started out bleak, then got more metaphysical and spiritual. Kauśalya would be making breakfast, and he could barely walk, but he would come in every morning with a new poem that he’d written and he’d read it to us at the breakfast table. A couple of days before he died, he wrote a very spiritual poem, and Kauśalya said, ‘That is really spiritual, Bill.’ And he joked, ‘That just shows you how bad off I am.’ ”

Bill’s last poem was kind of a summation. One could track his change of consciousness from the early poems to the final one, from darkness to light, from death to eternal life. He had gone from being depressed, thinking, “What was my life worth? It is all over,” to the final stage of his life, when he became joyous. He knew he was going to die, but he was joyous.

On what turned out to be the last night of Bill’s life, Muralīvadana and Kaumudakī came down from LA and spent the night. They and Kauśalya and Anthony were up with Bill until midnight, playing music and talking. Kauśalya made a wonderful meal, and Bill was happy and jubilant, and finally at midnight—everyone was laughing—he said, “That’s all the hilarity I can stand for one night” and went to bed. That’s the last thing he ever said.

KAUŚALYA DĀSĪ: In the morning when we found him and he had passed, we all chanted Hare Krishna around him. It was nice.

ANTHONY: He had probably died trying to write another poem, because we found him right by his computer, not by the bed. Every morning, he would write a new poem; the one he wrote the day before he died was entitled “The Song.” He had just seen a documentary on string theory, “The Elegant Universe,” and it had totally inspired him; he spoke of it in the poem. The documentary had opened his mind up a lot. The concepts really made sense to him, and he was able to see the concept of God as the Infinite One, and then he wasn’t afraid to die.

KAUŚALYA DĀSĪ: In that last poem, he wrote, “All matter is allowed to change, not die.” So, he came to feel that death was not the end, that everything is eternal, in an endless journey of transformation. And he could release himself to that and feel that it was all wonderful.

ANTHONY: He didn’t feel the need to pin it down, to know exactly what was going to happen. He didn’t really think any of us could know that, but he ended up feeling that he was eternal and that all life was eternal and that he was a part of it all. So it was all okay, and he could just surrender. He wasn’t clinging to this body anymore; he was exhilarated, happy to be part of the mystery of life taking him where he was supposed to go next.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Did he understand that the soul was a different quality of energy than matter?

KAUŚALYA DĀSĪ: Absolutely.

ANTHONY: He wrote in the poem about how everything in the universe—from the smallest to the largest—is united and eternal. A lot of the things that Kauśalya had said about death resonated with what he was hearing from string theory and physics, and I think he saw that this ancient philosophy was really in harmony with cutting-edge science.

KAUŚALYA DĀSĪ: I think that this is what liberated him— this sense of the eternal, that he was going to continue on, that there was more to life than just this one body’s experience. He came to understand it and know it in a deeper sense—more of a realization than an intellectual understanding. And he no longer had any fear.

ANTHONY: On that last night, Bill’s *prāṇa*—his energy, his essence—was so strong, you wouldn’t think he was about to die. He had a luminous quality and was up until midnight conversing and laughing and having fun with guests and friends.

I think he had finally accepted that it was time for him to go, that all things must pass, all forms must change, and he finally accepted it completely. And once he accepted it, he was filled with lightness of being, and joy. He wasn’t struggling or fighting anymore—he was almost giddy; he could laugh. He was joyous and childlike and playful and writing poems and taking joy from talking to everybody and hearing news.

The funeral was amazing. One day toward the end Bill came down to breakfast and said, “This is what I want to do for my funeral.” He gave us a complete list of everybody he wanted to come—there were people from all over the country—and he said, “I want you to hold it in a theater, not a church,” because the theater was his church. He spent his life in theaters. Art was sacred to him, his spiritual path.

So, he said, “Get my closest friends together and have them all read my last poems.” And so we invited them, and they all came, including one of his collaborators with whom he had written several operas. We had a grand piano on stage, his friends came up and read the poems, and his old friend and collaborator improvised music over the readings. His friend was an incredible musician and composer, so we got the most spontaneous, inspired musical settings.

KAUŚALYA DĀSĪ: It was even better than we had anticipated. It was a beautiful ceremony, and it was wonderful and inspiring to see how many people’s lives he touched in a positive way.

ANTHONY: Then we had a big vegetarian feast—Mexican, his favorite food—right outside the theater. And then we invited everybody back to the house and the celebration continued, just as he had requested.

In the program we printed for his memorial, we wrote, “Bill’s final two poems were inspired by string theory, in which cosmic and quantum forces are unified to explain the immutable laws of the universe.” And we reproduced the poems:

**Hidden Corridors**

The universe, it now appears to some,  
Has not just four but seven other doors  
For whom as yet descriptions are not fixed,  
A circumstance the scientist abhors.

So this fair earth that frames our fragile home  
And all that’s born and grows to sure demise,  
Must serve those selfsame universal laws  
However strange the shape and unknown size.

The storms we know from outside and from in,  
The terrors and the triumphs that abound,  
Are partial shadows of reality  
Faintly perceived, but never wholly found.

No wonder what I know of life is nil  
Since to myself I am a stranger still.

**The Song**

There is a music we cannot hear  
From strange vibrating strings we cannot see  
That fills the unimagined reach of space  
With soaring song of unknown harmony.

It sings the song of universal laws,  
And sends its message past the reach of stars  
That each must keep its own established place  
In patterns fixed so nothing rends or mars.

From micro speck to macro mass  
All matter is allowed to change, not die,  
And if the comet should slip from its place  
The sound corrects with nothing left awry.

What singer this with neither crown nor rod?

The Song itself that humans name as God.

For Bill, the theater was his church, and his art was his worship. And his last work of art, his last poem—and his life itself— ended with God.

In his own way, Bill exemplified what Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote in his purport to *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 1.5.22: “Human intellect is developed for advancement of learning in art, science, philosophy, physics, chemistry, psychology, economics, politics, etc. By culture of such knowledge the human society can attain perfection of life. This perfection of life culminates in the realization of the Supreme Being, Viṣṇu.”

**Afterword**

When Śrīla Prabhupāda addressed students and faculty at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT), he questioned

them, “Although you have so many departments of knowledge, where is the department that aims to find the difference between a living body and a dead body?” This most important science, the science of the soul, is not being taught.

The *Mundaka Upanishad* ([3.1.1–2](https://vedarama.com/en/books/mundaka/3.1.1)) explains,

*dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyāḥ samānaṁ vṛkṣaṁ pariṣasvajāte*  
*tayor anyaḥ pippalaṁ svādv atty anaśnann anyo ’bhicākaśīti*

**“The Lord and the living entity are compared to two birds sitting in a tree. While the illusioned living entity eats the fruits of the material world, the Lord as Supersoul and best friend witnesses these activities.”**

*samāne vṛkṣe puruṣo nimagno*  
*'nīśayā śocati muhyamānaḥ*  
*juṣṭaṁ yadā paśyaty anyam īśam*  
*asya mahimānam iti vīta-śokaḥ*

**“Although the two birds are in the same tree, the eating bird is fully engrossed with anxiety and moroseness as the enjoyer of the fruits of the tree. But if in some way or other he turns his face to his friend who is the Lord and knows His glories—at once the suffering bird becomes free from all anxieties.”**

The two birds—the *jīvātmā* and the Paramātmā—are situated in the heart.

If the science of the soul—the individual soul in relation to the Supreme Soul (technically called *yoga*)—were introduced in schools and colleges, the whole bogey of death—the ignorance of the living being’s eternal identity—could be dispelled. Everyone, from children to the aged, would be better prepared to approach death with knowledge, in light, rather than shrouded in darkness, with fear, anxiety, anger, and depression.

Bill was open to learning more—about himself, about the universe, and about God. And he came to sense the spirit that animates matter. In one of his last poems, after describing objects of the phenomenal world, he wrote,

In their place the scarce-known inner-eye Begins to fathom quite another part.

The secret core that gives to life its shape, The loving, not the pumping, of the heart.

And in another poem: “Now that the lifelong secret is revealed . . .

And all the old assumptions fall away . . . Shock turns to full acceptance of the fact/ Your death is just an ordinary act.”

Thus Bill moved from darkness to light, from illusion toward eternal reality. “Embodied beings . . . are bewildered because of the ignorance which covers their real knowledge. When, however, one is enlightened with the knowledge by which nescience is destroyed, then his knowledge reveals everything, as the sun lights up everything in the daytime.” (*Bg* [5.15–16](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/5.15))

## Returning Home: Caring for My Mother

Keśava Bhāratī dāsa Goswami

In the fall of 1995 I returned to my hometown of Oroville, California, to visit my seventy-nine–year–old mother, who was lying in a hospital bed on the verge of death. I had met Śrīla Prabhupāda and joined the devotees in 1972 and had been back home only once since then, twelve years later, just before my father passed away.

My mother had always been in good health, but I’d received a call informing me that two friends had found her at home unconscious and had brought her to the hospital. When I’d called from Bombay, where I was living, she had needed a minute before she could remember me, but her memory had seemed to return with the sound of my voice, and I had promised to be with her as soon as possible.

I arrived ten days later. From her hospital bed, my mother embraced me with tears in her eyes, the first I’d ever seen. I was her only child, but our relationship had been distant since I’d joined the devotees. As a devout Methodist, she’d believed that I’d sold my soul to Satan. “Please forgive me for being so closed-minded,” she said. I could hardly believe my ears. I begged her forgiveness for not being with her sooner.

During the two days I spent with my mother in the hospital, her condition improved dramatically, and the staff decided she would do better at home with me. There were no signs of stroke or heart attack, and the doctors concluded that she had fainted from malnutrition and dehydration and that she was in the beginning stages of Alzheimer’s. So she returned home, and for the next two weeks I took care of the household chores and visited with her while a home-care provider from the hospital helped her get back on her feet.

While I was there, my mother and I had long talks about time, old age, and God. Her Christian faith seemed strong, and when she was lucid we talked of spiritual issues. She got up every morning, even when she was in pain, and read the Bible with the help of a study guide from her church. She had always been an avid reader, but she mostly read magazines and novels. I tried to convince her to read devotional books, explaining that her circumstance was an opportunity for her to turn inward and to develop her relationship with God.

My mother had always been quite conservative in her beliefs, and she and I had long ago agreed to disagree about spiritual matters, but as we spoke, she admitted that she was feeling more favorable to what I was doing. She had even put me back into her living trust, although she had hesitated, not wanting to contribute anything, even indirectly, to ISKCON or the devotees. There was no question of my trying to convince her about Krishna consciousness, but at least we were becoming closer again.

Once she was feeling better and on her feet again, my mother insisted that I return to India to continue my life’s course, which she didn’t want to interrupt. As I was to realize more clearly over the next two years, one of my mother’s prominent characteristics was her intense aversion to giving anyone trouble or taking service from anyone, including her only son. She had grown to like living alone, and although she was shaken by the episode, her determination to stay independent was unshakeable.

Reluctantly I returned to Bombay, carrying my mother’s promise that I could come soon for another visit. But I now found myself in a dilemma. For more than twenty years my life had been dedicated to the mission of my spiritual master. I was in a renounced position, wearing saffron cloth, living in India as a monk detached from family responsibilities. Plus, I had administrative and teaching duties in one of our society’s largest temples, a service that demanded full dedication. But my mother, despite her protestation to the contrary, needed me.

After praying to Śrīla Prabhupāda, consulting my senior godbrothers, and thinking deeply about the situation, I concluded that I had to do whatever was needed to help my mother. Not only was it my God-given, scriptural duty to assist her, and not only was I probably the only one who could do it, but the performance of this duty would help purify my heart. Later I would realize the potency created by taking the trouble to fully care for someone.

Over the next couple of months, I phoned my mother regularly from Bombay, and during those conversations it became clear that she was in an intense state of denial. She didn’t want to admit to her decline, because she feared that she would lose her independence. In her anxiety, considering me a possible threat, she began to subtly discourage me from coming by repeatedly reminding me how much she was enjoying being alone.

Still, I had decided to go, and fortunately, a letter my mother had written explicitly asking me not to come arrived in Bombay only after I’d boarded my flight back to Oroville. When I arrived, there she was, sitting at her place on the couch, surrounded by stacks of opened and unopened mail, looking helpless. Her credit card had expired, and she had received notices for unpaid utility bills. Normally an extraordinarily organized and tidy person, she tried to explain the situation away by insisting that she had thought I was coming the next day and was sorting things that needed to be thrown away. But when I found the refrigerator empty, she could no longer hide that she needed help.

I began to organize my mother’s affairs—arranging automatic payments and establishing relationships with her banker, lawyer, doctor, and local merchants. They were of great help. Jackie Pogue, my mother’s banker, for instance, helped reacquaint me with a financial system I hadn’t used in almost twenty years. She even visited my mother at home to make sure her accounts were in order. I cooked for my mother and arranged for meals to be delivered when I wasn’t present. In between expressions of self-pity and anger, she regularly broke down in tears of love in appreciation for what I was doing.

On this visit, I had brought my worshipable Deity, Giri-Govardhana. My daily morning worship was quite elaborate compared with any religious service my mother had seen, and at first she was suspicious and skeptical. She thought the paraphernalia was material and that you couldn’t worship God with material things. I explained that everything belongs to God and should be offered to Him with love and devotion.

After a few days, my mother’s tone changed slightly. “You certainly go to a lot of trouble to do that every morning, don’t you?” she said. “Well,” I replied, “I’m serving God; don’t you think I should take some trouble?” Shortly later, while passing by, she asked, “Need any help with the dishes?” Her devotional service had begun.

During the next year and a half I made four more visits to Oroville. My presence was creating somewhat of a sensation around town, and several of the professionals with whom I dealt were impressed that I was coming all the way from Bombay to help my mother. Jean Mather, a childhood classmate of mine since kindergarten who had also been a student of my mother’s, even began to take interest in Krishna consciousness. She appreciated my answers to her questions and arranged programs in her home and lectures in a comparative religion class at a local college.

I didn’t preach to anyone. I just tried to be as friendly, conscientious, and caring as I could, but in my normal attire as a devotee of Krishna. And the local people responded. As they became accustomed to seeing me doing everyday chores, whatever resistance the conservative residents of that small northern-California town had to an orange-clad, shaven-headed Hare Krishna monk with clay stripes on his forehead melted away. I found myself explaining Krishna consciousness wherever I went, and a group of interested people began meeting weekly to hear more about Vaishnava philosophy and practice.

I promised my mother that whatever adjustments to her life we were making together were simply meant to keep her at home. She had saved enough for professional care, but she was adamant about not going into a nursing home. Neither did she want to become bedridden. But with each visit, I watched her slowly deteriorate. And yet at the same time I also witnessed a powerful effort on her part to stay active and regulated. As she began to feel secure as she saw for herself that I was simply trying to help her execute her own will, our relationship flourished, and I learned to appreciate more deeply the value of her association, especially in my formative years, when she had instilled in me values that later enabled me to take spiritual life seriously.

Whenever I was back in Bombay, I kept in touch with Jean to monitor my mother’s situation. In April of 1997, she e-mailed to tell me that my mother had been bleeding internally but had been afraid of going to the doctor, thinking he would put her into the hospital. So Jean had taken her to her doctor, and the preliminary report wasn’t good. There appeared to be a growth in my mother’s uterus, but the condition of the mass had to be analyzed.

I decided to be with her when she went back to the doctor. The day after I arrived, we received the results of her tests: she had a large malignant tumor. She was anemic from loss of blood, and she was weak and underweight—the tumor had been taking most of her energy. Still, she refused to submit to any treatment; she wanted nature to take its course. And even if she had requested treatment, the doctor admitted that he would not in good conscience have been able to prescribe the necessary treatment unless her anemia could be corrected and she gained some weight and strength; otherwise, any treatment could be fatal. She was terminally ill, with probably no more than a year to live.

The next day my mother couldn’t remember having had the appointment, what to speak of the diagnosis. For more than a year our conversations had prepared us for this inevitability, but it was coming much sooner than expected. After a few days of unconscious denial and deep philosophical and emotional exchanges, she finally understood and accepted that she was terminally ill. She was simple in her approach to matters of life and death—not especially philosophical—and being completely honest, she could grasp the truth when it was presented straightforwardly.

After consulting with Tamal Krishna Goswami and Śivarāma Swami, my two best friends, with whom I had been keeping in touch almost daily, I decided to stay on to take care of my mother until the end. She was now eligible for hospice care, which provided essential backup services. And so Krishna began making arrangements for my dear mother beyond either of our expectations.

While talking, my mother stayed alert and lucid, apparently in good spirits. But as soon as we stopped, she either fell asleep or became overwhelmed by lamentation. I tried again to convince her to read her own spiritual books, but she wouldn’t. Finally, she confessed a crisis of faith. She didn’t know what she was supposed to be doing. She had become overly dependent on her daily routine. Forced to abandon it, she couldn’t focus her mind and had doubts about God’s existence. Her pastor had visited her once in the hospital, but since then he’d been busy preparing to transfer to another church. She felt as if she were living in a spiritual vacuum.

Then I decided to try reading to my mother from the *Kṛṣṇa* book, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s summary study of the Tenth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, which describes the pastimes of Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

Her first reaction was, “What are you reading to me, a fairy tale or a nursery rhyme?”

I explained that these were the pastimes of God, descriptions of what was happening in His kingdom, and that her only duty now was to hear about these things, the only subject matter powerful enough to absorb her mind. She sat back and listened. I began reading for a half hour daily; then I gradually increased to three hours. It was difficult to keep her attention, so I read loudly and forcibly, with as much dramatic flair as I could muster.

One day, about thirteen chapters in, I looked up from the book to see my mother practically on the edge of her seat, rapt, eyes wide like saucers. “Mom, it looks like you’re enjoying this story,” I said. “Oh yes,” she replied. “It’s wonderful! I’ve never experienced anything quite like this. And you’re reading it so nicely.” “Maybe we should go back to the beginning of the book,” I suggested. “Oh yes, please do,” she implored. “I have so many questions.”

Hearing Krishna’s pastimes from Śrīla Prabhupāda had practically lifted my mother out of her body. I got out my laptop and went to the beginning of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, reading from the Preface, pausing when she asked questions. I carefully explained whatever I had learned from Śrīla Prabhupāda about the science of Krishna consciousness. This went on for hours each day, until May 30, when the crisis arrived.

The growth began intruding on my mother’s bladder, causing complications I wasn’t trained to handle. Despite my promise that I wouldn’t take her to the hospital, she allowed me to call an ambulance. I stayed up with her that night in the emergency room and the next day in the hospital. The emergency team and her family doctor concluded that not much could be done except to keep her as comfortable as possible. They agreed to allow her to go home. While the hospital staff arranged for her discharge, I ordered a hospital bed and the necessary paraphernalia to care for her. I also bought a CD player, thinking that I should introduce her to devotional songs chanted by Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Back in her house, the thing my mother had dreaded most happened. She was bedridden. But she was thankful to be in her own home and to have me as her caretaker. We took up where we had left off in our reading, and her questions became more personal. The loving feelings exchanged between us during her last few days are difficult to describe. They are rare in this world and testify to the power of Krishna consciousness and the association of Krishna’s devotees.

A few evenings later, a name—Nandarāṇī—popped into my mind while I was chanting. Then it dawned on me: my mother had become a devotee. “This is amazing,” I thought. Before retiring that night, I looked at her and said, “Hare Krishna, Mom.” She responded without hesitation, saying for the first time in her life, “Hare Krishna!”

The next day, I received an e-mail from Śivarāma Swami, with whom I had been keeping in touch. He assured me I was doing the best thing by staying by my mother’s side. Krishna had lightened my responsibilities in Bombay so that I could do this. “This will mend your relationship with your mother,” he wrote, “and help you in your emotional and hence spiritual life. I’m a hundred percent clear that you should continue what you are doing. . . . I hope that when I pass away I will have the kind of loving care she has.”

He then asked me to give the following message to my mother:

Dear Mrs. Beck, Hare Krishna.

You don’t know me, but I have heard a lot about you and know you indirectly. Your son, Keśava Bhāratī, is one of my closest friends, and we’ve been acquaintances for the last twenty years. So I guess you and I are also related in that way. I have heard about your terminal situation and am praying for you. I think Krishna has really looked after you, due to some great fortune of yours. You have such a loving son, who is also an elevated Vaishnava, to care for you and give you spiritual guidance in the last moments of your life. This type of opportunity is rare and unique. You are very, very lucky.

Since you really have nothing to do at this point other than think about Krishna, try to focus your mind on His name, form, activities—whatever attracts you the most. By your son’s association and such effort, no doubt you will achieve a most glorious destination soon. I am a bit far away, here in Budapest, but I think we are close spiritually. I will pray for your success at this momentous hour and intently follow your progress closer to Krishna.

If I can be of any other help, please let me know. Your well-wisher,

Śivarāma Swami

Then Śivarāma Swami suggested to me that my mother be initiated. “She should get every spiritual boost she can,” he wrote. “If she takes it seriously, I will give her a name. What do you think?”

I wrote back, telling him that the name Nandarāṇī had come into my mind the night before. “I’m convinced that Krishna arranged that we both simultaneously thought the same thing,” I wrote. “When I read your letter to her, she was amazed at how, having never met her, you could write such a personal letter. I explained to her that the meaning of the conversations we’ve been having are coming to life now and that it was natural for her to come in contact with a bona fide spiritual master. When I told her that you and I were both thinking about her connecting with a spiritual master for gaining support and strength at this crucial time, in tears she immediately accepted, saying, ‘Yes, this is just what I need and want. Please arrange it.’ ”

I asked Śivarāma Swami to send a letter accepting her as his disciple. I told him that she knew exactly what she was doing and that I would help her with the chanting.

In Śivarāma Swami’s letter to my mother accepting her as his disciple, he wrote, “I am always amazed at how Krishna works—so mysteriously. . . . Śrīla Prabhupāda said that a good child indicates that the parents must be good. You have such a good son, so you must also be a good person. Now that you are chanting Hare Krishna, that makes you perfect. Therefore, why should I not accept you and let our eternal relationship begin?”

After explaining the meaning of her new name, Śivarāma Swami continued:

We are servants and eternal family members of the Lord. You should try to understand that we have no other identification.

The world we live in is temporary. Our eternal, original home is the spiritual world. From there Lord Krishna descends to attract us lost souls back to His service. In that place, all speaking is song, all walking a dance, and all beings live eternally in full happiness. There everyone lives in harmony, accepting their position as a servant of the Lord. . . .

Try to absorb yourself in thoughts of Krishna. That is possible by always chanting God’s holy names: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. As much as possible chant aloud or in your mind to yourself. If you do so carefully, you will understand that Krishna is not different from His name, and by this chanting He will transport you back to Godhead. Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “This is the test. If one chants Krishna’s name at the time of death, he is guaranteed to go back to Him.” So try to concentrate on this chanting until the last breath. Krishna has sent Keśava Bhāratī there to help remind you when the time comes to chant. Remember to follow his direction when he instructs you.

Keśava Bhāratī is not just your son. He is your guru. He has saved you at the last minute and given you the greatest treasure, Krishna. He is a fitting son, for he has repaid his debt to you. Now you are indebted to him. That debt you can repay by chanting always. I am your initiator and he is your instructor guru. So now we are together. May you receive the full blessings of Lord Krishna. I feel happy that we have met like this, and I leave you in the capable hands of my good friend, your son and well-wisher.

The day of her initiation, my mother had another crisis. Nurses from the hospice twice had to help her eliminate, because the tumor was intruding. She lost a lot of energy, so when I read her Śivarāma Swami’s letter, it was difficult for her to reply. But she managed to say that she was eternally indebted to him and was trying to do the right thing. While in agony, she called out “Hare Krishna” again and again. When she woke up in the evening, after resting for some time, she could barely speak, but she asked me who was God. I replied confidently that Krishna is God. Shaking her head affirmatively, she repeated again and again, “Krishna is God. Krishna is God.”

The next day, I read Śivarāma Swami’s letter to my mother again to see if she remembered what had been happening. Before reading it, I said I wasn’t sure if she would remember what had happened the day before. She quickly replied that of course she remembered.

As I read the letter again, I became overwhelmed by emotion and began weeping like a baby. She took my hand and looked at me in a very knowing, loving way. When I finally made it through the letter, she had tears in her eyes as she repeated her new name a few times. Then she said over and over, “This is so nice.” She told me to tell Śivarāma Swami that she is happy to have met him.

Then I taught her the *mahā-mantra*, and she began chanting as much as possible. I could see that Śrīla Prabhupāda was pouring mercy on her, giving her, at the end, a well-deserved peace. She said that she wanted to be only with me, that I should politely tell everyone she wasn’t receiving visitors.

Thereafter, she asked me to play Śrīla Prabhupāda’s tapes constantly, and sometimes I chanted out loud to her. I had told her that hearing is the same as chanting, and Śrīla Prabhupāda’s voice—the *mahā-mantra*—was all she wanted to hear.

The hospice people commented on how strong the spiritual atmosphere was surrounding us. They were professionals who dealt with family members and death every day, but without exception they testified that they had never experienced anything like this.

My mother became sweet and charming company. I moved the weekly program to our home, and after hearing me lead *kīrtana*, lecture, and answer questions for the first time in a public program, she made me promise that I would “go on and on preaching Krishna consciousness.” I was shocked at her transformation.

A few days later, my mother withdrew further, even from me. At first she showed symptoms that made us all think she would pass away peacefully during the night. But the next morning she seemed disturbed and in severe pain. She spoke, but her words indicated she was bewildered and disoriented. The hospice nurses told me those were symptoms of a person close to death.

My mother began calling out Krishna’s name over and over when she was in pain. This offenseless chanting was paving her way back to Godhead.

I wrote to Śivarāma Swami:

It’s amazing how powerful the material energy is. The hospice people say that the cancer has entered the bones and therefore managing her pain is increasingly difficult. She seems peaceful and free from pain for hours, but if you slightly move her she’s immediately in crisis pain. I may be forced to slightly sedate her to make her comfortable. Her anxiety is making it very difficult for her to hear as she had been hearing just before she accepted her initiation.

Nandarāṇī doesn’t have the years of practice we’ve had. Her newfound feelings and commitment to Krishna consciousness, while real and meaningful, are undeveloped and therefore hard to maintain under the extreme circumstances she’s in. The only thing she responds to is constant preaching and the sound of Śrīla Prabhupāda chanting. Let’s see how she responds when the pain is under control again. All the descriptions in the scriptures about the time of death are coming alive.

Please pray that her memory doesn’t fail and that she can keep remembering to the end.

By June 10 my mother had withdrawn almost completely. I played Śrīla Prabhupāda’s tapes to her constantly and read aloud descriptions of the spiritual world, trying to convince her to let go of the miserable material body and give herself to Krishna. “All right, Nandarāṇī,” I kept saying, “the runway is cleared for takeoff. You’ve taxied enough. Push down on the throttle and just go back to Krishna.”

She would sometimes move her mouth to indicate that she wanted Śrīla Prabhupāda’s chanting to continue. I knew that everything was in Krishna’s hands, but I kept trying to absorb her in transcendental sound until her last breath. I read Śivarāma Swami’s letter to her numerous times, and that helped her regain her focus while she was conscious. Several times, she cried out Krishna’s name. I was convinced that she had become eligible for a much better destination.

On July 11, sometime between 1:30 and 2:30 a.m., Srimati Nandarāṇī devī dāsī, my mother, peacefully departed. Emotionally and physically exhausted, I had dozed off, and, true to character, she was alone. Śrīla Prabhupāda was chanting the *mahā-mantra*, I had just put water from Rādhā-kuṇḍa, the most sacred of all holy lakes, in her mouth, and she was decorated with Vaishnava *tilaka* and *tulasī* neck beads.

I wrote to Śivarāma Swami, “I know that she has your full mercy and the mercy of so many other great Vaishnavas and, as a result, has attained either the spiritual world or an exalted position before returning to Vraja—the eternal abode of Krishna.” My mother had told me she didn’t want a memorial service, but when her friends insisted, I agreed for their sake. It was held at her church, and about fifty people attended. The first words the pastor spoke about my mother were that she was a saint. I was surprised. I had been thinking to start my talk in a similar way, but I was concerned that it might be too much for such a conservative crowd.

The pastor had asked me to speak after the service, and in my talk I listed the divine qualities in the *Bhagavad-gītā* and gave examples of my mother’s behavior and character to confirm that she was indeed a saintly person. Then I described how our relationship had come to a deep level of communion from an extremely estranged condition, how we had called on the names of God together, achieving a rare state of oneness I likened to a miracle. We had realized together that there was only one God. I ended by saying that to me our relationship symbolized a simple solution for a world in dire need of oneness amid cultural differences.

After the service, whatever doubt I’d had about having spoken too intimately was removed by my mother’s friends, who all expressed how much they had enjoyed the message.

There was no funeral. My son, Rāma, and I went alone to the gravesite and chanted together: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare / Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare.

## My Father and Mother—Realizing Their Good Fortune

Giriraj Swami

When I was a member of ISKCON’s Governing Body Commission (GBC) in the 1990s, its meetings were long, arduous affairs. The group would meet once a year, in Mayapur, and in two weeks we had to deal with all the issues facing the society. The plenary sessions went from 9:00 a.m. to 12:30 p.m. and then from 3:00 to 6:00. And other than a minimal time for lunch, practically every other available moment was filled with other meetings—smaller groups of GBC members dealing with various issues.

It became accepted within my circle of close friends that if we wanted to finish our required daily quota of chanting sixteen rounds of the Hare Krishna *mahā-mantra*, we would have to do it before the meetings began at 9:00. Otherwise, there wouldn’t be enough time during the rest of the day, and even if we could find some, we would be so exhausted and absorbed in thoughts of the meetings, we would hardly be able to chant with focus.

My favorite place for chanting was Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *samādhi*.
