Every morning after *maṅgala-ārati* and *tulasī-pūjā* I would walk from the temple to the *samādhi* to chant, and it was my resolution to complete my rounds before leaving the *samādhi*—even though it meant missing some mandatory parts of the temple program, specifically the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class. I relished chanting in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *samādhi* and attending the *guru-pūjā* there.

One morning as I was chanting my rounds in the *samādhi*, something inside me began pushing me to go to the temple for class. I resisted, thinking that if I didn’t finish my rounds then, I probably wouldn’t be able to do so later in the day. And Śrīla Prabhupāda had written, “Of all the regulative principles, the spiritual master’s order to chant at least sixteen rounds is most essential.” (*Cc Madhya* 22.113 purport) So I thought that my mind was just trying to drag me away from my first priority of completing my rounds. But no matter how hard I tried to push the prompting aside, it kept coming back; I felt compelled to go to class. Finally, after fifteen minutes of internal struggle, I surrendered.

Walking back to the temple, I considered that Krishna in the heart must have wanted me to hear a talk by one of the most senior and dynamic leaders in the movement, a sannyasi known for his brilliant classes—classes that I very much appreciated and under other circumstances would have been most eager to hear. But when I entered the temple room, I was surprised: The speaker was a householder devotee who was not a particularly prominent member of the GBC, and not very well known to me. I was a bit taken aback.

Still, I sat down, near the doorway, to hear what he had to say. He was recounting a story about a powerful brahman who, despite his many good qualities, had difficulty with anger. Once, while the brahman was sitting beneath a tree in the forest, a she-crane perched on a branch above him and passed stool on his head. He glared at the bird with red-hot eyes, and the crane fell down dead. Later, the brahman went to a nearby village to beg alms door to door and a housewife asked him to wait while she went inside. Meanwhile, her husband came, tired and hungry, and she attended to him. Then she remembered the brahman and went to give him alms, but the brahman was furious and began to berate her. She responded, “I am no she-crane. And your anger cannot burn me. You better give it up. One is really a brahman when he gives up lust and anger and thus is forgiving.” And she gave him an address and the name of a person whom the brahman should meet if he wanted to make progress.

The brahman went to the address, but when he got there, he found a butcher’s shop, so he left. Eventually, however, he did meet the butcher, and the butcher took him home. There he saw the butcher faithfully, humbly, and selflessly serve his aged parents. “My mother and father are my sole claim to virtue,” the butcher told him. “My whole life is dedicated to them; I worship them. I wash their feet, and I do whatever I can to please them.”

Although I found the story intriguing, I thought, “What kind of *Bhāgavatam* class is this? *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* rejects material religious principles (*dharmaḥ projjhita-kaitavo ’tra*) and propounds the highest truth (*parama satya*)—pure devotional service to the Supreme Lord. This talk seems to be about subreligious principles—controlling anger and serving parents. What kind of class is this, anyway?”

After the class, I quickly went to my room to gather my things for the meetings and I was surprised to find a telegram waiting for me there, from Florida. In general, I did not get telegrams—they were used only in cases of emergency—what to speak of in a remote place like Mayapur at a time when almost all the senior devotees were already there. I opened the telegram and read: “I HAVE SUFFERED A MILD STROKE. I APPEAR TO BE OUT OF DANGER. STILL I WOULD LIKE YOU TO COME. FATHER.”

I was uncertain about what to do. When my parents had come to visit me in Calcutta, Śrīla Prabhupāda had told me, “You should spend time with your parents—it is your duty.” Still, it was compulsory for all GBC members to attend the meetings, and my father had said that he was out of danger. I consulted Bhakti Charu Swami, in whose quarters I was staying, and he advised me to go. He said that his ailing father had been waiting for him, and the day after his father got the news that Maharaja had left for France and would not be coming, he left his body. “If you do not go and your father were to die,” he told me, “you would never forgive yourself.”

I consulted my roommate, Radhanath Swami, who, like me, was from Chicago, and he also advised me to go. And I asked him some practical questions, because I was living and dressing as a strict sannyasi. He told me that when he had gone to stay with his parents, his father had approached him with tears in his eyes and said, “Son, I love you dearly, and I appreciate all that you are doing. But the neighbors, and my friends—they just won’t understand your dress.” Radhanath Swami told me, “I felt I had caused my parents so much pain by the way I joined the movement, the least I could do now would be to satisfy them and dress as they wished.” “What about eating?” I asked. “My parents and I really don’t have so much in common,” he replied, “and the one thing they feel they can do with me is take me to a restaurant. So I go with them and have a salad or something. It’s their way of spending time together.” Being a member of the philosophical subcommittee, which was dealing with some critical matters that year, I thought I must also approach the committee chairman, Ravindra Svarūpa dāsa, and he too gave me his approval. I determined to leave for the US as soon as possible.

Within a day I organized my plane ticket from Calcutta to Miami and my taxi from Mayapur to Calcutta. Before leaving, I approached the altar of Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva. Standing in prayer before the Deity, I felt that He told me, “Just as I helped Prahlāda’s father, Hiraṇyakaśipu, I will help your father.”

It was a difficult journey: three hours on rough roads from Mayapur to Calcutta, then almost twenty-four hours from Calcutta to Delhi, Delhi to New York, and New York to Miami.

In Miami I found that my father was now in the intensive care unit (ICU) of Jackson Memorial Hospital.

I joined my sister and brother-in-law outside the ICU. For the stroke, they told me, the doctors had wanted to give my father blood thinners, and as a routine, they had scanned his body to see if there was any bleeding. And in the course of that investigation, they had found that he had stomach cancer.

Only immediate family members were allowed in my father’s room, and only one at a time.

My sister, concerned that our father might die at any time, wanted me to go in immediately so that if he opened his eyes, he would see me one last time. With mixed feelings, I entered the room.

My father and mother had been born in Jewish families and identified with the Jewish heritage. From a young age, I had been deeply religious, and my spiritual quest eventually led me to Śrīla Prabhupāda and Krishna consciousness. Over time, though they couldn’t accept my leaving my Jewish faith or promising academic and professional career, my parents developed great respect for Śrīla Prabhupāda and his work. “I can say two things about your master,” my father told me. “He is a brilliant organizer, and he really knows how to deal with people.” In my father’s value system, those were very high compliments. And my mother said later that he had used Prabhupāda’s teachings in *Bhagavad-gītā As it Is* in making his decisions in the courtroom.

From early on, I knew that there was only one God— worshiped in different ways in different traditions and addressed in different ways in different languages. And Śrīla Prabhupāda said the same thing. He said that we are not concerned with people’s designations—Hindu or Christian or Muslim—but with every soul’s relationship with the Supreme Soul—God, or Krishna. He said that people can remain Hindus or Christians or Jews and that if they add Krishna consciousness, the scientific knowledge of the relationship of the individual soul with the Supreme Soul—the science of yoga explained in the

*Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*—they will become better Christians or Hindus or Jews.

With this in mind, I entered my father’s room in the ICU. There, I completely opened my heart to him, both as a son and as a devotee. I chanted Hare Krishna and sang various devotional prayers to him, especially prayers to Nṛsiṁhadeva, the protector of devotees and the remover of obstacles. Under other circumstances I would not have been able to do that, but because I was alone with him—with no other family members—and because he was not outwardly conscious (ordinarily, he was not open to Krishna consciousness), I was able to open my heart to him spiritually and say to him what I wanted and pray for him as I wanted. And I felt very, very satisfied.

After thirty-six hours, my father came out of his coma and was transferred out of the ICU. It turned out that his extreme condition was precipitated by some medication, not by the stroke or cancer per se. And after some days, and with some difficulty, we were able to fulfill his desire and bring him back to his hometown of Chicago, where he was admitted into Evanston Hospital.

There, he gradually improved, and eventually we were able to fulfill his second desire—to return home. So we achieved both immediate goals: to get him from the Miami hospital to the Evanston one, and from Evanston Hospital to his home in Glencoe.

At home he seemed stable, and with regular, frequent visits to the hospital, he seemed to be steadily improving. Under the circumstances, I was eager to return to India. That year, 1996, was the centennial of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s birth, and there was a major program planned at the Juhu temple, where I was a leader and pioneer of the project.

But it was a difficult decision. In my youth, when I first met Śrīla Prabhupāda, I had not felt much attachment for my family or community or country. In fact, even before my joining Śrīla Prabhupāda I had rejected the materialistic society of which my father was so much a part, and I was seeking new horizons, new frontiers. But now I actually wanted to be with my father. I was relishing my time with him and my mother and sister and brother-in-law and nephew and other relatives and family friends.

I considered how much I wanted to stay with my father yet at the same time was feeling called for some higher purpose. And it struck me that this was different from when I had first joined Śrīla Prabhupāda. Then, I had no particular desire to be with my family or old friends, but now I did. I wasn’t leaving in the mood of rejecting my family or society, but rather in the mood of responding to a higher calling. So I spoke with my sister, Gail, and then went in to tell my father.

He was still in bed. Only later in the morning, with assistance, would he shift to the sofa in the den. I told him that I was planning to return to Bombay. Because of the stroke and the weakness caused by the cancer, he could speak only with great difficulty, usually only a few words at a time. “Do you have to go?” he asked. “Well,” I said, “it is a very special event, and I feel I should be there. I can come back in two weeks.” And I added, “Gail is here.” “There is no substitute,” he replied.

In a way, I was glad to hear that my father had a special appreciation for me. Since I had joined ISKCON and been based in India—more than twenty-five years—my sister had been the one to spend time with our parents. Further, she was extremely intelligent and competent in practical matters, and she was expertly organizing and managing all of my father’s care. I guess I had imagined, or assumed, that she was his favorite and that he could do fine without me.

Almost all the nurses who were attending to my father were very spiritual people. They were tuned in to not just the process of healing, but also the process of death and dying, and they were tuned in to my father. When I mentioned my plan to go to India, one of the nurses, to my surprise, said, “I have seen that your father has a very special, intimate rapport with you. Although he does not express it in words, he has great regard and love for you.”

Before leaving, I brought up a spiritual topic with my father. Years earlier, when he had come to visit me in India, I had tried to explain some of the concepts in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s book *Easy* *Journey to Other Planets*, which was presented in contemporary scientific language but which included information beyond the reach of empirical modern science. But although my father considered himself to be more an agnostic than an atheist, as I had been speaking I had seen that he was becoming more and more incredulous with my every word, and I had realized that I had better end the discussion quickly.

It is a principle in Krishna consciousness that one should not “preach to the faithless.” One can engage them in service, but if one argues with them, they may become more doubtful, or even inimical. Thinking of that principle, and seeing how my father had been responding, I had decided never to enter into such a discussion, or argument, with him again. That, I had felt, would be better for all concerned, and I believe it was.

But now I wanted to take a chance. I wanted to see if my father could accept that he was not his body but was the consciousness within the body. If he could understand this one point, I felt it would make a great difference in the quality of the remainder of his life, and his departure. So I put forward two logical choices: either consciousness is a product of material combination, or it is a distinct energy—not physical and not chemical, but spiritual. As had been usual when we had engaged in such discussions in the past, he put forth many counter-arguments (he had been a champion debater at Chicago and Yale, then a lawyer, and then a judge), and I realized that even if I defeated him in the argumentation—which I felt I had—he would not change; he just was not open. But I was satisfied. I had tried my best, and I resolved never to try to convince him again.

So, I flew to Bombay, but two days later I received a phone call from my sister saying that my father had taken a drastic turn for the worse and that I should come back immediately. The airline was very accommodating and arranged a seat for me on the next flight.

Back in Glencoe, I spent hours with my father, often with him lying on the den sofa with his feet on my lap and me reading *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, especially the chapters about Prahlāda and his father and how ultimately his father was delivered by the mercy of Lord Nṛsiṁha, although his father was not at all a devotee but was in fact an atheist opposed to devotees and devotion to God; or I would chant rounds or recite prayers—all to myself. One of my favorites was

*samāśritā ye pada-pallava-plavaṁ mahat-padaṁ puṇya-yaśo murāreḥ*  
*bhavāmbudhir vatsa-padaṁ paraṁ padaṁ padaṁ padaṁ yad vipadāṁ na teṣām*

**“This material world is full of danger, and the ocean of nescience is very difficult to cross. But for those who have taken shelter of the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa, this vast ocean becomes like a puddle of water contained in a calf’s hoofprint. Although there is danger at every step, they cross right over it.” (*****SB***  [**10.14.58**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-10/14.58)**)**

With my father, I was not chanting or reading out loud, but still, I had faith that somehow he was benefitting.

While I was there, one of the nurses happened to mention a controlled, published scientific experiment. In the first group, the patients were given only treatment, and in the second they were given treatment and also had people praying for them. And in the second group, there were two subgroups: in one, the patients knew that people were praying for them, and in the other they didn’t. The results showed that the subjects who had people praying for them recovered significantly more quickly and completely than those who didn’t—even if they didn’t know that people were praying for them.

Altogether I was with my father for three months, except for two brief periods of travel, before he passed away. During that time I often thought of the *Bhāgavatam* class about serving one’s parents, how Lord Krishna knew what lay ahead and how He wanted to prepare me for it.

By the Friday before my father’s departure, my two closest cousins and their wives had arrived from Santa Barbara. On Saturday morning my father’s condition deteriorated dramatically, and for the first time he was really experiencing pain. Until then, I had wanted him to stay as long as possible. I had hoped that he would get somewhat better; and even otherwise, I had wanted him to stay as long as possible, for us to have as much time together as possible before he left. But when I saw how much pain he was in that Saturday, my mood changed; I didn’t want to see him suffer, and within my heart I was ready to let him go.

I took the train to the ISKCON temple on the North Side of Chicago, and I prayed to the Deities for him, especially to Gaura-Nitāi, and specifically to Nityananda Prabhu. It was as if the Deities were alive, and Nityananda Prabhu assured me that whatever I had prayed for in relation to my father would be fulfilled.

When I returned to Glencoe that evening, I found that my father had gone into an internal state of consciousness— apparently a coma, but actually what is called the stage of “active dying.”

The next morning, my sister became extremely upset with me and shouted at me, and I felt very hurt. I had done something that had altered the temperature and flow of air in the house, and my sister claimed that it would adversely affect our father. After the outburst, in my mind I went over and over what I had done and how she had responded. I knew that I had had a reason for doing it, I was not convinced that what I had done had caused any real harm, and I felt that my sister’s reaction was excessive. I just could not get the incident out of my mind. But then I had an insight: Our father was leaving his body, and emotions were running high. Fear, anxiety, sadness, anger. And some of that emotion had come out. No problem. I should do whatever I could to support and encourage my sister. We were all doing our best to serve our father.

There were always family members at my father’s bedside. I had many things that I wanted to say to him, and do with him, that I felt I couldn’t say or do with others present. And naturally, they all wanted to be with him. At least one or two were always there, and as the day went on, usually four or five. It was such a delicate situation—how could I ask them to leave? Then somehow, late Sunday afternoon, it happened that all but one left— one of my cousins. I went in and said, “I think I’d like to be alone with my father,” and in an understanding mood, he left, too.

One reason I wanted to be alone with my father was that there were some very personal things I wanted to say to him, and another was that I wanted to express my feelings for him within the culture of Krishna consciousness.

Alone with him at last, I placed my head under his feet, holding his feet with my hands and pressing their soles on my head. In Vedic culture, sons respect their fathers and students respect their teachers by touching their feet. And a guru shows the utmost mercy to a disciple by placing his feet on the student’s head.

I asked my father to forgive me for all the offenses I had committed against him during his life. Two in particular were vivid. I felt guilty, and I wanted to confess and ask his forgiveness.

In the top drawer of my father’s bedroom dresser was a tray in which he kept loose change—a lot of pennies and dimes and nickels, and a few quarters. Every once in a while when I was younger, I would sneak into his room and take some of the coins, always leaving enough, I thought, so that he wouldn’t notice anything missing. Somehow at that moment at the end of my father’s life, I felt extremely guilty for having stolen from him like that, so many times over many years, and I admitted what I had done and asked him to forgive me.

Another offense, which I considered very major, I had committed when I was older, perhaps in high school, or even college. He had been sitting on his easy chair in the den, reading, and as a sort of rebellious teenager I had asked something of him to which he had not agreed. Those were difficult times, and the generation gap was huge. Out of frustration, or exasperation, or anger, I had done something that I never should have: I had pushed him. It was the first, and only, time in my life that I ever did such a thing, and because the chair had been right up against the window, my father’s head had hit the window. I don’t think it hurt him so much physically, but I think that emotionally it hurt him tremendously. He had looked at me with incredulity, as if to say, “How could you have done that?” That image had remained in my consciousness. And now, at the final stage, I brought myself to apologize to him and beg for his pardon.

I believed that he could hear me. Vedic scriptures, and books on death and dying, tell us that hearing is the last sense to go. Even when other senses shut down, the sense of hearing remains.

In that time I had alone with him, I expressed my love for him. I expressed my appreciation and gratitude for all that he had done for me, and for others, and for who he was—a man of great integrity, wisdom, compassion, humor, loyalty, and care. I quietly sang Sanskrit songs and prayers for him, and I felt that he accepted everything I offered to him—my apologies and regrets, my affection and appreciation, and my heartfelt prayers for his eternal benefit, for his liberation from the repetition of birth and death and for his attainment of pure loving service to Krishna, God.

Someone knocked on the door. “Glenn, can we come in now?” “Yes.” I was satisfied. I had had the time I needed. They came in, and I went out.

It so happened that Radhanath Swami was visiting his parents in Chicago then, and he was planning to drive out to Glencoe the next day to meet me. So I phoned him and told him that my father’s departure was imminent and that we would have to wait. While we were speaking, the nurse tapped on the door and said, “He’s asking for you. It won’t be long now.” I told Maharaja what was happening, and we agreed that I would phone again later.

I rushed into my parents’ bedroom. All the family members were there—my mother, my sister, my cousins, their wives— along with the nurse. My father was on the double bed; I sat cross-legged next to him on one side and held his right hand, and my sister was in a chair on the other side, holding his left hand. And as prescribed in the Jewish tradition, we started to sing out loud, *Sh’ma Yis’ra’eil Adonai Eloheinu Adonai echad*: “Hear, O Israel: The Lord our God, the Lord is One.” It is the most sacred utterance in Judaism, the essence of the Hebrew faith, and rabbis recommend that when someone is leaving his body he and his loved ones should recite the Shema. Amid the loud, fervent singing and invocation of God and His glories, my father’s eyes opened wide, his mouth opened and then broadened into a smile, and he left.

That visage—his open eyes and broad smile—remained. A sublime, blissful atmosphere permeated the room. And my sister, who tends to be rational, even skeptical, blurted out, “I wonder what he saw that made him open his eyes so wide and smile like that.”

For some moments, everyone was too stunned to really appreciate that my father had left. Then my mother began to weep and ran out of the room. My sister nodded to me and said, “Giriraj, go and be with her.” I found her sitting on the living room sofa sobbing. I sat next to her—I didn’t really know what to say or do—and held her hand. My sister’s son, Jacob, then four and a half, came to cheer her up, saying, “Grammy, Grammy, don’t cry, don’t cry, don’t be sad.” And he took her by the hand and tried to lead her into the den. “Come, come. Let’s watch *Apollo* *13*”—his favorite movie. And so all three of us went into the den.

Around dawn I went into my parents’ bedroom. It was May, and the new leaves and buds on the branches of the trees and shrubs were visible through the room’s large picture windows. It seemed a beautiful season for renewal—rebirth and renewal.

As the family members awoke, we met in the living room and prepared notices to send to the press—obituaries and details of the funeral. Most of the notices would appear Tuesday morning, just hours before the funeral.

In the Jewish tradition, family members do not speak at the funeral. The idea is that they should be free to just mourn. Yet after my father left, I felt I had to say something. On Monday morning my sister came into my room. “You are so good at writing letters,” she said. “Why don’t you write something for Dad.” So I did. I wrote a letter to him thanking him for the sacrifices he had made for us and things he had done for us, telling him that we would try to uphold the noble values he had taught us and exemplified, asking him to forgive us for our lapses and shortcomings, and expressing my hope that he would be pleased by our efforts and help us—smile down upon us—from wherever he was. After composing it I showed it to her. “This perfectly encapsulates the whole experience,” she said, and she hugged me and we both wept.

Despite the short notice, a great number of people—hundreds of judges, lawyers, relatives, friends, and all sorts of acquaintances and admirers—gathered at North Shore Congregation Israel, a synagogue, for the service. Rabbi Herbert Bronstein led the service and spoke, and as an exception, he allowed one of my father’s best friends, Seymour Simon, an Illinois Supreme Court justice, to speak, too. Seymour began, “One thing about Al Teton—whenever anyone would hit the tennis ball to his court, he would always run to return it, even if it was far away, even if there was practically no chance he could reach it, or if he did, return it across the net into the opposite court. But he would always try his best. And that is how he approached life. Whatever the situation, he always tried his best.” Then the rabbi read my letter, and the service came to a conclusion.

At the end of the service, no one moved. There was a very special atmosphere in the spacious sanctuary. Then, gradually, people got up and started to walk out, and some accompanied my father’s body in procession to the cemetery for the burial.

In the Jewish tradition, after the funeral the family sits shivah. *Shivah* is Hebrew for “seven.” For seven days the family sits at home and in the morning or evening (or both) has religious services. Friends and relatives come, often with food, to provide comfort and company.

On the first evening, our neighbors Phil and Lydia Rash-man, both lawyers, came to visit. They commented that they had never experienced a funeral like my father’s. Usually when the service was over, people just got up and walked out. They’d never before seen everyone remain in their seats so long; it had been extraordinary. And the Rashmans, along with many others, commented on how moving and inspiring my letter to my father had been.

Soon after my father’s death, Radhanath Swami came to visit me. We discussed many topics, and I related to him one episode with my father. My father had been very sick in the hospital, and the family had felt that someone should be with him overnight, that someone should always be with him. And because my sister had her son and our mother to look after, it was logical that I should be the one. So I had slept on a sofa near my father’s bed, so I could be available for him at any time. He had been in such a condition that he couldn’t get out of bed on his own. Whenever he had needed, I had helped him to the bathroom, and when he hadn’t made it, I had cleaned up the mess. I had been exhausted—emotionally, of course, but also physically—and had wanted to get some sleep, but I was always afraid that my father might need me, and so I had remained awake, alert. And the few times I had dozed off, I’d heard some sound and thought he’d needed me, which often he had. So it had become very difficult. I had been struggling, but I’d known I had to do it.

Toward the end of the night, about dawn, my father had asked me what I would do if he gave me a million dollars. Wanting to ease his mind, I gave a general answer, but really, I wanted only to serve him. He was my father, and it was my duty. And I loved him and didn’t want him to suffer any more than he had to.

As I related the episode to Radhanath Swami, he recalled the incident of Īśvara Purī’s taking care of his spiritual master, Mādhavendra Purī. When Mādhavendra Purī was in the last stage of his life, Īśvara Purī served him in all respects, cleaning up his stool and urine with his own hand. Pleased with Īśvara Purī, Mādhavendra Purī blessed him with the great treasure of ecstatic love for Krishna. After recounting that incident, Radhanath Swami commented, “You gave everything for your father’s care, just as Īśvara Purī did for his spiritual master. And just as Mādhavendra Purī wanted to bestow upon him the greatest treasure in his possession—pure love of Godhead—so your father wanted to bestow upon you his treasure. It was a material treasure, but that was the treasure he had, and he wanted to give it to you.”

I had had the exact same thought. Naturally, I was also concerned about my father’s destination, and I discussed that question with Radhanath Swami too, saying how when I had been reading the history of Prahlāda and his father in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, some verses and purports had struck me as especially relevant to my father and his situation.

After Prahlāda’s father’s death, Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva asked Prahlāda to accept some benediction, but Prahlāda refused to accept any boon for his material benefit. He did, however, pray for his father: “O Supreme Lord, because You are so merciful to the fallen souls, I ask You for only one benediction. I know that my father, at the time of his death, had already been purified by Your glance upon him, but because of his ignorance of Your beautiful power and supremacy, he was unnecessarily angry at You, falsely thinking that You were the killer of his brother. Thus he directly blasphemed Your Lordship, the spiritual master of all living beings, and committed heavily sinful activities directed against me, Your devotee. I wish that he be excused for these sinful activities.” (*SB* 7.10.15–17) And Śrīla Prabhupāda explains in the purport, “Although Hiraṇyakaśipu was purified as soon as he came in contact with the Lord’s lap and the Lord saw him, Prahlāda Mahārāja still wanted to hear from the Lord’s own mouth that his father had been purified by the Lord’s causeless mercy. Prahlāda Mahārāja offered this prayer to the Lord for the sake of his father. As a Vaiṣṇava son, despite all the inconveniences imposed upon him by his father, he could not forget his father’s affection.”

Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva replied, “My dear Prahlāda, O most pure, O great saintly person, your father has been purified, along with twenty-one forefathers in your family. Because you were born in this family, the entire dynasty has been purified.” And in the purport Śrīla Prabhupāda explains, “The word *triḥ-saptabhiḥ* means seven multiplied by three. In one’s family one can count back four or five generations—to one’s great-grandfather or even one’s great-grandfather’s father—but since the Lord mentions twenty-one forefathers, this indicates that the benediction expands to other families also. Before the present family in which one has taken birth, one must have been born in other families. Thus when a Vaiṣṇava takes birth in a family, by the grace of the Lord he purifies not only that family but also the families of his previous births.” In a purport to another related verse, Śrīla Prabhupāda explains further, “Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura says that although Hiraṇyakaśipu was already purified, he had to take birth on a higher planetary system to become a devotee again.”

Based on these verses and purports (although I am far from the standard of Sri Prahlāda, I was accepted as a servant by a devotee of the highest caliber—Śrīla Prabhupāda—by his causeless mercy), I considered that my father would have to take birth as a devotee before he could be fully liberated and go back home, back to Godhead. Radhanath Swami replied that both are possible—he could take birth as a devotee and then go back to Godhead, or, if the Lord wished, he could go back to Godhead directly. “We can never know for sure,” he said. But then he added, “Personally, I always like to think of the person’s receiving the Lord’s maximum mercy.”

Some days later, I opened my computer and found a letter from a disciple, with a most appropriate quotation from Śrīla Prabhupāda: “The parents of my disciples will realize their good fortune at the time of their passing away.”

After my father left, my sister and I knew that one of us would have to take care of our mother—that she would have to live with one of us—and given that I was living in India, in Bombay, in the temple, it was clear that my mother would stay with my sister. So, after winding up affairs in Glencoe, we all set out by road for the Teton Mountains, Grand Teton National Park, and as planned, we reached in time to celebrate my mother’s seventy-eighth birthday, on August 9. And she was so happy that after the dinner party, cake, and birthday song, she spontaneously began singing Hare Krishna—the first, and last, time I ever witnessed that. From there I returned to Bombay, and the others continued to Santa Barbara, where earlier my brother-in-law had gone ahead to rent a house. Three years later, because of health reasons, I ended up based nearby, in Carpinteria.

In September 2002, a group of devotees and I went to Zaca Lake Retreat, an hour’s drive north, to observe my birthday and Rādhāṣṭamī. Zaca Lake is a holy place to Native Americans, and the entrepreneur who took it over wanted to retain its pristine beauty. We hiked and chanted *japa* in the hills, and performed *kīrtana* on boats on the lake. There was no cellphone reception and no telephones in the cabins—only one phone in the office and a public phone outside. On Rādhāṣṭamī, the day before the end of the retreat, which was meant to be the biggest day of activities, my brother-in-law phoned and told me that Grammy, my mother—he called her Grammy in deference to Jacob—was ill and had been taken to the hospital and that I should come immediately. It didn’t look good, but they were awaiting test results.

So I made arrangements for the continuation of the retreat in my absence and then drove to Santa Barbara. The tests confirmed it: my mother had lung cancer.

We all decided that at least one family member should always be with my mother, and as a woman, my sister was the natural choice to spend the night. But she also had to take care of Jacob, and my brother-in-law, who also took some turns, worked. Suddenly the idea flashed through my mind to invite Mother Saṅgītā, who is a registered nurse, to come and help. I phoned her, and she took the first possible flight from Philadelphia to Santa Barbara.

When the doctors had said that my mother had lung cancer, the family members—with the doctors—had concluded that she might leave us soon. But something within me just could not accept that she would. One might say that I was in denial, but there was another side to it. I just didn’t feel that it was time, and something within my heart desperately wanted her to stay. I mentioned my being “in denial” to Saṅgītā, and she tried to help me accept the reality of the situation, though later she acknowledged that denial can serve a purpose—that a person may need time to process something and that denial can allow the person the time that he or she needs. But still, I didn’t feel that it was time for my mother to go. I was not in denial of the fact that she had cancer, but I just could not accept that her time had come. And I knew from experience that sometimes Krishna intervenes at the last minute, according to His will. In further discussion with Saṅgītā, we both agreed that although devotees have a human side that deals with death and dying like other people do, they also have a spiritual side, and that just as it is a mistake to think that devotees are beyond the processes of other people, it could also be a mistake to try to reduce their thoughts and feelings to typical psychological patterns.

In any case, I refused to accept that my mother’s departure was imminent. I prayed for her to be able to remain with us, and on October 7, wrote to devotee friends who knew her, “My mother is in intense pain and serious condition in the hospital. Please pray for her.”

Then, all of a sudden, my mother’s condition improved. On October 19, I wrote, “My mother has miraculously recovered from her immediate health crisis and has left the hospital to return home—by the grace of the Lord and the devotees. Thank you for your concern and prayers.”

Still, she had terrible back pain, sometimes excruciating; it was too much for her to bear. So I requested my acupuncturist, Dr. Subhash Gharmalkar, a householder devotee, to treat her, and my mother, who would never have otherwise agreed to such an apparently painful alternative treatment, was so desperate— nothing else was working—that she agreed. So Dr. Subhash regularly drove up from Huntington Beach, about five hours round trip, and treated her, with some success.

When my mother had been in the hospital, the question had come up as to whether we should tell her that she had cancer. I was against it—completely against it—because I felt that if she knew, she would feel discouraged, her morale would suffer tremendously, and she would lose her spirit to live. It would rob her of whatever happiness she had, and she would probably die very soon.

This created a moral dilemma, or question: Was it our duty, or obligation, to inform her? In times of medical crises, the family would always come together, and during my mother’s illness we often gathered to meet and discuss at the hospital itself. In fact, my mother’s, and the entire family’s, primary care physician began to call us the “Teton Mafia.” We would discuss and decide together as a family—my sister, brother-in-law, me, two cousins, and, when practical, their wives.

I felt very strongly that we should not tell her, but it was a dilemma. The primary care physician, Dr. Jeffrey Kupperman, specialized in pulmonary diseases, critical care, and internal medicine, and he was also a family friend, so we felt comfortable asking him. His response was balanced and practical. He said that from his experience, “If a patient wants to know, the patient will ask, and if the patient doesn’t want to know, the patient won’t ask. If she doesn’t ask, you needn’t feel obliged to tell her—you can, but you needn’t feel obligated.” So far, she hadn’t asked. And my cousin John supported my position. Still, the issue remained.

I also wanted to consult Dr. Subhash. He knew my mother, he was a perceptive healer and a sincere devotee, and I had faith in his judgment. His reply was, “She already has a tendency to be depressed, and if you tell her, she’ll become very depressed, and it won’t be good for her.” He confirmed what I felt, and ultimately the family concurred. And to the end, my mother never inquired into her underlying medical condition.

For what turned out to be almost two more years, my mother led a very happy life. My sister took charge of her care in all respects. Initially, my sister, with her husband, did most of the care herself, but as my mother became weaker, my sister needed more help, and fortunately our family had the means to engage caregivers, who eventually came every day, as well as a companion to take my mother out, generally for lunch and a movie.

This last period was quite a happy time for my mother. I felt gratified that after being away from her for more than thirty years, mainly in India, I could spend some time with her and in whatever little way I could, encourage her to come closer to God.

Initially, my mother would come once a week to my place for dinner (*prasāda*) and then a video. Together we would watch something from my library. First we saw all the videos about Śrīla Prabhupāda, for whom her admiration and respect kept increasing. After those, I tried a religious movie from India, but that was too foreign to her. Then we rented videos with religious themes, beginning with *The Ten Commandments* and *Ben-Hur*. And when we’d exhausted those, we watched good, wholesome movies, especially from the forties and fifties. Two of her favorites were *The Wizard of Oz* and *The Sound of Music*. As her condition declined and she became weaker, I spent time with her at my sister’s house, first once a week, then twice a week, and toward the end, as often and for as long each time as practical.

There was no talk of death; neither my mother nor I, nor anyone else, spoke of it. Then one day when she was home in her room with her companion Kimberly, she mentioned that she was seeing a pony she had had when she was a little girl. This was the first time she said anything out of the ordinary. And it seemed to be the beginning of her accepting the transition from her present life to the next.

“This is probably the spirit’s way of easing her into the transition,” Kimberly told me. “I feel that the veil is very, very thin anyway, and when one starts moving into that transition period of getting ready to cross over and the veil gets thinner and thinner, one starts to perceive things—perhaps smell things, or hear things—and then you can start to have images in your mind of people you have loved in the past, that they are there with you. Your psychic abilities increase. I think that’s what was happening. I knew that she was having that experience and that it felt very good to her, very calming, and that the prospect of being with that pony again felt wonderful.”

Although my mother still did not speak of death, she seemed to gradually be getting ready. Her spirit was still strong, but her body was getting weaker. She was having more and more difficulty walking, and finally she had to be pushed in a wheelchair. At this stage, my sister arranged for around-the-clock professional care.

Sometimes Kimberly would come and they would sit together looking out at the view from the patio, and with beautiful music playing in the background, they would just be together, in a meditative state. “We were not talking about passing over,” Kimberly later said. “We were just enjoying where we were in that moment. I knew—and deep down she probably knew too— that our days together were numbered.”

During those last three weeks, things changed. I thought of the times my mother and father had met Śrīla Prabhupāda. I searched the Bhaktivedanta VedaBase for references to her and came across a conversation on Juhu Beach that I had forgotten:

**Giriraj:** My parents telephoned last week, and at first they couldn’t recognize my voice. They couldn’t believe that it was me.

**Prabhupāda:** Then?

**Giriraj:** Krishna consciousness has changed the . . .

**Prabhupāda:** So your father said that “I could not . . .”

**Giriraj:** Yes. He said, “Are you sure it’s you?”

**Prabhupāda:** (laughs) Just like a lawyer. . . . Mother is also very nice. Unless father and mother is nice . . . I told your mother also.

I sent e-mails out to disciples and friends, informing them of my mother’s condition and asking them to pray for her, and I gained much encouragement from their support and prayers. Saṅgītā dāsī posted a prayer for her on the Vaisnavas C.A.R.E. website. By early June, devotees all over the world were praying for my mother.

Gradually she weakened further, and eventually she spent most of her time in bed, barely able to speak. Sometimes I sat in her room and expressed myself to her in a subverbal way, speaking to her in words but without vocalizing—and I felt that she replied in the same way. I wasn’t sure whether it was real or just a projection of my own mind, telling me what I would have liked for her to say, but when I mentioned it to one of the nurses— almost all of them were quite attuned to subtle levels—she said, “Yes, it is possible. At the end of life there can be that kind of internal communication.” She said that she believed it was real.

There is another story in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*—of Kapiladeva giving spiritual instructions to his mother. Although usually the parents give instructions to their children, on occasion a child may be blessed with some spiritual knowledge that the parents don’t have and thus it is part of his duty and service to his parents to share that knowledge with them. So, in that mood, I spoke to my mother about the importance of guru, that by surrender to guru and service to guru one can achieve all success in life. I felt that she was listening to every word I was saying, taking it deeply into her heart and accepting it. And I quietly sang to her the *Guru-vandanā* prayers by Narottama dāsa Ṭhākura:

*“śrī-guru-caraṇa-padma, kevala bhakati-sadma, vandoṇ mui sāvadhāna mate*  
*yāhāra prasāde bhāi, e bhava toriyā yāi, kṛṣṇa-prāpti haya yāhā haite*

**“The lotus feet of our spiritual master are the abode of pure devotional service. I bow down to his lotus feet with great care and attention. My dear brothers! It is through the grace of our spiritual master that we can cross over this material existence and attain Kṛṣṇa.**

*“guru-mukha-padma-vākya, cittete koriyā aikya, ār nā koriho mane āśā*  
*śrī-guru-caraṇe rati, ei se uttama-gati, ye prasāde pūre sarva āśā*

**“Make the teachings emanating from the lotus mouth of our spiritual master one with your heart, and do not desire anything else. Attachment to his lotus feet is the best way to make spiritual advancement. His mercy fulfills all desires for spiritual perfection.**

*“cakhu-dān dilo yei, janme janme prabhu sei, divya-jñāna hṛde prokāśito*  
*prema-bhakti yāhā hoite, avidyā vināśa jāte, vede gāy yāhāra carito*

**“He who has given us the gift of transcendental vision is our lord, birth after birth. It is by his mercy that divine knowledge is revealed within our hearts, bestowing pure love for Kṛṣṇa and destroying ignorance. The Vedic scriptures sing of his character.**

*“śrī-guru karuṇā-sindhu, adhama janāra bandhu, lokanātha lokera jīvana*  
*hāhā prabhu koro doyā, deho more pada-chāyā, ebe yaśa ghuṣuk tribhuvana*

**“O spiritual master, O ocean of mercy and friend of the fallen! O universal teacher and life of all people! Alas, alas, O master, please be merciful unto us and give us the shade of your lotus feet. May your glories now be proclaimed throughout the three worlds.”**

I explained to her the meaning and significance of each verse, and I felt that she took it all into her heart. Then I explained the importance of chanting the holy names, and I chanted for her, first offering respects to my spiritual master:

*“nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāmin iti nāmine*

**“I offer my respectful obeisances unto His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, who is very dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa on this earth, having taken shelter at His lotus feet.**

*“namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

**“Our respectful obeisances are unto you, O spiritual master, servant of Sarasvatī Gosvāmī. You are kindly preaching the message of Lord Caitanyadeva and delivering the Western countries, which are filled with impersonalism and voidism.”**

Then I sang the holy names of the Pañca-tattva:

*śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya prabhu-nityānanda*  
*śrī-advaita gadādhara śrīvāsādi-gaura-bhakta-vṛnda*

**“I offer my obeisances to Śrī Kṛṣṇa Caitanya, Prabhu Nityānanda, Śrī Advaita, Gadādhara, Śrīvāsa, and all others in the line of devotion.”**

And then I chanted the Hare Krishna *mahā-mantra*:

*hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare*  
*hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare*

My mother opened her eyes and turned her head toward me. I quickly came close and sat on the floor by her bed. She extended her arm and stroked my cheek. “I love you, darling,” she said.

My sister, who seemed to know just what to say to our mother at this stage, had told me, “Our mother gave everything to her family; her family was her life. You should tell her how much you appreciate all that she did for the family, and what a wonderful mother she was. Tell her that we all will be fine after she leaves, that we all will be together and take care of each other, and that she has nothing to worry about. She is free to leave now. She can go peacefully to her next destination.”

Still, our mother was staying. She was giving us more precious moments of her association. And it seemed that she was waiting. The nurses told us that often terminal patients wait for something—a loved one to arrive, an issue to be resolved, an event to take place—before they depart. My brother-in-law and nephew were away on a school field trip, and she may have been waiting for them. After my sister phoned them, they returned.

My sister naturally tried to cheer up my mother in various ways, and one way was by singing old songs, some favorites from my mother’s generation.

On the last day, I felt we should have the rabbi and the cantor come. The rabbi was out of town, but the cantor came and sang the Jewish liturgy in a most beautiful and moving way. All the family members were gathered at her bedside—my sister, her husband, the cousins, their wives, the children, and me.

We could see from my mother’s slow, heavy, intermittent breathing that the end was near. The cantor had to leave for the temple for the Friday evening services. The family remained surrounding her, various members touching her lovingly, loudly singing the Shema—and she breathed her last. My cousin Andrew burst into tears. He said what a special person she was, how she always acknowledged him and was always affectionate to him, and he concluded, “Angels fly because they take themselves lightly.”

Later, Kimberly told me, “My relationship with your mother was like no relationship I ever had. For the first time I was wholly welcomed and accepted—instantly. And I felt the same towards her. There was an instant love, an instant connection, and it grew in its own way. It started off as employer-employee, then it became like grandmother-granddaughter, and then like mother and daughter; then it changed and it was like best friends, and then it switched and I was the mother and she was the daughter, and I was taking care of her, and then at the very end she became my baby. That’s how bonded and connected I was to her.

“We really went through a process together, and that was so valuable to my growth—my spiritual growth and my human growth—my ability to open up my love, to love people unconditionally right away, like I did with her. It removed that fear, and she never ever rejected me—ever. It was a relationship that was absolutely essential to my personal growth. I wouldn’t be the person I am today if I hadn’t had that relationship with her and Gail and David and you. The whole unfolding of that situation was just beautiful, absolutely beautiful.

“And I know I am going to see Roz again. I feel her. I’ve got a picture of her at home. It takes me right to her, and I know she is with me. I know she is. I just believe it, and that is how I live my life.”

That Friday, June 11, I tried repeatedly to speak with Radhanath Swami at the Brooklyn temple. He was in New York for the five-day Ratha-yātrā celebrations, and it was very hard to reach him. In the end, I just left a message for him saying that my mother’s departure was imminent and that he should pray for her—and hoped he would get it.

Later I learned that he had gone into the temple room, packed with devotees who had come for the festival—it was also Śrīvāsa Paṇḍita’s disappearance day—and had announced that Giriraj Swami’s devoted mother was leaving her body and that the *kīrtana* was being dedicated to her. And he had requested all the devotees to pray for her.

Radhanath Swami had been leading *kīrtana* and praying for my mother at the exact time that she departed, and I felt that even though he and the other devotees had not been physically present in her room to chant Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare, by some transcendental arrangement they had been chanting for her, and she had received benefit. Twenty minutes after he had made the announcement, she left.

On June 8, I had posted on my disciples conference, “My dear mother is quite ill, and she may leave us at any time. So I request you all to pray for her.” After she left, I informed my disciples and asked them to continue to pray for her and to hold memorial programs for her in different places.

I also informed my dear godsister Yamunā-devī, who first met my parents when they came to Vrindavan in 1971. She had accompanied them through the village of Vrindavan and then to Mathura and Agra, where they saw the Taj Mahal. She wrote back, “I remember when Dina and I visited you in your house in Vrindavan. We asked you one question, and you took three hours to answer it: ‘How has your relationship with Śrīla Prabhupāda changed since his departure?’ The departure of loved ones helps us to change, to go deeper. Surely this will happen with your beloved mother’s departure.”

On Sunday, June 13, my sister called the rabbi, Alvin Sugarman, and informed him of our mother’s passing away, and she asked if he could attend her burial in Chicago. When I took the phone, he said, “At this very moment I am walking down Fifth Avenue in New York, and the Hare Krishna Festival of the Chariots is passing by. There is a lot of singing and dancing and giving away of food.”

There were formalities to be completed. We arranged for our mother’s body to be transported to Chicago to be buried next to our father’s. Rabbi Sugarman managed to stop on his way back to Santa Barbara and join us for her graveside service. Then we returned to Santa Barbara to sit shivah and have a special memorial service, at which I spoke about my mother and the eternality of the soul—the eternality of her soul. Later, Rabbi Stephen Cohen told me how impressed he had been by my talk, which had combined some of the teachings of the *Bhagavad-gītā* with my own experiences and realizations.

Over seventeen years before, on New Year’s Day 1987, also in Chicago, I had given another talk, at another family gathering, in which I had also drawn from Vedic philosophy. The occasion then had been one of joy—my sister’s wedding—and I had read an excerpt from Śrīla Prabhupāda’s book *Kṛṣṇa, the* *Supreme Personality of Godhead*. The passage had meant so much to my father that afterward he had asked me for the text, which describes how, in Krishna’s manifest pastimes on earth, He spent His early years in the care of His foster parents in the rural village of Vrindavan, some distance from His original parents in the city of Mathura. When, after sixteen years, Krishna and His brother Balaram were finally reunited with them, He told them:

“My dear father and mother, although you have always been anxious for the protection of Our lives, you could not enjoy the pleasure of having Us as your babies, as your growing boys, and as your adolescent youths. . . . My dear father and mother, a man cannot repay his debt to his parents, from whom he gets this body, which can bestow upon him all the benefits of material existence. According to the Vedic injunctions, this human form of life enables one to perform all kinds of religious activities, fulfill all kinds of desires, and acquire all kinds of wealth. And only in this human form is there every possibility that one can get liberation from material existence. This body is produced by the combined efforts of the father and mother. Every human being should be obliged to his parents and understand that he cannot repay his debt to them. If, after growing up, a son does not try to satisfy his parents by his actions or by an endowment of riches, he is surely punished after death by the superintendent of death and made to eat his own flesh. If a person is able to care for or give protection to old parents, a chaste wife, children, the spiritual master, *brāhmaṇas*, and other dependents but does not do so, he is considered already dead, although he is supposedly breathing. My dear father and mother, you have always been anxious for Our protection, but unfortunately We could not render any service to you. Until now We have simply wasted Our time; due to reasons beyond Our control, we could not serve you. Mother and Father, please excuse Us for Our sinfulness.” (*Kṛṣṇa* Ch 45)

It is often the case that when a son or daughter leaves home to pursue a higher, spiritual goal, he or she is unable to spend time with his or her parents, or serve them, as he or she would have liked. But the Lord, knowing the soft, caring heart of His servant, His devotee, out of His own compassionate heart takes care of the devotee’s parents. And so the Lord reassured Prahlāda, and thus all devotees, “Your father has been purified, along with twenty-one forefathers in your family. Because you were born in this family, the entire dynasty has been purified.”

“You have left your mothers and fathers,” Śrīla Prabhupāda had told me and another devotee in 1973, “and you have done the right thing.” For years, I had taken his statement as simple fact. But in the course of taking care of my parents at the end of their lives, I had sometimes questioned what I had done. Now the Lord’s instruction, and the history of Sri Prahlāda and his father and Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva, and my own practical experience with my parents, confirmed for me that I *had* made the right decision, even for my parents. “The parents of my disciples will realize their good fortune at the time of their passing away.”

## The Perspective of Relatives Caring for My Mother

A talk by Nirguṇa dāsī at the opening of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan on August 26, 2010.

My respects to all. Giriraj Swami, you and the hospice have been a divine arrangement for my being able to care for my mother in Vrindavan for the last two years of her life.

My parents brought me to Śrīla Prabhupāda when they met him in 1971 in Calcutta, where they also met Giriraj Swami and his parents. And they brought me to Vrindavan in 1975. So it was my great good fortune to be able to bring my mother to Vrindavan and care for her here.

In 2008 my brother reluctantly considered letting me bring my mother to Vrindavan from what had by then become “Kolkata.” His two concerns were the lack of proper medical care in Vrindavan and where I would stay, as I had to give up my rented apartment in MVT.

After some failed attempts at renting a flat, I spoke to my friend Viśākhā Priyā dāsī and asked if I might approach Giriraj Swami about renting his. I did, and Maharaja readily and kindly agreed. He requested only that I use it wisely in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s service.

My brother’s question about medical arrangements was answered by the palliative care offered by Bhaktivedanta Hospice. By the time I brought my mother to Vrindavan in the late summer of 2008, she had been suffering from Alzheimer’s disease for about five years and had been bedridden for the last three of those. She had requested that she never be taken to a hospital. So this facility made available by Maharaja in Vrindavan was most fortunate for her and for us.

In January this year my mother began to refuse food. She simply would not swallow anything I put in her mouth. Dr. Śyāma-vallabha and Dr. Anantasiṁha came regularly to see her, and they always answered my calls promptly. In addition to seeing to my mother, they also helped me to cope psychologically and emotionally, as it was so hard to see my mother starve to death, which was the reality of the situation. They arranged everything we needed to keep her as comfortable as possible— an air mattress, a device for administering measured amounts of pain medication, an oxygen cylinder, and medicines that we were sometimes unable to obtain, even after trying many places in Delhi.

A few days before Gaura-pūrṇimā, my mother stopped taking even water. Dr. Anantasiṁha expertly outlined all that was about to occur and helped me prepare myself. My mother was in the last stages of Alzheimer’s, and she had not spoken a coherent sentence in a year, yet in the last month, when we questioned her over and over, she clearly stated thrice that she did not want any food. Now, though she was too weak to speak, she would firmly close her lips if I tried to give her water. Dr. Śyāma-vallabha was also advising me on the phone, telling me to be strong and let her go. Finally, she went very peacefully and gracefully—one day after Gaura-pūrṇimā, in the midst of devotees singing Krishna’s names.

Thanks to Bhaktivedanta Hospice, my mother had state-of-the-art care at home—and in *sacred* Vrindavan. The hospice staff was always most helpful, coming regularly to give injections and replace catheters. In short, Bhaktivedanta Hospice was a God-sent, or Krishna-sent, in every sense of the word.

My brothers too were both moved by and extremely grateful for this selfless service. Through the hospice’s sincere care for my mother, they saw the true Vrindavan spirit of *sevā*.

The role of the hospice and Giriraj Swami in my mother’s exemplary departure onward from Vrindavan cannot be acknowledged enough. Bhaktivedanta Hospice is performing a service of utmost value. Their support for dying devotees, given with loving care, allows devotees to make their transition from this life in a dignified and peaceful way, in remembrance of Krishna. Surely this service is most pleasing to Śrīla Prabhupāda, who instilled in us the value of living and dying in the holy *dhāma*.

## Care, Support, and Love

A talk by Tilak Chand Mittal, a Vraja-vāsī, at the opening of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan on August 26, 2010.

My name is Tilak Chand. I stay in Mathura. My wife was very sick with cancer for five years. When I came to know about the hospice here, the Vrindavan Institute of Palliative Care, I brought her here.

The doctors told us, “She does not require to be admitted. She should be taken care of at home.” So we were treating her there. The team of doctors and nurses were visiting to give home care, and they also guided us about medical care and gave us spiritual counseling. At times, when her symptoms required, I would bring my wife here. The team gave us expert guidance about symptomatic treatment, and we were pleased with that. They gave us so much love. Along with my wife, our family members and I personally received a lot of care and support and encouragement from them.

On the 11th of July, 2010, my wife passed away. Because I am greatly indebted for the services received by my wife and also ourselves, even after her departure, I keep coming here regularly to ask if I can help and do some service.

I thank the hospice management for building this facility and providing these services. And I am very grateful to all of you.

## An Auspicious Departure— A Community Project

Ācārya Ratna dāsa

On October 20, 2010, I was talking to students after a class at IIT Kharagpur when I received a phone call from my elder brother, Sunil. “Come home immediately!” he said. “Father is in critical condition.”

When I reached home, my father was in a weakened, helpless condition. He could not sit or stand or walk, eat, move his limbs, or talk. He could not even recognize me.

I thought of all my father’s selfless sacrifice for me. He had been simple, humble, and compassionate, never demanding anything and always showing me a lot of love and affection. In all my life, he had never beaten or even scolded me. He had always encouraged me in my spiritual life, and even when I decided to quit my career as a software engineer and join ISKCON as a full-time *brahmacārī*, he had happily given his consent.

I also remembered my father’s pious activities. He had often arranged *satsaṅgas,* spiritual programs, and always served saintly people, inviting many sadhus to our home, giving them charity, and seeking their blessings.

So, I wondered, how could I serve him in this most important phase of his life, when he was approaching death? I meditated on this question, and for the whole day devotees, family members, relatives, villagers, and I did *nāma-saṅkīrtana* together. I thought of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s words in his purport to *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 1.9.43: “Every man or animal must die at a certain stage of life, but one who dies like Bhīṣmadeva attains perfection.” How, I considered, could I help my father to die like Bhīṣmadeva, in Krishna consciousness?

When I discussed the problem with Rādhe-Śyāma Prabhu, the temple president of ISKCON Pune, he told me about Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan, established to help people die with dignity in a devotional setting, and put me in touch with Dr. Viśvarūpa, the hospice’s assistant director. Dr. Viśvarūpa told me to bring my father immediately. When I asked him about the charges, he replied, “The official charges are Rs. 2,000 per day plus medicine, but as a full-time temple devotee, you have given your life to serve Krishna. Don’t worry about finances; just bring your father.”

I was happy and hopeful about taking my father to the hospice in Vrindavan, but our relatives and neighbors were against my father’s taking the long journey. They felt that he might leave his body along the way. But in the end we decided to go— my father, five relatives, and myself—and, with the prayers of the devotees, we arrived safely in Mathura. There we were met by two devotees, a stretcher, and an ambulance to transport my father to Vrindavan.

At the hospice, the staff welcomed us, oriented us, situated my father, and arranged for our stay, *prasāda*, and all necessities. Dr. Anantasiṁha spoke confidentially with the family, guiding us on how to take care of my father in a loving, caring, and sensitive way, and Dr. Viśvarūpa spoke to us about the nature of the soul, the glories of Vrindavan, and my father’s good fortune. The family’s doubts were all answered; everyone felt sure that we were in the right place.

My father was the hospice’s first ISKCON patient. His room was very nice—sunlit, well ventilated, and spacious enough to accommodate both him and the rest of us. We could hear the sweet chirping of birds outside, and from the window we could view the surrounding shade trees and get glimpses of cows from the nearby *gośālā*. My father’s bed could even be brought into the bathroom so that we could bathe him.

Before arriving, my father had developed a bedsore, but with the nurses’ care and an air-circulating bed, it healed. He had been coughing, but his chest soon cleared and he could breathe normally. Daily we would give him *caraṇāmṛta*, sacred water that has bathed the deities, from Vrindavan’s main temples; sprinkle him with holy waters from Rādhā-kuṇḍa, Śyāma-kuṇḍa, Mānasī-gaṅgā, and the Yamuna; and anoint his body with the dust of Vraja. We could see my father becoming more peaceful every day, which also relieved our anxieties.

While in the hospice, my father received so much expert care and loving attention. Dr. Viśvarūpa and Dr. Anantasiṁha were available around the clock, and nurses were always in attendance. Every day, Divyavapu Prabhu would bring *mahā* garlands and *caraṇāmṛta* from the deities of Krishna-Balaram Temple. Rādhikā Mātājī would come and read aloud from Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *Kṛṣṇa* book and talk with my mother and sister. And so many other devotees and staff would visit, provide whatever was necessary, and help make my father as comfortable as possible. It was the holy month of Kārttika, so devotees were visiting Vrindavan from all over the world, and many, including sannyasis and senior devotees, would come visit my father.

We would often have *kīrtana* in my father’s room in addition to the hospice’s regular morning program, afternoon *Kṛṣṇa* book readings, and evening *kīrtana*, *bhajana*, and singing of the *Dāmodarāṣṭaka* prayers, a highlight Karttika. Recordings of Prabhupāda singing played softly all through the night. The atmosphere was both home-like and spiritual.

On November 16 my father’s health became more critical. Devotees assembled to hold all-night *kīrtanas*, and prayers were offered from several temples in India. My sister sponsored the next day’s Deity services, and Bhakti Puruṣottama Swami offered a special *pūjā* to Nṛsiṁhadeva at ISKCON-Mayapur, in West Bengal. Radhanath Swami phoned from America, explaining to my father the glories of Vrindavan and service to Radha-Krishna, and chanting the *mahā-mantra* in his ear. Giriraj Swami also called, and chanted for almost ten minutes. I was awestruck to see all the divine arrangements—the intense love, affection, and mercy bestowed on my father.

Deena Bandhu Prabhu told us that there are three types of Vraja-vāsīs: those who are born in Vrindavan, those who live in Vrindavan, and those who die in Vrindavan. By the causeless mercy of *guru*, Gaurāṅga, and the Vaishnavas, at 9:18 a.m. on November 18, Kārttika Dvādaśī, amidst devotees chanting the holy name, my father departed from this world.

Dr. Viśvarūpa performed the final rites, and the next day the hospice hosted a memorial of talks and *kīrtana*, followed by a feast. With a choked voice, my brother told those assembled, “We could not have served our father so nicely at home. Even relatives won’t do as much as the devotees did here at the hospice. Bhaktivedanta Hospice and its devotees have made a permanent home in my heart.”

At home, my family members had been confused and bewildered, but at the hospice they gained courage and strength and felt themselves fortunate to have been in Vrindavan and helped their loved one depart. Later, we all did Vrindavan and Govardhana *parikramās*.

I am extremely grateful to have had the opportunity to serve my father in his last days and help him depart in the best possible Krishna conscious manner. My family members and relatives now feel very happy with and proud of my association with ISKCON, in which one can be offered so much genuine love, care, and selfless service, and they have increased their commitments in Krishna consciousness.

I would like to offer special thanks and gratitude to Dr. Viśvarūpa. He was, for me, not just a director and doctor, but also like an elder brother. He helped my father and family members in all the stages of my father’s illness and passing— before we arrived at the hospice, during our stay, during and immediately after the period of my father’s departure, and ever since. He still calls us to encourage us and give us inspiration.

It is said that to raise a child properly, it takes an entire village. Similarly, to help a person die gloriously it takes an entire community, and this is exactly what is provided by Vrindavan’s Bhaktivedanta Hospice.

## Chapter III – Hospice Care

## Preparing for Life’s Ultimate Test—with Help

*A talk by Indradyumna Swami at the residence of Saṅgītā dāsī (Susan Pattinson, RN, CHPN), in Wallingford, Pennsylvania, April* 23*,* 2008*.*

For a materialistic person the prospect of death is frightening. Why? Because such a person has little or no understanding of what transpires at death. It’s understood that people are afraid of what they don’t know. Many are afraid of the dark because they can’t see what’s happening around them when the lights are off. In the same way, they are afraid of death because they are in the dark about what happens at that time.

But devotees of the Lord aren’t in the dark—they know from the *Bhagavad-gītā* what happens at death. The Lord explains:

*dehino ’smin yathā dehe kaumāraṁ yauvanaṁ jarā*  
*tathā dehāntara-prāptir dhīras tatra na muhyati*

**“As the embodied soul continuously passes, in this body, from boyhood to youth to old age, the soul similarly passes into another body at death. A sober person is not bewildered by such a change.” (*****Bg*** [**2.13**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.13)**)**

Death is only a change of body. Life continues, so essentially there is no reason to lament. Even if one doesn’t go back to Godhead, if one has lived piously one gets a better birth. Krishna says to Arjuna on the battlefield of Kurukshetra:

*yadṛcchayā copapannaṁ svarga-dvāram apāvṛtam*  
*sukhinaḥ kṣatriyāḥ pārtha labhante yuddham īdṛśam*

**“O Pārtha, happy are the** ***kṣatriyas*** **to whom such fighting opportunities come unsought, opening for them the doors of the heavenly planets.” (*****Bg***  [**2.32**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.32)**)**

In Jagannath Puri, a holy place on the east coast of Odisha, the cremation ground near the beach is called Swarga Dwar, the gateway to heaven. It is understood that after death a pious person may be elevated to one of the heavenly planets, as may a devotee who has not yet fully awakened his or her Krishna consciousness.

In May 1977 Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote to his departed disciple Jayānanda dāsa, “I so hope at the time of your death you were remembering Krishna and as such, you have been promoted to the eternal association of Krishna. If not, if you had any tinge of material desire, you have gone to the celestial kingdom to live with the demigods for many thousands of years and enjoy the most opulent life of material existence. From there you can promote yourself to the spiritual world. . . . But as you were hearing *kṛṣṇa-kīrtana*, I am sure that you were directly promoted to Kṛṣṇaloka.”

Going back to Godhead is the desire of every devotee. In order to achieve it, devotees know they have to practice Krishna consciousness seriously, so that they will be able to remember the Lord at the time of death. You could say that the most important moment in one’s life is the moment of death. That may sound odd, but it’s a fact, because our consciousness at death determines whether we take birth again or go back to Godhead.

*yaṁ yaṁ vāpi smaran bhāvaṁ tyajaty ante kalevaram*  
*taṁ tam evaiti kaunteya sadā tad-bhāva-bhāvitaḥ*

**“Whatever state of being one remembers when he quits his body, O son of Kuntī, that state he will attain without fail.” (*****Bg*** **8.6)**

Our whole life is actually preparation for death. There is a Bengali proverb quoted by Śrīla Prabhupāda: *bhajan karo sadhan karo marte janle hoy*—“Whatever *bhajana* and *sādhana* one has performed throughout one’s life will be tested at the moment of death.”

Going back to the spiritual world is not as simple as some of our brothers and sisters may think—that if one is “good” he or she goes to heaven and if one is “bad” he or she goes to hell. It is much more complicated. Krishna says in the *Bhagavad-gītā* that the intricacies of karma are impossible to understand.

There is a story that an ascetic in India offered his blessings to various people, according to their situation.

*rāja-putra cirañ jīva mā jīva muni putraka*  
*jīva vā maro sādhur mā jīva marā iti*

**“O prince, may you live a long life; my dear** ***brahmacārī*****, may you die now; O saintly person, you may live or die; but you, O butcher, don’t live or die.”**

He blessed a prince to live long, because the prince was enjoying the fruits of his past pious activities but after death would have to reap the harvest of his present impious deeds.

He gave the benediction to a celibate monk, a *brahmacārī*, to die immediately, because he had been living a life of austerity and penance but as soon as he died he would gain the rewards for his spiritual practices.

He told a sadhu, a saintly person, that he could live or die, because he was transcendental to the material world and was thus unaffected by all so-called happiness and distress. In this life or the next, he would relish spiritual pleasure in service to God.

But to a butcher he said, don’t live or die—because his occupation was so horrible that by living he was accruing so many sinful reactions and in death he would suffer them all to the fullest extent.

So, our destination at death is determined by our actions in life. More specifically, they cause us to think in a certain way at death, which in turn causes us to take an auspicious or inauspicious birth.

As devotees, we try to serve Krishna throughout our lives with the hope of remembering Him at death. But what happens if we’re not conscious at death? After all, most people are not.

The great saint Rāmānujācārya once asked the *pūjārī* of Raṅganātha, the Deity of Lord Vishnu worshiped at Srirangam, to ask the Lord, “What happens if Your devotee cannot remember You at the moment of death?” And the Lord, through His *pūjārī*, His pure devotee, replied, “Then I will remember My devotee.” The Lord is so kind.

Still, we must practice Krishna consciousness seriously throughout our lives, for it is such service that attracts the attention of the Lord. It is said that just as a mother cow through affection can spot her calf among thousands of calves, so the Supreme Lord sees His devotee among the multitude of mankind, because of His love for the devotee.

The Lord Himself told Durvāsā Muni,

*sādhavo hṛdayaṁ mahyaṁ sādhūnāṁ hṛdayaṁ tv aham*  
*mad-anyat te na jānanti nāhaṁ tebhyo manāg api*

**“The pure devotee is always within the core of My heart, and I am always in the heart of the pure devotee. My devotees do not know anything else but Me, and I do not know anyone else but them.” (*****SB***  [**9.4.68**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-9/4.68)**)**

So, although death may not be a pleasant subject, it has to be discussed. We don’t want to make the mistake of the ostrich, which buries its head in the sand when confronted with danger. If we are prepared for death, we have nothing to fear. After death, our devotional service will continue here in the material world or, if we are so fortunate, in the spiritual world.

It’s a serious matter. One can’t cheat death. Once, there was a sculptor who visited an astrologer and learned the exact moment of his death. Some days before his predicted departure, he sculpted nine images of himself and placed the statues around his workshop. When Yamarāja, the superintendent of death, arrived on that fateful day, the man froze among the figures, thinking that Yamarāja would not be able to catch him. But, as we know, Yamarāja is one of the twelve Mahājanas, authorities on the science of Krishna consciousness. He is very intelligent. Yamarāja thought, “Enough of this nonsense” and called out, “Who is the so-called artist who has made these forms? Just see how faulty these creations are! The work is very sloppy. The limbs are out of proportion. The coloring and hues are all wrong.”

As Yamarāja was speaking, the sculptor’s false ego flared up and he said, “How can you say such things? I am an expert sculptor.”

Yamarāja smiled and said, “Thank you. Please come with me.”

One cannot cheat death. One has to soberly prepare for that final moment honestly—not dishonestly, like the sculptor. People are always making plans for the future, but they don’t realize that the future is eternal. They see only as far as their education, career, and retirement—no further.
