This is amazing! It is such an amazing phenomenon that it has been immortalized in the *Mahābhārata*. There it is described that while in the forest, four of the Pāṇḍava brothers died after drinking poisonous water in a lake. When Yudhiṣṭhira found them, he was devastated. Then suddenly a voice came from the sky. It was Yamarāja, who said that if Yudhiṣṭhira could answer thirteen questions correctly, he would bring Yudhiṣṭhira’s brothers back to life. The questions—and answers—were as follows:

Q: Who is the best companion?

A: Steady intelligence is a very good friend who can save one from all dangers.

Q: How can one acquire something very great?

A: By the performance of austerity.

Q: What is *amṛta* \[nectar\]?

A: Milk is nectar.

Q: What friend is bestowed upon a man by the demigods?

A: The wife.

Q: What is the best of all possessions?

A: Knowledge is one’s most valuable asset.

Q: What is the best happiness?

A: Satisfaction.

Q: When is a sacrifice considered to be dead?

A: A sacrifice is devoid of merit when there are no gifts given in charity to the brahmans.

Q: From where does one obtain the best of all food?

A: From the cow, because the milk she gives is made into ghee. Ghee is necessary for the performance of sacrifices, and it is sacrifice alone that produces the clouds that make rainfall. It is from rain that the seeds sprout and thus all foods are produced. Therefore it should be understood that the cow is the root cause of all food.

Q: What is the king of knowledge?

A: The king of education is the attainment of knowledge pertaining to the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

Q: What is the best bath?

A: The true bath is to wash the mind of all impurities.

Q: Who is truly happy?

A: One who cooks his own food, who is not a debtor, and who does not have to leave home in order to earn his livelihood is actually happy.

Finally Yamarāja asked: What is the most wonderful thing? Yudhiṣṭhira reflected for a moment and replied:

*ahany ahani bhūtāni gacchantīha yamālayam*  
*śeṣāḥ sthāvaram icchanti kim āścaryam ataḥ param*

**“Every day hundreds of millions of living entities go to the kingdom of death. Still, those who are remaining aspire for a permanent situation. What could be more wonderful than this?” (*****Mahābhārata*****,** ***Vana-parva*****, 313.116)**

This last question and Yudhiṣṭhira’s response are perhaps the most often quoted of the thirteen. And rightfully so, because although it is obvious that we are suffering in material life and will die, we still think we can remain and enjoy. And if we can’t enjoy in a first-class way, we’re so shameless that we’ll accept even fifth-class so-called pleasure in this world.

So, it’s imperative that we use time wisely to invest in our spiritual lives. That way, when death comes, as it always does— even if it is unexpected and untimely, we will be ready. But some investment must be there.

We are all—including those of us who may be a little careless about making such an investment in our lifetimes—fortunate to have devotees who are thinking about our departure. For example, Mother Saṅgītā’s main devotional service has been to help terminally ill devotees make a transition from one body to another—or better yet, from their temporary bodies to their eternal forms in the spiritual world. For years, she and others have been working hard to create an ideal situation in which we can leave our bodies. It is a hospice, but this hospice is more than just a place where one can die in peace. It is a place where devotees can leave their bodies in full Krishna consciousness, assisted by other devotees who are trained in the art of dying.

In large measure because of Saṅgītā’s vision and desire, there will soon be a hospice in Vrindavan, which is the best place to have a hospice. It is said that Vrindavan is the best place to be born, the best place to live, and the best place to die, because being born in Vraja, living in Vraja, and dying in Vraja all guarantee passage back home, back to Godhead. So even if we have not perfectly prepared ourselves for death, Mother Saṅgītā and the hospice staff will make it easier for us to remember the Lord at death. Of course, special mention must also be made of Giriraj Swami, for his great contribution to the building and running of the hospice.

So we are grateful to Saṅgītā for kindly thinking of our future. She is like an angel, working so hard to ensure that we have every opportunity to die as the Lord instructs us, in full Krishna consciousness. All of us should get to know her and appreciate her, for hers may well be the last face we see in this world!

## Bhaktivedanta Hospice—A Dream Fulfilled

Devotee Care

A talk by Gopal Krishna Goswami at the opening of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan on August 26, 2010.

I feel very honored to be here. I am really pleased by this wonderful project, which I have seen develop. I am very glad the project is completed and is being inaugurated today.

In a purport of *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta*, *Ādi-līlā*, Śrīla Prabhupāda writes about the service and dedication of Śrīpāda Īśvara Purī toward his spiritual master, Sri Mādhavendra Purī:

“At the last stage of his life Śrī Mādhavendra Purī became an invalid and was completely unable to move, and Īśvara Purī so completely engaged himself in his service that he personally cleaned up his stool and urine. Always chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* and reminding Śrī Mādhavendra Purī about the pastimes of Lord Kṛṣṇa in the last stage of his life, Īśvara Purī gave the best service among his disciples. Thus Mādhavendra Purī, being very pleased with him, blessed him, saying, ‘My dear boy, I can only pray to Kṛṣṇa that He will be pleased with you.’ Thus Īśvara Purī, by the grace of his spiritual master, Śrī Mādhavendra Purī, became a great devotee in the ocean of love of Godhead.

“Īśvara Purī pleased his spiritual master by service, and by the blessings of his spiritual master he became such a great personality that Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu accepted him as His spiritual master.” (*Cc Ādi* [9.11](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/9.11) purport)

It is stated in our scriptures that the Lord is pleased when one engages in the service of His devotee—*chāḍiyā vaiṣṇava-sevā nistāra pāyeche kebā*: unless one serves the Vaishnavas, one cannot get liberation from the material clutches. This principle of service, especially to the devotees of the Lord when they are preparing to leave their material bodies, and the idea of hospice are now part of ISKCON. Bhaktivedanta Hospital in Mumbai, under the most capable leadership and guidance of Radhanath Swami, has done tremendous service to Vaishnavas by providing them with care for their spiritual, emotional, mental, and bodily needs.

I have had the fortune of visiting Bhaktivedanta Hospital on numerous occasions, almost from the beginning, and I must say that the care the doctors there have given to the devotees is outstanding. I had the privilege just this year of seeing Maha-vishnu Goswami’s departure, and before that so many other devotees’, and it is really amazing how the doctors take extra pains and go out of their way to take care of the devotees. All the devotees who eventually left their bodies were exceedingly pleased by the efforts of the devotee doctors at the Bhaktivedanta Hospital.

Knowing the excellent work the team has done in Mumbai, I have no doubt that the hospice will be managed very well. I’ve seen devotees dying in Vrindavan since 1975. When somebody came and was about to leave his or her body, we would supply a room in the guesthouse, which was all we had. Then gradually, as more devotees started to come, we had to arrange facilities elsewhere. I visited the hospice when it was in the rented premises, and I must say that even there the care was first-class. I want to applaud everything that has been done in the temporary hospice and other makeshift facilities.

This is very much needed. One of the important things that the GBC is focusing on is devotee care. In the early days we were all committed to spreading the movement, increasing book distribution, etc., but now our leadership has realized that unless we take care of devotees, the other things are not so important. So great stress is now being placed on devotee care. There is a GBC subcommittee called Devotee Care, and that committee is focusing on identifying areas in which devotees can be taken care of and has even established a sound devotee-care policy. This will go a long way to inspire devotees to dedicate their lives to this Krishna consciousness movement, because when devotees see that a senior devotee is about to leave his body and no one’s taking care of him, they lose faith in the movement. So this hospice will definitely serve an urgent need, because our devotees are getting older—devotees who have dedicated their lives to this movement.

I am very grateful to Giriraj Swami for playing such a big role in helping Prabhupāda in the early days of establishing his movement in India, especially the temple in Bombay, and for the contribution and the sacrifice he has made in realizing this hospice project. And I am also grateful to Radhanath Swami for organizing the whole team, for taking up this very important service to the Vaishnava community.

Until today, I hadn’t been so aware about Arcā-vigraha dāsī being this project’s inspiration, but I did have the privilege of meeting her some fifteen years ago, on several occasions. She was a nice devotee.

From our side, we try our best to do whatever we can, and we wish the hospice all success. I have full confidence that it will be successfully managed, and I think that over time we will have to build more hospices. This is just the beginning of a chain of facilities that the Krishna consciousness movement needs to provide for taking care of devotees. The hospice is just one angle of approach. We have to take other angles also, because devotees are looking forward to devotee care and this is one of the very important agenda items that the movement is now pursuing.

## A Great Service

A talk by Padmanābha Goswami at the opening of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan on August 27, 2010.

Respected Sri Giriraj Maharaja, senior devotees—Abhirāma Prabhu, Brahmānanda Prabhu, Deena Bandhu Prabhu, and *mātājīs*—just this morning I got the order and invitation from Giriraj Maharaja: “Today is the opening ceremony of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice, so you have to come and join us.” I told Maharaja, “Yes, I have some meetings in Mathura, but I’ve decided that I have to cancel those meetings and attend this ceremony,” because I have great attachment for the land where we are doing this ceremony. Actually, many devotees, especially the Prabhupāda disciples, know that we have had a very close relation with ISKCON and the Gauḍīya Maṭha for the last four generations. Our great-grandfather, Śyāma Madhusūdana Goswami, was a very close associate of Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura and Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī. My grandfather, Sri Krishna Chaitanya Goswami, was very close to Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī. And my father, Viśvambhara Goswami, was very close to Śrīla Bhaktivedanta Prabhupāda. For generations, our family has been engaged in the service of Sri Rādhā-ramaṇa, Śrīla Gopāla Bhaṭṭa Gosvāmī’s Deity.

I just want to explain to you that this land where Giriraj Swami has constructed this hospice belonged to my family for the last five generations, more than one hundred and fifty years. There were only *kadamba* and *tamāla* trees in this area, and in the middle of the ground floor of the structure that was here, there was a room and stairs, and on the first floor there was only one room with a gate and window Because it was so peaceful—one of the most beautiful places in Vrindavan—my great-grandfather often came here to chant. There was not much construction—only a few buildings were around.

When Śrīla Prabhupāda came to Vrindavan and construction of the Krishna-Balaram temple was going on here in Raman Reti, he often came to this land on his morning walk and would chant here. So this land is very auspicious and very special. A few times I saw him here with devotees, walking around and chanting.

I want to tell you the brief history. In 1990 Śrīla Prabhupāda’s senior disciples and my good friends Abhirāma Prabhu and Ranchor Prime, from London, both came to me in my Gaura Mahaprabhu temple during the Jhulana-yātrā time. They had a plan to make a Bhaktivedanta Park for devotees to come to walk and chant, and they asked me to sell this land, which at that time belonged to me, my brother, and my cousins. We have six or seven family members, and they had decided that we had to make plots and sell them, but I was not agreeing, because of just one condition—I didn’t want to cut the *kadamba* and *tamāla* trees from this land. This is the only land in the whole of Vrindavan where you can see this many *kadamba* and *tamāla* trees.

So when Abhirāma and Ranchor requested me to sell this land, I told them, “Okay, I will discuss with my family members and my cousins.” They were ready. So I told Abhirāma, “We are ready.” We sold this land, but with one condition made to Abhirāma and Ranchor: “Please try to maintain these *kadamba* trees and *tamāla* trees. Don’t cut them. Whatever the design and whatever construction you do, according to your map, you just save these trees.” Today, from the bottom of my heart, I thank Maharaja and Abhirāma Prabhu that they saved these trees.

So this is the history about this land. This land is not an ordinary land. This land is not a simple piece of land. This land is where the Goswamis, and also Śrīla Prabhupāda, spent time chanting, and it is just near the *parikramā* path.

Then Abhirāma told me that Giriraj Maharaja wanted to construct a hospice here. All these things, I told him, are very auspicious for the Vraja-vāsīs. *Durlabho mānuṣo deho dehināṁ* *kṣaṇa-bhaṅguraḥ*. This human life is very rare. Nobody knows when it will finish. When devotees from all over the world come to Vrindavan, they want to spend their life here, they want to die here, because they want the association of Radha and Krishna.

So this very nice service of starting a hospice on this land in this holy city of Vrindavan, was inspired by Śrīla Prabhupāda, the great personality of this universe, and it was carried out by Giriraj Maharaja. Actually, this is not a simple or an ordinary thing. This is a big responsibility, and when Lord Krishna lifted Girirāja, all the Vraja-vāsīs put up their sticks to help Krishna lift the hill, so we are the Vraja-vāsīs—we just put up our sticks, and Maharaja will lift all this Girirāja. Actually, it is Girirāja-dhāraṇa who lifted Girirāja. And we are all the Vraja-vāsīs— what can we do? What these doctors can do? They cannot do anything without the mercy of Radha and Krishna. But here is the best thing: all the medical services will come with the spiritual atmosphere. Here the doctors will give the capsule with *hari-nāma*. Here the doctors will give the pills with *hari-nāma*. Here the doctors will put the injection with *hari-nāma*. So the devotees will not feel any pain.

This is a great service for all human beings. Actually, the doctors are like a second God. When the patients suffer with pain, the doctor’s job is also a very big job.

So today I pray at the lotus feet of Sri Rādhā-ramaṇajī for the great success of Giriraj Maharaja’s mission and this project, and I want to see that this project should be spread in other places also for the service of human beings and the devotees. Today I am really thankful to Giriraj Maharaja for giving this opportunity and inviting me here to this ceremony. Thank you.

## Unlimited Service, Unlimited Mercy

A talk by Giriraj Swami at the opening of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan, August 26–27, 2010.

It is a great occasion for us all to be here for this landmark of the completion of some service and the beginning of more service. That is the nature of devotional service, which is both the means and the end—it never stops.

Hearing Padmanābha Goswamiji speak about the land, I was reminded of Śrīla Prabhupāda and the Juhu land. Before Śrīla Prabhupāda went to America he was staying in Andheri at the Scindia Colony and was coming to Juhu every day to read *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* to Srimati Sumati Morarji. On the way to her bungalow, he would pass some land, and he would think, “This would be a nice place to have a temple of Krishna.” And then, so many years later, it came to be. When I hear of the rich tradition of *bhajana* here and how Śrīla Prabhupāda also came here on his morning walks and chanted, I can understand that he must have appreciated the place, and now it has come in his service.

The beginning of this project was Mother Arcā-vigraha’s desire. She had built a house in Radha Raman Bagicha, which had been part of this same large area owned by the Rādhā-ramaṇa Goswamis. Just adjacent to her plot, the British ISKCON *yātrā* had bought a plot and built a facility where Śivarāma Swami and Govinda Swami often stayed.

Arcā-vigraha was a renowned artist in South Africa, and she had traveled the world looking for both spiritual enlightenment and themes for her artwork. She had lived with Bedouins in the desert and with different types of people in different parts of the world. And when she came in touch with Krishna consciousness, she thought to go to India and build a new home. When she was diagnosed with cancer, I took it that Krishna was accelerating her journey back home and suggested that she move to Vrindavan.

So she came here to spend her last days. She loved Vrindavan. Externally Vrindavan is not now exactly what it was like fifteen years ago, when Arcā left (though Vrindavan is always Vrindavan). Then, there was a bird sanctuary near where we are now, between here and Arcā’s house, and every morning and evening the trees would fill with peacocks and cuckoos and parrots, and you could hear their calls. Just next to her house was a small ashram and *gośālā*, with sadhus and cows, and down the street was Balaram Baba and ever-present *kīrtana*. One night on Gaura-pūrṇimā, which is also when Holi is celebrated, there had been a dramatic performance of *kṛṣṇa-līlā*, and the two children who had played Radha and Krishna came into her room dressed as Radha and Krishna to give (and take) her *darśana*. She just loved Vrindavan.

Arcā wanted other devotees at the end of their lives to have the same opportunity that she had to live in Vrindavan in a home, or homelike atmosphere, with the association of devotees. And it really seems that Srimati Radharani and Śrīla Prabhupāda have made all arrangements. I had never heard the term “hospice” at the time she expressed her desire, but later, when I came to know what hospice care was, I realized that Arcā-vigraha had gotten the equivalent of it just by the love and inspiration of the devotees. Devotees who knew physical therapy came, devotees who knew massage came—and there were also people who offered esoteric healing modalities that had mixed results. But devotees came forward to help her physically, or medically, and devotees also came to encourage her spiritually, by reading and chanting and speaking with her about Krishna consciousness. Senior men—Tamal Krishna Goswami, Śivarāma Swami, Keśava Bhāratī Maharaja—came regularly; Bhūrijana Prabhu came every afternoon.

And Bhakti Bhṛṅga Govinda Swami was amazing; he was there all the time. After doing his *pūjā* in the morning, he would bring his Gaura-Nitāi Deities and *govardhana-śilās* and place them on Arcā’s altar, within her view. Whatever it was, he would be there; if there was any crisis, he would be there immediately. And he took care of the caregivers, making sure that they were all right. He became a *śikṣā-guru* to Arcā-vigraha. We always talk about remembering Krishna at the time of death, but what does that mean? Do we think of Krishna playing a flute in the clouds? What does it actually mean? Govinda Swami would explain it to her: it means that you think of Krishna in Vrindavan with His associates in His pastimes, and you may even think of His associates with a personal mood of longing to assist them in their service to Him. All the readings, *japa*, *kīrtana*, and pilgrimages to holy places took on even greater meaning and significance. Devotees would come and read to Arcā, and sometimes we would recite prayers with her, beautiful prayers composed by our *ācāryas*. We would have dramatic readings of Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *Vidagdha-mādhava*, with Tamal Krishna Goswami, Śivarāma Swami, Govinda Swami, and other devotees playing different roles.

It was a very uplifting experience for all of us, and I think that is something that everyone who works with dying people, especially those who are God conscious, experiences. Serving devotees and caring for devotees, especially at the end of life, is extremely rewarding. As Shakespeare wrote, “The quality of mercy is not strained. It droppeth as the gentle rain from heaven . . . It is twice blessed; it blesseth him that gives and him that takes.” Service to the devotees pleases Śrīla Prabhupāda and Lord Krishna, and that is what bhakti means: to please Krishna—by pleasing His devotees.

Originally I had very ambitious ideas for fulfilling Arcā-vigraha’s request. I wanted to get a large area of land and construct cottages like small houses, so each devotee would have his or her own house, just like Arcā-vigraha did. But when we saw that getting that much land for an affordable price would take us far from the Krishna-Balaram temple, we decided against it. We figured that we would do what hospices try to do almost everywhere: create the atmosphere of a home in an institutional setting. In visiting various hospices in America, I witnessed that. They have a living room and dining room and other arrangements to make the patient feel at home. We had made it our first priority to care for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s followers who had given their lives in his service, but after discussions with Abhirāma Prabhu and Padmanābha Goswamiji and other devotees, we concluded that we must also serve the Vraja-vāsīs, and that, too, is happening.

So many people helped along the way, I can’t even begin to name them, but one was Abhirāma. Just before Tamal Krishna Goswami went to Mayapur for what was to be the last time, Goswami Maharaja and I had a long talk on the phone, for which I am very grateful. We discussed practically every aspect of my service and my life, including the hospice project. He said, “I am very interested in this project, and if you want you can make me a trustee.” Then he left, and that created . . . We’ve all been seeing how the departure of a Vaishnava has tremendous effects. In some ways, Vaishnavas can have more effect through their departures than they had during their presence. So, one effect of Tamal Krishna Goswami’s departure was that the whole movement became united—which was very rare—in Mayapur. The devotees became united in remembering him and glorifying him, a Vaishnava, a servant of Śrīla Prabhupāda. And then the people who were dear to the departed Vaishnava become more dear to you, because of their connection with the departed soul.

Such was the case with my relationship with Abhirāma Prabhu. At one stage, Tamal Krishna Goswami felt that the management in ISKCON needed a little jolt or boost, not that the same people, who have too much to do, and who may not even have much experience or qualification to do it, keep trying to do everything. His idea was that certain householders, whom he considered to be very qualified, should be given a chance. One was Abhirāma Prabhu, one was Nareśvara Prabhu, and one was Advaita Candra Prabhu. And so there was some reorganization of important committees, including the MVT.

So I knew that Abhirāma had a relationship with Tamal Krishna Goswami and that he was expert in building resorts and ashrams. And I had been very impressed with the MVT, so I phoned him, and we both saw it as an opportunity for us to serve Śrīla Prabhupāda and Tamal Krishna Goswami together. His contribution in terms of the development was great.

One contribution that I should acknowledge is that for Śrīla Prabhupāda’s Centennial different devotees had donated for what was to be a Prabhupāda Park. The land here was purchased, but then nothing was developed—it was just there. As one by one, various lands that we thought might be suitable for the hospice vanished, Abhirāma thought of the park land, and he approached the different donors, and they all were completely willing to dedicate the land to the hospice. They thought, “This is Prabhupāda’s service—serving Prabhupāda’s devotees. Prabhupāda will be more pleased.” And so they gave the park land for the use of the hospice.

There were so many devotees involved in the purchase of this land and the development of the project—too many to mention. Likewise, with Arcā-vigraha, so many devotees from the community came to help—singing in *kīrtana*, reading, bringing deities, bringing *mahā-prasāda*. Govinda Maharaja used to make a peanut *burfi* that Arcā really liked. That raises another important issue: quality of life versus quantity of life. It is an axiomatic principle in hospice care that the patient has the right to decide how he or she wants to spend his or her last days. And quite often we may have to choose between quantity and quality. Peanut butter *burfi*, for example, may not be so healthy for a patient, but in a small way, it may improve his or her quality of life.

In the context of hospice care, quality of life often means to what extent the patient wants to accept medical interventions that will disturb his or her consciousness. To what extent does he or she want to be free from impediments to just think about Krishna, chant and hear about Krishna, and have loving exchanges with devotees and family members. A devotee may decide, “I don’t want chemo,” “I don’t want radiation,” “I don’t want all these tubes in me,” or, “I don’t want to go to the hospital”“I just want to live my life in its natural course and remember Krishna and reciprocate with loved ones.” That’s something we have to respect. Family members and loved ones will naturally want the person to stay longer. I’m also like that. We were like that with Prabhupāda. But ultimately it is really between the person and Krishna. It is not for us to decide or try to impose our will upon them. Sometimes by getting out of the way of their desires in terms of their departure, we’re doing them a better service.

So, we want this hospice to be a house—Prabhupāda’s house for his sick children—and we invite you to come and serve the devotees here in any way. A devotee, even during life, but especially at the end, may go through different periods. For some time a devotee may really just want to be alone and process things on his or her own—with just Krishna and Prabhupāda, or maybe a few intimate friends. At other times the person may want company, to open the doors wide and let anyone who wants, enter. We have to be sensitive to that.

Some services to the patients will require training. For two months—January and February—Mother Saṅgītā and Mother Kauliṇī will be here giving a training course for skilled volunteers to help the patients. And there are other services— making garlands for the deities, cooking, cleaning, whatever— that devotees can offer quite easily, even to patients in their own homes. In fact, we minister to more patients in their own places than in our physical facility here. They don’t have to stay here. We also want to extend our care to patients at home.

The opening of the hospice is a landmark; we have come to a certain stage in our service. But it is also just a beginning. When people have a terminal illness, you could say it is the beginning of the end, and for us, opening the hospice is the end of the beginning—now our real service begins.

I feel greatly blessed, honored, privileged, and almost overwhelmed to be part of this effort with so many wonderful devotees. At the beginning, before I had even mentioned the project to anyone else, I asked Kṛṣṇa-candra dāsa (Hrishikesh Mafatlal), “Is this possible? Can we do this?” And he said, “Yes, I’ll be there for you. Don’t worry.” He gave assurance. At that time devotees were helping more on an individual basis. Raghunātha dāsa was the director of the Bhaktivedanta Hospital, and he was interested. It began like that, but gradually we realized that the project was huge, beyond just Kṛṣṇa-candra and Raghunātha and Abhirāma and Saṅgītā and me. We needed local devotees who would always be there, who wouldn’t come and go. Then I discussed the situation with Radhanath Swami, who immediately stepped forward. Maharaja had just been serving Bhakti Tirtha Swami, who was so much in the mood of caring for devotees, and he, like so many of us, was inspired and influenced by that mood. So, Radhanath Swami said, “Yes, I’ll speak to the doctors.” And Mādhavānanda and Viśvarūpa have been wonderful. They have been friends since their college days, and they complement each other in their work very nicely.

Our debt of gratitude is unlimited. All I can say is “Hare Krishna.” It’s all in those two words—everything is there, without limit. Hare Krishna.

## Celebrating a Year of Service—Bhaktivedanta Hospice

Fifteen Months Later

Talks by Radhanath Swami, Mālatī dāsī, Giriraj Swami, and Viśvarūpa dāsa at Bhaktivedanta Hospice in Vrindavan on November 7, 2011.

RADHANATH SWAMI: We are very grateful for all of you who have come here today.

Śrīla Prabhupāda came to Vrindavan when he was almost sixty years old, when most people retire from life, and spent six years here preparing to fulfill the glorious mission given to him by his spiritual master. When he was sixty-nine he departed from Vrindavan for Mumbai and Calcutta and then sailed to the West on the Jaladuta.

When Śrīla Prabhupāda became extremely ill in the early days of ISKCON, in 1967, he returned to Vrindavan, having told his disciples that the best place for him to leave this world would be Vrindavan—and that if Krishna wished, the best place for him to get well to carry on the mission would be Vrindavan. He stayed in his rooms at the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple, and sure enough, with the atmosphere of Sri Vrindavan-dhāma, his health became strong again. And for the next ten years he traveled throughout the world, until 1977, when he again became ill. Then he came to Vrindavan for his last days.

Śrīla Prabhupāda taught how to live a life of Krishna consciousness, how to give our life in Krishna consciousness, and how to depart from the world in Krishna consciousness. And regarding the future after his departure, he entrusted his mission to all his sincere and dedicated followers for all generations to come.

He established the Krishna-Balaram temple to give people from all over the world an opportunity to come here and learn how to live in Krishna consciousness. His intention was that with the inspiration they gained from the Krishna-Balaram temple and the association of devotees, they would travel throughout the world and give their lives in Krishna consciousness, which means giving Krishna to others.

Bhaktivedanta Hospice is a facility to give the best possible care—physical, emotional, and spiritual—to devotees who have given their hearts and souls and lives in devotional service, so they can depart this world surrounded by loving devotees, in Prabhupāda’s embrace, at the lotus feet of Krishna-Balaram here in Raman Reti, Prabhupāda’s eternal home. If you depart this world in Prabhupāda’s home, then the purpose of returning to Prabhupāda’s home beyond this physical body will be fulfilled. Of course, we know that anywhere a devotee passes his or her life is supremely auspicious if the devotee has given his or her life to devotional service. But still, it is our tradition to come to Vrindavan, or Mayapur, or Puri—the holy places—to pass from this world.

Giriraj Swami had a loving disciple, Arcā-vigraha dāsī, and as she was passing from this world in Vrindavan, her concern, her care, for people—and Giriraj Swami’s own love for the Vaishnavas—planted in his heart the seed to create a place to care for devotees who are preparing to pass from this world in Vrindavan. He and I discussed this, and devotees from Bhaktivedanta Hospital as well as from our congregation in Mumbai, especially Kṛṣṇa-candra and others, were very enthusiastic to take full responsibility.

It was an incredible effort to plan and build this hospice, to deal with all that it takes in a little place like Vrindavan, in Uttar Pradesh in India, to create this facility to train people in hospice care and engage them in service. Actually, from a medical perspective—staff, pharmacy, medical care—it is a huge project.

And we deeply and firmly believe in Śrīla Prabhupāda and Chaitanya Mahaprabhu and our *ācāryas’* message that service to Vraja-vāsīs is a very, very dear service to Krishna. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu wanted us never to consider Vrajavāsīs to be ordinary people. Prabhupāda once chastised a devotee for getting into an argument with a Vraja-vāsī, and he told him to leave Vrindavan. Many of these Vraja-vāsīs, practically from birth, in their own special, unique ways, love Krishna.

So we wanted this hospice to serve the Vraja-vāsīs and to serve those devotees in ISKCON who have given so much of their lives and souls. And the devotees engaged at the hospice, they are not going to become rich, they are not going to become famous, but they have dedicated so much money, made so much endeavor, to build and maintain the hospice. They desire only to be instruments of Prabhupāda’s and Radharani’s and Krishna’s blessings upon devotees who are here in Vrindavan on that final journey, the ultimate journey home, back to Godhead.

These are the principles and the inspiration of the Bhaktivedanta Hospice.

I am here today simply to offer my deep, heartfelt gratitude to all the devotees who are part of this effort, because it can be a thankless task. Very few people know this, but these devotees are giving their lives to perform a very, very intimate, beautiful service, one of the most difficult services—to help people leave behind this material world forever and enter into Radharani’s and Śyāmasundara’s loving embrace. For those who are not from Vrindavan, who come from cities and other places to stay and live here only to be surrounded by death and dying twenty-four hours a day, it is a great sacrifice, and they are doing it out of love, devotion, *sevā*.

So to everyone involved in this project, we just want you to know that we recognize you and offer you so much gratitude and love.

Thank you very much.

MĀLATĪ DĀSĪ: Two weeks ago in Mumbai there was a presentation to the North American GBC representatives regarding the hospice, and I was amazed by the amount of endeavor that was put into establishing this very impressive building and by the amount of attention in planning how to take care of the various types of people (local people, foreign people, ISKCON people) when they come here. The only lament that Kṛṣṇa-candra expressed to me was that this beautiful first-class facility is underutilized, and the main reason, from what I can understand, is that people aren’t really aware of it.

I knew I would be coming to Vrindavan shortly, and one of my desires was to become more familiar with the hospice. The day I came I was quite surprised. I have a friend here in Vrindavan who doesn’t speak English, and I don’t speak Hindi, but we have been friends since the nineties, since I first met her here. Her name is Hasti Tuṣṭī dāsī, and she is eighty-five years old, a disciple of Gopal Krishna Maharaja. Our communication is through the heart, and I usually see her in the temple. But this time I hadn’t seen her for a few days, and I was wondering where she was. To my surprise, when I came to the hospice, here she was. And she was quite happy to be here.

After I met her, I took a tour of the hospice, and I immediately felt that I wanted to do some service. And the service that I could see myself doing was to help raise awareness of the hospice.

There are so many details here that one can appreciate— like the fact that when the building was built, the architectural plans had to be redrafted to accommodate all the trees, so that none would be lost. When I heard that, I thought if so much attention is given to the trees, imagine the attention given to the fortunate human beings who come here.

And to see Śrīla Prabhupāda sitting here with us—he was the first person I saw when I entered the hospice. When I entered through the doorway and saw Śrīla Prabhupāda, I felt everything was all right.

I am very honored to be here with all of you. If there is any way I can assist in creating awareness for the hospice, it would be my great honor to do so.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: It is a great honor and privilege and pleasure to be here. We were last here on Lord Balaram’s appearance day to open the hospice, and now one year has passed and the activities of the hospice are continuing in a most exemplary way, which is to the credit of the staff—the doctors and nurses and administrators—all the staff. It is often easier to create something than to maintain it. Of course, it was not easy to create the hospice, but if maintenance is more difficult than creation, then how much more difficult it will be to maintain the hospice. But these dedicated devotees have stayed together and stuck with the project through various trials and tribulations, which are inevitable in life.

Our whole practice of Krishna consciousness is tested at the time of death. Śrīla Prabhupāda used to quote a saying that whatever *sādhana* and *bhajana* you do in your life will be tested at the time of death. *Bhajan karo sadhan karo marte janle hoy*: “Whatever spiritual activities you do will be tested at the time of death.” Death is the final exam. Therefore it is the most critical time in a person’s life, and the devotees entrusted with the care of a person—physical, emotional, and spiritual—have a great responsibility and also a great opportunity to help the soul achieve the ultimate goal of life.

From the material point of view, it is quite trying to care for a terminal patient. In ordinary medical professions, the doctors and nurses attend to the patient in the hope that the patient will get better and return to a normal, happy life in the material world. But in hospice care every patient dies, and so it is a different mood. Even in the West, they don’t have hospitals and hospices together, because the mood is too different. The mood of taking treatment to get better to go back to life as it was before entering the hospital doesn’t go well with the mood of the hospice patients. So they always keep the two divisions separate.

For the soul to detach itself from the body is a very difficult process, and it may be a painful one, but the devotees who help the soul through that process are the best friends and well-wishers of that soul—and also of the family members and other loved ones of the patient, for whom the experience can be very stressful and trying. The hospice staff support them as well— physically and emotionally and spiritually. So it’s an opportunity to serve devotees and their loved ones. Sometimes the loved ones are not themselves devotees, but I’ve gotten reports from devotees whose parents have come to Vrindavan to die, that their family members became transformed, to some extent by the Vrindavan atmosphere, but to the greatest extent by the love and care of the devotees. They have never in their lives seen such love and care. In one case, the siblings of a devotee remarked, “We always thought that you left the family and that we were the ones taking care of our parents, but actually now, at the most critical time, we see that you’re the one who’s taking care of our mother and all of us.” So it can be a very transformative experience for friends and family members to see the love and care of the doctors and nurses and volunteers for the patients and their families.

But the service requires spiritual realization. It requires realization that the soul is different from the body. The reason hospitals and even more so hospices are dreaded is because people identify with the body. When ordinary people hear the word *hospice*, they often think, “Oh no, not hospice,” because to them it means defeat. It means that you are giving up hope of fighting the disease; you’re admitting defeat in the face of death. And they don’t want that, because they identify with the body. They don’t have any concept of life beyond the body. But the devotees—doctors, nurses, volunteers, and anyone else involved in any way—know that the real person is not the body but the soul, that the bondage of the soul within the body is actually a miserable condition, and that if the soul becomes free from the repetition of birth, death, old age, and disease in varieties of material bodies, that is the best thing that could ever happen.

A newspaper reporter once challenged Śrīla Prabhupāda, “Your philosophy is very negative. You are very negative about the world and about work in the world.” And Śrīla Prabhupāda replied, “Our business is to go to the prisoners and educate them: ‘The jail life is not your real life. Your breaking bricks is not your proper work. You should get out of the jail and be free.’ So is that negative? That’s very positive.” So, it is actually a very positive experience to leave the body if in the process you become free from this imprisonment in the jail of material existence, in the physical body; and it is that knowledge, that realization, and that faith that propel all the devotees involved with the project and make each case a glorious opportunity to help a soul go back home, back to Godhead, and to help everyone connected with that soul realize who they are and what is the real purpose of life.

So I put my head at the feet of these devotees. It’s easier to get a good idea than to implement it. And these devotees have implemented it—not just in the act of creation, but every day, every hour, every minute, every second, as an ongoing process. We are eternally indebted to them.

Although it is true that not everyone is aware of the hospice yet, as they become more aware they’ll come and take more advantage of it. But the fact that it is here gives great hope and encouragement to the devotees, and to anyone who knows about it, because the time will come for all of us. As Śrīla Prabhupāda said to one of his young disciples during His Divine Grace’s last days here in Vrindavan, “Don’t think this won’t happen to you.” It will happen to all of us. And just knowing that this facility is here, that this care is here, gives great hope.

We have focused on the critical time of leaving the body, but care is needed in every stage of life. We need hospitals with devotees who exemplify that type of care. We need to help old people as they become less capable to take care of themselves. They may not be terminal patients, but in old age they gradually lose the ability to take care of themselves. So we need that sort of care. We need that spirit of care to extend to every area of a person’s life. But because everything is tested at the time of death, culminates at the time of death, we’ve begun this project.

The devotees involved with Bhaktivedanta Hospice are special, great souls. I feel honored and blessed to be associated with them, if only in a small way. And I am sure that they will get immense blessings from Śrīla Prabhupāda, from all the devotees, and ultimately from Sri Sri Krishna-Balaram, who are so eager to get Their lost sons and daughters back to Them.

Hare Krishna.

VIŚVARŪPA DĀSA: I feel honored and grateful for the wonderful opportunity that was offered to us in 2006 when this project came to us, and we feel very, very enlivened. We feel that Śrīla Prabhupāda is honoring us with more and more service— more intense service, more crucial service. That is why I am very grateful to Radhanath Swami Maharaja and Giriraj Swami Maharaja for thinking us to be a little capable of handling this service, and I am sure that it is only with their blessings that there are some positive results.

We serve patients who are terminally ill, who have a life expectancy of six months or less (those who have terminal diseases but whose life expectancy is more than six months stay at home). So we have inpatient care here, and we have home care. About 80 percent of the people who are registered with us stay in their homes, and our team visits them to provide regular care there.

The hospice also offers Respite Care for patients’ caregivers. When caretakers become emotionally or physically exhausted, the staff offers fifteen days of relief, taking care of the patient at the hospice facility. During this time, caretakers go to be with their children, do yoga, catch up on sleep, and come back feeling rejuvenated to take the patients back home. That is called Respite Care.

The fourth type of care we give is a most important, essential aspect of this hospice’s service—End-Of-Life Care. When the final stage comes, when the active dying process begins, there are some big transitions for the soul to make, and a great deal of emotional upheaval. So we have a well-planned, well-designed package of services for that time—mainly spiritual care and counseling, with about 10 percent medical care—for comfort management.

The last is Bereavement Care. When a patient leaves, we care for his near and dear ones, who are affected emotionally and spiritually, for over thirteen months, until after the first anniversary of the death, when the family members relive the whole event again. So, until one month after that, we take care of the family; we follow up with them.

The medical care here is mainly directed toward comforting the patient. We give no guarantee or assurance that the patient will recover from the disease, but we do everything we can to make his journey back home as comfortable as possible, with as little pain as possible. And we also care for the nearby family members who themselves are helping to care for the patient. So we are also caring for the caretakers. Our hospice offers complete holistic care; we take care of the patient who is dying, and we take care of all the people who are taking care of the dying person. They also undergo emotional ups and downs, many unexpected. Some are angry, some are depressed, some are frustrated. The patient is also sometimes angry, sometimes depressed, sometimes frustrated—not always accepting. So all these different kinds of emotional traumas receive our attention. This is what hospice is.

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s disciples, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s followers, and the blessed Vraja-vāsīs who have dedicated their lives in service to Radha and Krishna—we feel it is a great honor and privilege to try to comfort them in the last challenging moments of their lives and to help them to remember Radha and Krishna. Their remembering can be hampered by pain, so we try to help them to break free and remember, to assist them in every possible manner so that they can remember the Lord at their last passing moment. Sometimes our *guru mahārāja* says that it is like we take them all the way to the door of Godhead.

So we thank everybody, especially Radhanath Maharaja, for this wonderful service. I am very proud to be a participant in this group, and we hope that we will be able to do our jobs nicely. We only pray to the devotees that our consciousness may always remain humble, with a service attitude, and that we can serve the devotees all the time in the future. Also it is my privilege that because of this service, Giriraj Maharaja is in touch with us and we can speak to him regularly and get his guidance. We are deeply honored.

## Chapter IV – Facing Death Die Before Dying

*Adapted from Śacīnandana Swami’s introduction to* The Beggar IV: Die before Dying *by Bhakti Tirtha Swami*.

“Philosophy,” said Śrīla Prabhupāda, “means to keep death in front of your eyes.” Death is not to be ignored. It can teach us important lessons for life, because death forces us to look for what is essential—something that stands as important even before the scrutinizing eyes of the greatest of thinkers, who think everything to its final conclusion. Ultimately, death— like birth—is always very personal and private. Our own death will be unique, different from anyone else’s.

“Die before dying” is an expression for the death of the false ego, a goal of all spiritual traditions. Before our spiritual identity can emerge, our false sense of who we are has to die. The false ego is compared to a knot in the heart, which binds the eternal soul to an illusory life. This knot has to be cut. Once one has done this, one can easily see past the greatest illusion and recognize that actually there is no death. “For the soul there is neither birth nor death at any time. He has not come into being, does not come into being, and will not come into being. He is unborn, eternal, ever-existing, and primeval. He is not slain when the body is slain.” (*Bg* 2.20)

This subject is not exclusive to the Vedic tradition. Christianity also speaks about the death of the old and sinful human, and the simultaneous birth of the new and better person. (Romans 6) The mystical traditions in Christianity know even of a *mors mystica*—the death of self-centered desires, which is necessary in order to enter our loving relationship with God. Once, when the Christian theologian Meister Eckhart was asked, “How should the person be, who can behold God?” he answered very simply, “He should be dead.” He meant that the egomaniac in us has to die so that God can take control of our lives. Only then can we become His instruments.

The German poet Goethe expressed it like this in “Blessed Longing”:

And till you confront this test, This dying-and-becoming You’ll only be a dismal guest At the earth’s dim gloaming.

In the Vedic tradition, many mantras, either to one’s spiritual guide or to God Himself, begin with the word *namaḥ*—*na* meaning “not” and *maḥ* “me,” which refers to the wrong conception of oneself as the body. *Namaḥ* means “not me” and “not my desire” but “You, Krishna, and Your desire.” Thus the syllables *namaḥ* refer to the subject before us, the death of the ego.

The spiritual person will die two deaths. Just as it is important for the human being to take two births—one physical and the other spiritual—so he also has to go through two deaths: the death of the false ego and the death of the material body. If one has overcome the false ego, the much-feared physical death loses its horror. When it occurs in the natural course of life, it can be as easy as moving from one room to the next, although it can also be accompanied by the dramatic drum beats of intense physical challenges. In any case, for a fully God-conscious person there is nothing to fear, since with death he is moving only from one hand of God to the other.

Please do not be shocked or afraid; it is actually nice to die before dying. One can live a better and more fulfilled life after the ego has been unloaded. It is only by illusion that one thinks self-centered attachments are making one happy. Happiness always lies in freedom, not in mental bondage. Isn’t it a source of celebration when a prisoner can be done with his prison life and start his real life? One of the best and most joyful examples of dying before dying is the butterfly. Before going into the cocoon for his transformation, the butterfly is an earth-bound, ugly caterpillar, eating only dirt. After he has died before dying, he leaves the cocoon as a beautiful, colorful creature who roams in freedom through the air.

Unfortunately, many of us live in the prisons of our minds and cannot even imagine a life in actual full bliss and knowledge. Enticed by a false sense of success and progress, we do not live according to our actual spiritual potential. This is brought to light by the story of two caterpillars: Trusty and Feary lived in a mountain valley. One day the news came to them that at the end of their valley was a huge tower that would lead to a life of total, instant fulfillment. One had only to climb it. When they went to investigate, Trusty and Feary found thousands of caterpillars trying to climb the tower. Finally they managed to get a place in the line, and amongst the struggling, fighting, pushing caterpillars, they began their way up. The tower of progress had them.

Half way up, Trusty began to doubt their journey. “If everyone shoves and struggles and fights now to get to perfection,” he said to Feary, “I can’t imagine that in a perfect place up there we will ever be friends. This is the wrong way to progress.” Feary saw his point but was not willing to return. “If everyone is climbing up the tower of progress,” he argued, “it must be good.” Nevertheless, Trusty was determined to follow his inner conviction. So he said goodbye to Feary, climbed down the tower, and returned home.

One day, back home, Trusty heard a voice from a tree: “Hey, Trusty, look at me.” When he looked up, he saw another of his friends, a caterpillar who was about to enclose himself in his cocoon. “What are you doing?” Trusty asked. “I am going into the transformation,” the caterpillar replied. “Soon I will fly high into the sky.” “I just came from high up in the sky,” Trusty told him. “It’s not what you think.” But the caterpillar had already closed the cocoon.

One month later, the cocoon opened up, but by the time Trusty saw it, there was only an empty silk shell. Overnight, while he was sleeping, his friend had turned into a butterfly and early in the morning had flown to the flowers, where he drank deep from their nectar.

Trusty was fascinated. He wanted to leave the earth, he wanted to be high in the sky, but it was clear to him that the tower of progress could not provide that.

One day Trusty decided to also climb the tree. When he reached its leaves, he saw that a thin silken thread was emerging from his body. So he just went along with nature: he covered himself like he had seen his friend do, and one day, a month later, he left the cocoon as a beautiful butterfly. Oh, how happy he was to finally fulfill his desire—in a natural way.

Meanwhile, Feary was still climbing the tower of progress. But as he was nearing its cloud-covered top, he heard distraught voices above him: “Oh, no, it’s only a lie. We’ve been cheated.” The next thing he heard was someone falling and crashing to the ground. “Oh, no,” said one of the voices, “another one has fallen. I might be next.” “Don’t speak so loud,” another voice replied. “The others might hear.”

Feary was terrified. But then he heard yet another voice, from nearby. “Hey, Feary,” it called. When he looked to the side, he saw a beautiful creature with colorful wings, dancing in the air around him. “Hey, Feary, it’s me.” “Who are you?” Feary asked apprehensively. “Look into my eyes, Feary,” the beautiful creature beckoned. When Feary looked deeply, he recognized his old friend and, full of surprise, said, “Trusty, you’ve come at a crucial stage of my life. Please help me; I am going to die. This tower doesn’t lead anywhere. Everyone is pushing upwards, but we will all fall down when we reach the top. I’m almost there; then I, too, will fall and die.”

“I have come to get you,” Trusty assured him. “Just trust me.” Feary was happy. His friend would not betray him. Trusty then lifted him off the tower and brought him down to the ground. Soon Feary, too, climbed a tree in the valley and entered a cocoon to die before dying. His old caterpillar ego dissolved, and he emerged as a wonderful butterfly to fly in freedom.

In the beginning we might be shocked to hear “die before dying.” But if we take shelter of proper spiritual guidance and a genuine process, unafraid of transformation, we will learn to fly in the spiritual sky by transforming the ego and becoming a trusting, free soul.

## Facing Death Alone—with Krishna

An account by Vārṣabhānavī dāsī in Visalia, California, October 28, 2001.

GIRIRAJ SWAMI: Vārṣabhānavī dāsī works for Merrill Lynch in Johannesburg, and her company offered her a course in New York City to help her develop her skills as a financial analyst. The Merrill Lynch building is just across the street from the World Trade Center, and she was there when the attack on 9/11 took place. She witnessed the terror. And it is not just the people who were injured or killed and their families and friends who were affected; even the people who were just there and saw it were affected profoundly. So we shall ask her to share her experiences and realizations.

VĀRṢABHĀNAVĪ DĀSĪ: Just before the incident took place, there were police sirens unrelated to the attack. We were in the auditorium and the lecturer was speaking. I looked at my watch; it was 8:50 a.m. Within seconds, we all heard a huge bang and the building we were in literally shook. At that stage I was thinking that it was an earthquake—something like that. Then the lecturer mentioned that it felt and sounded like the bombing of the World Trade Center in 1993. We were stunned, but we still didn’t know what was happening.

Then a few of our tutors ran into the auditorium and said that a plane had crashed into the World Trade Center. We all thought it was an accident. We didn’t consider that it could have been a terrorist attack. So we went to the next classroom and looked out the window. We could see the World Trade Center—flames were coming out of the building. The airplane was actually in the building, and it was just horrific. Some of my colleagues were hysterical; many were crying. I was just shocked, stunned. Flames were coming out of the building, and I was thinking, “There are so many people suffering in there.” A year ago I had an accident: my feet were burned when hot oil fell onto them, and it was extremely painful. So I could only imagine what they were going through in the building.

Then we saw people jumping out of the building. I remember one person in particular. He wore pants and a shirt and tie, and his tie was flapping. It was really horrible. Some of those who had jumped seemed to be trying to stay afloat; they were flapping their arms. I was thinking, “Oh, Krishna, please help them.” We were witnessing this whole incident, but we were completely helpless. We couldn’t help them or do anything. So I was praying, “Please help them, Krishna.”

About fifteen minutes later, I saw the second airplane, and it was so deliberate. I was thinking that this was evil. The second airplane flew around the corner and just crashed right into the second building. Everyone screamed. Until then, everybody had thought it had been an accident, but when the second airplane came, we realized, “This is no accident.” We were next to the building, and we were high up, and I was thinking that I could die at any moment. There could be more planes. “I must start chanting,” I thought. “Now is the time; now is the test.” But I couldn’t chant. I was numb. I walked up and down the lecture room thinking that I had to chant, but I couldn’t.

There was one colleague of mine, Patrick, from Brazil. He was sitting down and praying. I really admired him. He had his hand on the table and his head on his hand and he was saying prayers. And I was thinking that it didn’t matter whether he was a Jew or a Muslim or a Christian or whatever; he was praying.

Then the building supervisor came up and told us, “I don’t know what is happening, but you’re not safe up here. Better come down.” We were on the twenty-second floor. Everybody, although they were in shock, was very orderly; no one panicked. Everyone kept their cool, and we all walked down the stairs.

When we got down to the ground floor there were people all around. Outside there were huge crowds everywhere, and police vehicles, ambulances, and fire engines. When I think about it now, those first fire engines—all those firemen died. It was chaos. I was thinking, “Better just stay inside, and if I’m going to leave my body, I’ll just stay here and chant the holy names.” Then I thought of my parents. I phoned my brother and told him what had happened and that I was safe and not to worry about me.

Everyone was dumbfounded and aimless. So, I took my chanting beads and sat on the floor and just chanted. It felt good. One of my colleagues came up to me and said, “It’s really not safe here. We should get out immediately.” He took me out—I was going to stay put and just chant there—and outside was complete chaos. The buildings were in flames, police vehicles coming and going . . . I couldn’t comprehend the disaster. I can still recall the mood of New York before the incident—it was vibrant, happy, and jolly. The entire mood had reversed. Anyway, we walked down the street, away from the disaster, and hoards of people were following.

About five minutes later, one of the towers came crashing down. There was smoke everywhere. We had just missed it. And I was thinking that Krishna had worked through my colleague to get me out of that building. As we were walking down, I didn’t chant but I was trying to think of Krishna—to some extent. We walked about forty blocks. Most of the people walking with us were very orderly. Almost nobody spoke. Everyone was silent.

When we arrived at our hotel, we sat in the bar inside the entrance. There was a television, and we watched. We still didn’t really know what had happened or who had done it. On the television we heard that the Pentagon had also been hit. Everyone was stunned. Many of the people in the bar were taking shelter of alcohol.

About fifteen minutes later, a person who worked for Morgan Stanley in the South Tower arrived—he had been on the sixty-third floor and had managed to escape. He was shaking all over. He said, “I was there. I was right in the building,” and he told us his story: When the first tower was hit, most of the people in the South Tower started evacuating, but there was a message on the intercom that they should return to their desks. He said that there was no way he was going to stay; something inside him had told him to keep on going. When he was walking down the staircase, he saw a fat lady who was having a heart attack. Another lady was pregnant. I felt very upset and asked him what had happened to them. “I don’t know,” he said. “I just kept on going. Something inside said, ‘Keep on going.’ I don’t know if they made it or not.”

New York after that was just not the same. The whole vibrant mood was gone. One or two days after the terrorist attack, there were many bomb threats. I don’t know who it was—whether it was the same organization or someone else. We were staying in mid-Manhattan. One night, at 10:30, I was awakened by the hotel alarm. The hotel people told us to evacuate. For me it was the last straw. I was thinking, “What more? This is too much.” It was very intense. I was in my nightwear, and we had to walk down the stairs and outside. We were just walking and walking. I felt so alone, really alone. I had only my beads, and I was chanting. I was just chanting and crying. I was thinking, “It’s just Krishna. It’s just Krishna there.” Śrīla Prabhupāda used to say that you should chant as though you’re a helpless child. It was the first time I experienced that. I had only the holy names. I didn’t even have proper clothing; I didn’t have anything—just my *japa* beads and the holy names. So, everyone was outside, walking, and many were crying. It was just too much for people to handle.

I also had nightmares about the incident. In one week I had three nightmares. But one, in retrospect, taught me something. I don’t know where I was, but there was a creature coming toward me trying to kill me. I was trying to defend myself, as Draupadī initially did. I was throwing objects at it, but that didn’t work. The creature was coming toward me stronger and stronger. Then I stopped throwing things at it and started screaming, “Krishna! Krishna!” and the creature stopped and laughed. He said, “You do not know Krishna” and walked away. When I turned, I saw my spiritual master standing next to me. Now when I think of it, it’s true: I do not know Krishna. But I have my spiritual master, who knows Śrīla Prabhupāda and Krishna.

I learned a lot from the whole experience—to be more dependent on the Vaishnavas and to take shelter of the holy names. I chant so mechanically. The experience taught me to focus on the holy names and to chant with feeling.

## Die to Live

*Excerpts from* The Beggar IV: Die Before Dying *by Bhakti Tirtha Swami.*

**I Died on August 5th**

On August 5, 2004, I truly began the process of ending my existence on this planet in this body. During that week, I discovered that I had sugar diabetes and advanced melanoma. Prior to then, I knew of other health problems, but they were insignificant compared with the new ones that had developed. The old health problems should have been enough in themselves to alert me that my body is very fragile. Just having a material body is itself a disease because the body incarcerates the soul and limits the soul from its most blissful, fulfilling activities.

Every day, people must confront the shocking news that they or someone near to them has become possessed by a life-threatening disease. When people have this encounter, they feel angry, bewildered, and cheated, wondering why it must happen to them. They would like to think that these kinds of disasters happen to others and not to themselves. We forget that practically everyone who meets such a destiny is thinking the same: “Why has this happened to me instead of to someone else?” If each person had his or her wish, these misfortunes would be experienced by others and never by him- or herself. Persons who feel cheated are eager to project their own misfortune on others. Such people are concerned about not being cheated themselves while being eager to cheat others.

People normally feel some happiness, even if others are suffering, as long as they are not personally experiencing any difficulties. How wonderful it would be if we could think of ourselves as fortunate when we are chosen to experience challenges for our growth, which can relieve another person from having to undergo the same difficulties. This, of course, is the mood of a high-class devotee who is *para-duḥkha-duḥkhī*, who feels the miseries of others and enjoys their happiness with them.

Feelings of anger and bewilderment are due to not understanding that the Supreme Lord’s mercy comes in many different ways. It is easy to accept God’s mercy when we get what we want. This is like mercy coming from the front. However, when it comes from the side, it can be bewildering, and even more so when it comes from behind. When it comes from behind, it can be extremely disturbing, because the conditioned *jīva* loves to feel that he or she is in control. This, of course, is a big illusion, for we are always at the mercy of the Lord. However, we normally think that we are much more in control than we really are. Actually, the Lord’s mercy coming from behind, as in the case of an apparent calamity, is even more an expression of the Lord’s mercy because, in such instances, the Lord helps the *jīva* to accept and perceive his or her eternal identity as a wonderfully dependent servant.

We must remind ourselves that the Lord is always finding ways to send us His love. As we remind ourselves of this, it becomes easier to take advantage of the many opportunities that become manifest. Life is full of adventures and opportunities to serve humanity and glorify the Lord. When a person can accept everything as an expression of Krishna’s mercy and love, nothing can be too disturbing. Every moment becomes another opportunity to serve humanity, worship the Supreme, and express gratitude.

August 5th was truly a rebirth, for it gave me a chance to die to live. I, the beggar, am full of gratitude for receiving the Lord’s mercy from the front, from all sides, and yes, most wonderfully, from the back.

Why Be Afraid of Death?

Death removes everything that is false; death reveals to us our true friends; death exposes our true priorities; death brings forth wisdom; death educates us on our fears and weaknesses; but, most importantly, death reminds us that these material bodies and material universes are not our homes. *Since death is calling you, don’t fear or hide, but present yourself and see what she has to offer. After all, she is a just, faithful, and loyal servant of Krishna.*

**Cancer: An Offering of Tough Love**

Cancer is alive. So for it, as for any living entity, survival is of foremost importance. It is a natural instinct for people to organize their lives around trying to maintain their existence. My cancer is definitely life-threatening to my physical body. The medications and treatments I am taking are designed to kill the cancer cells, so it is natural for them to fight just as hard to survive. All life ultimately comes from Krishna, God, so all life has its purpose and can offer a learning experience. *Today I want to* *send my love to the cancer, for after all, everything is for our benefit. Thank you so much, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.*

The cancer has helped me in connecting on a much deeper level with my biological family. We are all so different, but we were brought up very similarly by my mother, who was most thoughtful, kind, and considerate. When she passed away from ovarian cancer, we bonded very strongly as a family in prayer and support. When my brothers and sisters heard of my disease, they all flew in to spend some quality time with me. We discussed so many important issues, and prayed, laughed, and cried together. In all cases, we were fully there for each other. *Today* *I want to send my love to the cancer, for after all, everything is for our benefit. Thank you so much, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.*

By the mercy of Śrīla Prabhupāda, my spiritual mentor, I have been brought in contact with a beautiful and diverse spiritual family of godbrothers and godsisters. These wonderful servants have made the most amazing sacrifices year after year. All over the world, they have spread the teachings of *sanātana-dharma*, sacrificing wealth, health, family, education, and prestige. Several of them are now aging and even passing away. During this sickness, I have been in touch with spiritual godfamily from all over the world. We have connected on deeper levels as servants in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s mission more than ever before. Of course, we are also wondering who among us may be next to leave his or her body. This has helped us all look closer at our own health and, most significantly, to reexamine how much we need and depend on each other. *Today I want to send* *my love to the cancer, for after all, everything is for our benefit. Thank you so much, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.*

By the mercy of guru and Krishna, I have been given so many loving disciples. They are praying for me daily, organizing special *pūjās*, *yajñas*, and other ceremonies. Most have raised their own standards and made greater commitments. They are coming together as a spiritual family more than ever before, and trying to hold on to my instructions. They are reaching out to make each other more accountable. They are taking shelter of more spiritual uncles, aunties, and others for *śikṣā*. They are sending me more love than ever before. They are reading my books, listening to my tapes, and listening to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s tapes more seriously than they have during their entire spiritual journeys. When I was healthy, they were attentive, but nowhere near as much as they are now. *Today I want to send my love to* *the cancer, for after all, God does not make mistakes and He arranges everything for our benefit. Thank you so much, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.*

This seemingly fatal disease has caused me to look closer at my own attachments, gross and subtle. It has very clearly put in front of me what needs to be transcended before returning back home. The cancer has allowed me to be much more appreciative of what I have experienced and accomplished. It has increased my appreciation of the wonderful years of excellent health that I have had most of my life. It has allowed me to look closer at what is really important in contrast to what is superficial or secondary. It has helped me to be more mindful of each moment and each encounter. It has clearly shown me how much I am not in control of my life—and never was. This has surely made me more dependent on guru’s and Krishna’s mercy. The uncertainty and desperation the cancer has produced is giving me a wonderful chance to detach myself from my bodily identifications and to search out more connections with my permanent, eternal identity. *Today I want to send my love to the cancer, for after* *all, God does not make mistakes and He arranges everything for our benefit. Thank you so much, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.*

The cancer has helped me better understand the sufferings others have to endure. I had prayed desperately to become a more empowered agent of change, to understand their suffering. I wanted the ability to help others deal with, grow from, and move through their pain. Now it is no longer an academic experience, but it is experimental. The disease has helped me to develop a deeper level of wisdom and compassion far beyond what I normally possess. It has also allowed me to reflect and write with greater sensitivity, with hopes that I will connect on a deeper level with the hearts of my readers. *Today I want to* *send my love to the cancer, for after all, God does not make mistakes and He arranges everything for our benefit. Thank you so much, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.*

My dear cancer cells, you are a messenger. A messenger who brings honest, accurate, bad news should not be faulted, even though the truthful news is unpalatable. A messenger has the duty to deliver the precise message. You have done a very good job as a courier. The message has reached its destination. I have received it in full, and I am acting upon it with intense haste and gravity. This wake-up call has helped me to look closer at Lord Chaitanya’s last prayer:

*āśliṣya vā pāda-ratāṁ pinaṣṭu mām adarśanān marma-hatāṁ karotu vā*  
*yathā tathā vā vidadhātu lampaṭo mat-prāṇa-nāthas tu sa eva nāparaḥ*

**“I know no one except Kṛṣṇa as my Lord, and He will always remain as such, even if He handles me roughly by His embrace or makes me broken-hearted by not being present before me. He is completely free to do anything and everything, for He is always my worshipable Lord, unconditionally.”**

It is easy to be appreciative and grateful when everything in our lives happens according to our desires. However, the real test of our faith and love comes when we have challenges and setbacks that are confusing. It can bewilder us even more when the Supreme Himself seems to allow or arrange such sufferings, or when He may seem to handle us roughly or not be present before us. However, all of what the Lord does is best for our coming back to Him. So this chronic disease has given me a better understanding of unconditional, unmotivated service. It has helped me move faster toward embracing unconditional love toward *guru* and Krishna and more genuine service and appreciation for all sadhus.

My beloved cancer, you must also die; you either die with me or before me, but neither of us can remain forever in these hellish bodies. So there is no longer any need for us to be enemies, for our ultimate outcome with material nature is the same—it destroys us. This is a time of giving thanks, for I am so appreciative of the many wonderful things you have brought to me. Now you must die, and I must also eventually die; in both of our cases our time is very limited. Therefore, once again I want to send you love and appreciation for all the wonderful things you have added to my life. *Especially today, I send my love to you, the cancer, for after all, God does not make mistakes and He arranges everything for our benefit.*

I, the lowly beggar, again thank you, cancer cells, for offering me tough love.

**Live Until Dying**

I do not know how long I have to live in this present body, due to terminal illness. Surely I am near death, or perhaps I will have died by the time you, my beloved, read these prayers. Even if I, your loved ones, or you survive a terminal illness, death will still make an appointment with each of us in the near future. So, yes, we are all born to die. If we can die even before dying by understanding the temporary nature of the material body and the eternality of the soul and turning ignorance into knowledge, lust into love, selfishness into selflessness, enviousness into appreciation, and hatred into compassion, we will die to live. By bringing death to ignorance, lust, selfishness, enviousness, and hatred, we will gain our rightful claim to inherit and live in the kingdom of God. *Rarely do we know the exact time of* *our death, but even if we do, still we must live until dying.*

In the past I had a few very good astrologers offer me readings after learning the particulars of my birth. They all agreed that I am more an entity of the future and that my life would begin to accelerate with greater and greater achievements after fifty-five years of age. They expressed how I would have international influence over communities, world leaders, etc., which would grow until I stepped back from all of this in my eighties to enter into *nirjana-bhajana* (a state of consciousness where one no longer relates to the temporary material body and world).

Well, I am now fifty-five years old and, yes, I already have contact with many world leaders and some basic influence with several international communities. However, as I write this last meditation, it seems that my visit to this material world and my stay in this limited material body is over. Perhaps due to a higher influence, these readings are now wrong. *Rarely do we know the* *exact time of our death, but even if we do, still we must live until dying.*

People are very different when they believe they will soon die. Some will increase their bodily identification and attachments by trying to experience as much sensual gratification as they can before giving up the body. Some become paralyzed by depression, self-pity, fear, and anger. Others see it as a time for the soul to connect more with its true eternal identity, fully accepting the inevitable while living with gratitude in the present. Such rare souls understand the science of dying to live, dying before dying. *Rarely do we know the exact time of our* *death, but even if we do, still we must live until dying.*

Even if a person leaves the body very early, this should not present a problem if we die before dying, because we would have lived in the present to our very best, learning from the past while joyfully anticipating the future. Some of the greatest personalities left the body at an early age. Jesus left at thirty-three years of age, Śaṅkarācārya at thirty-two, Lord Chaitanya at forty-eight, Martin Luther King Jr. at thirty-nine. Sometimes the body dies in a seemingly untimely manner; however, the higher authorities know how long each of us is to remain incarcerated in these material bodies.
